Spaces of norms, determinant of cohomology and Fekete points in non-Archimedean geometry
Sébastien Boucksom and Dennis Eriksson

Abstract
. Let L L be an ample line bundle on a (geometrically reduced) projective variety X X over any complete valued field. Our main result describes the leading asymptotics of the determinant of cohomology of large powers of L L , with respect to the supnorm of a continuous metric on the Berkovich analytification of L L . As a consequence, we establish in this setting the existence of transfinite diameters and equidistribution of Fekete points, following a strategy going back to Berman, Witt Nyström and the first author for complex manifolds. In the non-Archimedean case, our approach relies on a version of the Knudsen–Mumford expansion for the determinant of cohomology on models over the (possibly non-Noetherian) valuation ring, as a replacement for the asymptotic expansion of Bergman kernels in the complex case, and on the reduced fiber theorem, as a replacement for the Bernstein–Markov inequalities. Along the way, a systematic study of spaces of norms and the associated Fubini–Study type metrics is undertaken.



Introduction

Fekete points and transfinite diameter are classical notions in logarithmic potential theory in the plane. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the
m
-diameter

\delta_{m}(K)
of a compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}
is defined as the supremum of the geometric mean distance between
m+1
points in
K
, maximizers being called
Fekete configurations
. The
m
-diameter of
K
admits a limit
\delta_{\infty}(K)
as
m\to\infty
, called the
transfinite diameter
of
K
, which turns out to coincide with the logarithmic capacity of
K
. Further, Fekete configurations become asymptotically unique in the limit, in the sense that they equidistribute to a certain canonical probability measure on
K
, called its
equilibrium measure
.

In several complex variables, a similar picture was only rather recently obtained. The first steps were taken by Leja in the 1950’s, introducing a notion of
m
-diameter
\delta_{m}(K)
for a compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
, defined in terms of the supremum of certain Vandermonde-type determinants. The existence of the transfinite diameter
\delta_{\infty}(K)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\delta_{m}(K)
was established by Zaharjuta
[
Zah75
]
, and the next key step came with Rumely’s observation in
[
Rum07
]
that the general results in arithmetic intersection theory developed in
[
CLR03
]
yield in particular an exact formula for
\delta_{\infty}(K)
in terms of pluripotential theory, generalizing the classical Robin formula for the logarithmic capacity in the plane, and involving plurisubharmonic envelopes and mixed Monge–Ampère operators in the sense of Bedford–Taylor. This triggered joint work of the first author with Berman and Witt Nyström
[
BB10
,
BBW11
]
, which built on Bergman kernel asymptotics to establish a general version of Rumely’s formula in the setting of complex projective manifolds, and combined it with a variational argument to prove the equidistribution of Fekete configurations in this context.

The main purpose of the present paper is to study versions of these results in non-Archimedean (Berkovich) geometry. While many results hold over an arbitrary non-Archimedean complete valued field
K
, the full picture relies on more refined non-Archimedean pluripotential theory as developed in
[
BFJ16
,
BFJ15
,
BG+16
,
BJ18a
]
, and hence requires
K
to be trivially or discretely valued and of residue characteristic
0
.

Asymptotics of relative volumes

The Bouche–Catlin–Tian–Zelditch asymptotic expansion of Bergman kernels
[
Bou90
,
Cat99
,
Tia90
,
Zel98
]
is a fundamental result in complex geometry, which describes the asymptotic behavior of the
L^{2}
-norms associated to large tensor powers of a positive Hermitian line bundle. As noticed in
[
BB10
]
, it can be reformulated as an asymptotic expansion for the logarithmic volume ratio of such
L^{2}
-norms, as follows.

First, define the
relative volume
of any two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on an
N
-dimensional complex vector space
V
as

(0.1)

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}):=\log\left(\frac{\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime}}{\det\|\cdot\|}\right),

where
\det\|\cdot\|
,
\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
denote the induced norms on the determinant line
\det V=\bigwedge^{N}V
. In terms of the unit balls
B,B^{\prime}\subset V
of the two norms,

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\vol B}{\vol B^{\prime}}\right)+O(N\log N),

where the error term
O(N\log N)
vanishes when the two norms are Hermitian.

Let next
X
be an
n
-dimensional complex projective manifold. Every smooth, positively curved metric
\phi
on an (ample) line bundle
L
over
X
induces, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, an
L^{2}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(m\phi)}
on the space of global sections
\Hnot(mL)=\Hnot(X,L^{\otimes m})
. The asymptotic expansion of Bergman kernels mentioned above turns out to be equivalent to the existence of a full asymptotic expansion

\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(m\phi)},\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(m\psi)}\right)=m^{n+1}a_{n+1}+m^{n}a_{n}+\ldots+O(m^{-\infty})

for any two such metrics
\phi,\psi
. Up to a multiplicative constant, the leading order coefficient
a_{n+1}
can further be identified with a fundamental functional in Kähler geometry, the
relative Monge-Ampère energy
1
1

1

Note that the present normalization, which is more convenient for the purpose of this paper, is not uniform accross the literature.

(0.2)

\en(\phi,\psi):=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\int_{X}(\phi-\psi)(dd^{c}\phi)^{j}\wedge(dd^{c}\psi)^{n-j}.

We use additive notation for metrics on line bundles, so that
\phi-\psi
is a function on
X
, and
dd^{c}\phi
,
dd^{c}\psi
denote the curvature
(1,1)
-forms of
\phi,\psi
.

Consider now an arbitrary complete valued field
K
, i.e. a field
K
complete with respect to an absolute value. In the Archimedean case,
K=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, by the Gelfand–Mazur theorem. In the non-Archimedean case, the main examples are
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
\mathbb{C}_{p}
, or fields of formal Laurent series and their completed algebraic closure, but the trivially valued case is also important for the study of K-stability (see
[
BJ18b
]
). Let
X
be a geometrically reduced projective scheme over
K
,
L
be a line bundle on
X
, and
\phi,\psi
be continuous metrics on (the Berkovich analytification of)
L
. Building on a result of Chen and Maclean based on Okounkov bodies
[
CMac15
]
, we show the existence of the
relative volume of
\phi,\psi

(0.3)

\vol(L,\phi,\psi):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi}\right)\in\mathbb{R},

cf. Theorem
9.8
. The main result of the present paper is as follows.

Theorem A
.

Let
X
be a geometrically reduced projective scheme over any complete valued field
K
, and
\phi,\psi
be continuous psh
2
2

2

A shorthand for plurisubharmonic.
metrics on an ample line bundle
L
over
X
. Then

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\en(\phi,\psi).

In the Archimedean case, it follows from results of Demailly that a continuous metric on
L
is psh iff it is a uniform limit of Fubini–Study metrics (see Theorem
7.1
). In the non-Archimedean case, we use this as a definition, and prove compatibility with the more common notion of semipositive metric in this context, due to S.W. Zhang and involving nef models, when
K
is nontrivially valued. We also show that a continuous metric
\phi
is psh iff it becomes psh after ground field extension. The relative Monge–Ampère energy of continuous psh metrics can still be defined by (
0.2
), the latter being understood in the sense of Chamber-Loir and Ducros
[
CLD12
]
in the non-Archimedean sense.

In the Archimedean case, Theorem A reduces to
[
BB10
]
after passing to a resolution of singularities. In the discretely valued case, it was established in
[
BG+16
, Theorem A]
. The main contribution of the present paper is thus to establish Theorem A for a densely valued, non-Archimedean ground field
K
.

The
psh envelope

\env(\phi)
of a continuous metric
\phi
on
L
is defined as the pointwise supremum of the family of all continuous psh metrics
\psi
on
L
such that
\psi\leq\phi
. We say that
continuity of envelopes
holds for
(X,L)
if
\env(\phi)
is continuous (and hence psh) for each continuous metric
\phi
on
L
.

Corollary B
.

Assume that continuity of envelopes holds for
(X,L)
. For any two continuous metrics
\phi,\psi
on
L
, we then have

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\en(\env(\phi),\env(\psi)).

In the Archimedean case, classical results in pluripotential imply that continuity of envelopes holds whenever
X
is normal, and Corollary B is indeed also a consequence of
[
BB10
]
in that case.

In the non-Archimedean case, we similarly expect continuity of envelopes to holds as soon as
X
is normal. As of this writing, continuity of envelopes (and hence Corollary B) has been established when
X
is smooth, and one of the following is satisfied:

•

X
is a curve, as a consequence of A. Thuillier’s work
[
Thu05
]
(see
[
GJKM19
]
);

•

K
is discretely or trivially valued, of residue characteristic
0

[
BFJ16
,
BJ18a
]
, building on
multiplier ideals and the Nadel vanishing theorem;

•

K
is discretely valued of characteristic
p
,
(X,L)
is defined over a function field of
transcendence degree
d
, and resolution of singularities is assumed in dimension
n+d

[
GJKM19
]
, replacing multiplier ideals with test ideals.

Sketch of the proof

As already mentioned, Theorem A basically follows from
[
BB10
]
in the Archimedean case, and we henceforth assume that
K
is non-Archimedean. The main tools involved in the proof can be summarized as follows.

The reduced fiber theorem

The proof of Theorem A is fairly easily reduced to the case where each metric is induced by an ample model
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L})
of
(X,L)
, i.e. an ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}
extending
L
to a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
over the valuation ring
K^{\circ}
. Besides the supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
defined by the model metric
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
, the space of sections
\Hnot(mL)
is then also equipped with the lattice norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}
induced by the
K^{\circ}
-module
\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})=\Hnot(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes m})
. This lattice norm, which is to some extent the analogue of the
L^{2}
-norm in the present non-Archimedean context, coincides with the supnorm when
\mathcal{X}
has a reduced special fiber, but not in general. Using the Bosch–Lütkebohmert–Raynaud reduced fiber theorem
[
BLR95
]
, we prove however that the distortion between
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}
remains bounded as
m\to\infty
, which enables us to replace the supnorms with the lattice norms in proving Theorem A.

Knudsen–Mumford expansion

Our main tool is then the Knudsen–Mumford expansion of the determinant of cohomology
[
KM76
]
, which plays the role of the asymptotic expansion of Bergman kernels in the complex case, and provides for
m\gg 1
a polynomial expansion (in additive notation for
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles over
\spec K^{\circ}
)

(0.4)

\det\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})=\frac{m^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\langle\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\rangle+\dots

with leading order term the Deligne pairing
\langle\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\rangle
. Since models over
K^{\circ}
are non-Noetherian in the densely valued case, some care is however required to apply this result, and the relevant explanations are provided in Appendix A, based on F. Ducrot’s approach
[
Duc05
]
.

Metrics on Deligne pairings

The Knudsen–Mumford expansion yields an expression of the relative volume of the metrics as a difference of model metrics on the Deligne pairing
\langle L^{n+1}\rangle
. The final ingredient in the proof of Theorem A consists in relating model metrics on Deligne pairings with mixed Monge–Ampère integrals, which is accomplished via the Poincaré–Lelong formula of
[
CLD12
]
and a careful monotone regularization argument.

Transfinite diameter and Fekete points

Following the strategy developed in
[
BB10
,
BBW11
]
, we establish the existence of transfinite diameters, and combine it with a differentiability result proved in
[
BFJ15
,
BG+16
,
BJ18a
]
under appropriate assumptions on the ground field
K
to infer equidistribution of Fekete points.

Let as above
X
be a geometrically reduced projective scheme over a complete valued field
K
. Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
, and set
N:=\dim\Hnot(X,L)
. The data of a basis
{\bf s}=(s_{i})
of
\Hnot(X,L)
determines a generator
s_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge s_{N}
of
\det\Hnot(X,L)
, as well as a section

\det{\bf s}\in\Hnot\left(X^{N},L^{\boxtimes N}\right),

expressed as the Vandermonde determinant

(\det{\bf s})(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\det\left(s_{i}(x_{j})\right).

Every continuous metric
\phi
on
L
induces a continuous metric
\phi^{\boxtimes N}
on
L^{\boxtimes N}
, and a
Fekete configuration
for
\phi
is a point
P\in(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}
that computes the supnorm
\|\det{\bf s}\|_{\phi^{\boxtimes N}}
. The choice of a norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\Hnot(X,L)
induces a norm
\det\|\cdot\|
on
\det\Hnot(X,L)
, and the
diameter
of
\phi
with respect to
\|\cdot\|
is defined as the normalized supnorm

\delta(\phi,\|\cdot\|):=\frac{\|\det{\bf s}\|_{\phi^{\boxtimes N}}}{\det\|s_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{N}\|},

which is independent of the choice of basis
{\bf s}=(s_{i})
.

Theorem C
.

Let
L
be any line bundle on a geometrically reduced projective scheme
X
over a complete valued field
K
. For any two continuous metrics
\phi,\psi
on
L
, the
transfinite diameter

\delta_{\infty}(\phi,\psi):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\delta(m\phi,\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})^{n!/m^{n+1}}

exists in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and satisfies
\log\delta_{\infty}(\phi,\psi)=\vol(L,\psi,\phi)
.

This is inferred from the existence of the limit (
0.3
) defining the relative volume
\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=-\vol(L,\psi,\phi)
via an estimate of the operator norms of the embeddings
\det\Hnot(mL)\hookrightarrow\Hnot\left((mL)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}\right)
with respect to the norms induced by
\phi
on both sides, which is again ultimately deduced from the reduced fiber theorem.

From now on we assume that
L
is ample. To state our last main result, we shall say that
differentiability holds
at a continuous metric
\phi
on
L
if:

(i)

the psh envelope
\env(\phi)
is continuous (hence psh);

(ii)

for all
f\in\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
we have

\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\vol(L,\phi+tf,\phi)=\int_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}f\,\left(dd^{c}\env(\phi)\right)^{n}.

Differentiability is known to hold at all continuous metrics when
X
is smooth and one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

K
is Archimedean
[
BB10
]
;

•

K
is non-Archimedean, trivially or discretely valued, of residue characteristic zero
[
BFJ16
,
BG+16
,
BJ18a
]
;

•

K
is discretely valued of characteristic
p
,
(X,L)
is defined over a function field of
transcendence degree
d
, and resolution of singularities is assumed in dimension
n+d

[
GJKM19
,
BG+16
]
.

In a forthcoming paper
[
BGM20
]
, it will be shown that continuity of envelopes implies differentiability at all continuous metrics.

Importing the variational argument of
[
BBW11
]
, itself based on an idea of
[
SUZ97
]
, we prove:

Theorem D
.

Let
X
be a geometrically reduced, projective scheme over a complete valued field
K
. Let
\phi
be a continuous metric on an ample line bundle
L
over
X
, of volume
V=(L^{n})
, and assume that differentiability holds at
\phi
. For each
m\gg 1
, pick a Fekete configuration
P_{m}\in(X^{N_{m}})^{\mathrm{an}}
for
m\phi
. Then
P_{m}
equidistributes to the probability measure

\mu_{\phi}:=V^{-1}(dd^{c}\env(\phi))^{n}.

as
m\to\infty
.

Organization of the paper

The paper is organized as follows:

•

Section 1 contains background material on norms on finite dimensional vector spaces over a complete valued field. We present the results in the Archimedean and non-Archimedean cases as uniformly as possible.

•

In Section 2 we discuss determinants of norms and their relation to relative spectra.

•

Section 3, which stands somewhat apart from the rest of paper, applies the previous result to construct metrics on spaces of norms, following
[
Ger81
]
.

•

Sections 4 and 5 contain background material on Berkovich spaces and metrics on line bundles. We recall the standard constructions of model metrics, and compare them to Fubini–Study metrics. We discuss the relation between the reduced fiber theorem and finiteness of integral closure.

•

Section 6 establishes our first key tool, to wit boundedness of the distortion between the supnorms and lattice norms induced by a model. As a consequence, we obtain a precise description of the behavior of supnorms under ground field extension.

•

In Section 7 we study limits of Fubini–Study metrics, compare them to Zhang’s definition of semipositive metrics, and discuss the related notion of semipositive envelope.

•

In Section 8 we review the Bedford–Taylor/Chambert–Loir–Ducros mixed Monge–Ampère operators, and relate them to Deligne pairings.

•

Section 9 contains the proof of Theorem A and Corollary B.

•

Section 10 shows the existence of transfinite diameters and equidistribution of Fekete points, i.e. Theorem C and Theorem D. We also show how the results can be applied in the case of toric varieties.

•

In the Appendix we explain how F. Ducrot’s approach to the Knudsen–Mumford expansion for the determinant of cohomology
[
Duc05
]
and the related notion of Deligne pairings can be extended from the usual Noetherian case to arbitrary schemes.

Part I
Spaces of norms and determinants

1.
Spaces of norms

The goal of this section is to review some basic material on finite dimensional normed vector spaces, treating in parallel the Archimedean and non-Archimedean cases (including the trivially valued case). All the results are more or less well-known, but our proof of density of diagonalizable norms among all norms in the non-Archimedean case (Theorem
1.19
) appears to be new.

1.1.
Complete valued fields

Here and throughout the article,
K
denotes a
complete valued field
, i.e. a field endowed with a (possibly trivial) absolute value
|\cdot|:K\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, with respect to which it becomes a complete metric space. The value group
|K^{\times}|
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and is thus either discrete or dense. We sometimes use the
additive value group

\Gamma_{K}:=\log|K^{\times}|\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Recall that
K
is
Archimedean
if, for each nonzero
a\in K
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|na|>1
. This implies that
K
has characteristic
0
, and hence contains
\mathbb{Q}
, and that the restriction of
|\cdot|
to
\mathbb{Q}
is equivalent to the standard absolute
|\cdot|_{\infty}
, by Ostrowski’s theorem. As a result,
K
is a complete field extension of
\mathbb{R}
, and hence
K=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
(up to normalization of the absolute value), by the Gelfand–Mazur theorem.

Otherwise,
K
is
non-Archimedean
, and this holds if and only if
|\cdot|
satisfies the ultrametric inequality
|a+b|\leq\max\{|a|,|b|\}
for all
a,b\in K
. We then have a corresponding real-valued valuation
v_{K}:=-\log|\cdot|
on
K
, whose valuation ring
K^{\circ}
is thus the closed unit ball of
K
, with maximal ideal
K^{\circ\circ}
the open unit ball and residue field
\widetilde{K}:=K^{\circ}/K^{\circ\circ}
. Since the value group is a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
, the valuation ring
K^{\circ}
is of Krull dimension at most
1
, and it is Noetherian if and only if
|K^{\times}|
is discrete. In that case,
K^{\circ\circ}
is a principal ideal; a generator
\pi_{K}
of
K^{\circ\circ}
is called a uniformizing parameter, and is unique up to multiplication by a unit.

If on the other hand
K
is algebraically closed, then
\widetilde{K}
is algebraically closed as well, and
|K^{\times}|
is divisible. In particular,
K
is then either trivially valued or densely valued. The completion of an algebraic closure of any non-Archimedean field
K
is denoted by
\mathbb{C}_{K}
. It is the smallest complete algebraically closed extension of
K
.

A field
K
is
local
if its unit ball
K^{\circ}
is compact. This holds if and only if
K
is either Archimedean, or non-Archimedean with finite residue field
\widetilde{K}
. In the latter case,
K
is trivially or discretely valued, and in fact either a finite trivially valued field, or isomorphic to a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}{(\!(t)\!)}
(up to normalization of the absolute value).

An
immediate extension
of a non-Archimedean field
K
is a complete field extension
F/K
with the same value group and residue field as
K
. The field
K
is
maximally complete
if it admits no nontrivial immediate extension. By
[
Kap42
]
,
K
is maximally complete iff it is spherically complete, which means that any decreasing sequence of closed balls has non-empty intersection. A discretely valued field is maximally complete, and every algebraically closed field admits an immediate maximally complete extension, unique up to isomorphism
[
Kap42
, Theorem 5]
.

Example 1.1
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
, and endow the field
K=k{(\!(t)\!)}
of formal Laurent series with the
t
-adic valuation. An algebraic closure of
K
is given by the field of Puiseux series

k(\!(t^{1/\infty})\!)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}k(\!(t^{1/n})\!),

whose completion
\mathbb{C}_{K}
is realized as the field of formal series
f=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Q}}a_{r}t^{r}
,
a_{r}\in k
, with support
\supp f=\{r\in\mathbb{Q}\mid a_{r}\neq 0\}
containing only finitely many elements with a given upper bound. The immediate maximally complete extension of
\mathbb{C}_{K}
is given by the Malcev–Neumann field
k(\!(t^{\mathbb{Q}})\!)
of power series
f=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Q}}a_{r}t^{r}
with well-ordered support. Note that
f=\sum_{n\geq 1}t^{-1/n}
is in
k(\!(t^{\mathbb{Q}})\!)\setminus\mathbb{C}_{K}
, so that
\mathbb{C}_{K}
is not maximally complete.

Example 1.2
.

Similarly, the completion
\mathbb{C}_{p}
of an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is also not maximally complete. Denote by
A
the Witt ring of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
,
i.e.
the valuation ring of the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The immediate maximally complete extension of
\mathbb{C}_{p}
is obtained as the quotient of the Malcev–Neumann ring
A(\!(t^{\mathbb{Q}})\!)
by the ideal of formal power series
f=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Q}}a_{r}t^{r}
such that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{r+n}p^{n}=0
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for all
r\in\mathbb{Q}
, cf.
[
Poo93
, §4]
.

1.2.
The space of norms

Let
V
be a fixed finite dimensional
K
-vector space, and set
N:=\dim V
.

Definition 1.3
.

A
seminorm
on
V
is a function
\|\cdot\|:V\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(i)

\|av\|=|a|\|v\|
for all
a\in K
,
v\in V
;

(ii)

\|v+w\|\leq\|v\|+\|w\|
(resp.
\|v+w\|\leq\max\{\|v\|,\|w\|\}
) for all
v,w\in V
if
K
is Archimedean (resp. non-Archimedean).

We say that
\|\cdot\|
is
pure
if it takes values in
|K|\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. A
norm
is a seminorm
\|\cdot\|
such that
\|v\|=0\Longleftrightarrow v=0
. We denote by
\mathcal{N}(V)
the set of all norms on
V
.

The group
\GL(V)
acts on
\mathcal{N}(V)
by composition.

Example 1.4
.

If
K
is Archimedean, mapping a norm
\|\cdot\|
to its closed unit ball
B
(centered at
0
) sets up a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{N}(V)
and the set of all convex bodies of
V
that are centrally symmetric when
K=\mathbb{R}
, and
S^{1}
-invariant when
K=\mathbb{C}
. The inverse map is obtained by setting

\|v\|=\inf\left\{r\geq 0\mid v\in rB\right\}.

Example 1.5
.

If
K
is trivially valued, the closed balls
B_{r}
, of radius
r
, of a norm on
V
form an increasing filtration of
V
by linear subspaces, which is exhaustive (
B_{r}=V
for
r\gg 1
), separating (
B_{r}=\{0\}
for
r\ll 1
), and right-continuous (
B_{r}=\bigcap_{r^{\prime}>r}B_{r^{\prime}}
). Conversely, any such filtration defines a norm by setting

\|v\|=\inf\{r\geq 0\mid v\in B_{r}\}.

In other words, the data of a norm with respect to the trivial absolute value is equivalent to that of an increasing flag
\{0\}=V_{0}\subset V_{1}\subset\dots\subset V_{n}=V
of linear subspaces, together with a increasing sequence
0=r_{0}<r_{1}<\dots<r_{n}
.

Equivalence of norms over
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
is usually established as a consequence of the compactness of the unit cube. Crucially, equivalence of norms still holds over any complete valued field.

Proposition 1.6
.

Any two norms
\|\cdot\|
,
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V
are equivalent,
i.e.
there exists
C>0
such that
C^{-1}\|\cdot\|\leq\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\leq C\|\cdot\|
.

Proof.

For the convenience of the reader, we repeat the simple standard argument, in order to show that it applies to the trivially valued case as well. Note first that the result implies that
V
is complete with respect to any norm
\|\cdot\|
. Indeed, after choosing a basis
(e_{i})
of
V
,
\|\cdot\|
will be equivalent to the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
associated to
(e_{i})
, which is complete since it is isometrically isomorphic to
K^{N}
.
We argue by induction on
N=\dim V
, the desired result being trivial for
N=1
. We are going to show that any given norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. For each subspace
W\neq V
, the restriction of
\|\cdot\|
to
W
is complete, by induction. As a result,
W
is closed with respect to
\|\cdot\|
, and hence
\inf_{w\in W}\|v+w\|>0
for each
v\in V\setminus W
. In particular,

c_{i}:=\inf_{a\in K^{N}}\left\|e_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}a_{j}e_{j}\right\|>0

for all
i
. For each
a\in K^{N}
and each
i
with
a_{i}\neq 0
, we get

\left\|\sum_{j}a_{j}e_{j}\right\|=|a_{i}|\left\|e_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{a_{j}}{a_{i}}e_{j}\right\|\geq c_{i}|a_{i}|,

and hence
\left\|\sum_{j}a_{j}e_{j}\right\|\geq(\min_{j}c_{j})\max_{j}|a_{j}|
. By the triangle inequality, we also have
\left\|\sum_{j}a_{j}e_{i}\right\|\leq N\max_{j}|a_{j}|\|e_{i}\|
, which proves that
\|\cdot\|
and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
are indeed equivalent.
∎

As noticed during the proof, each linear subspace
W\subset V
is closed with respect to any norm
\|\cdot\|
, and
\|\cdot\|
thus induces a quotient norm
\|\cdot\|_{V/W}
on
V/W
, defined as usual by

\|\bar{v}\|_{V/W}:=\inf_{w\in W}\|v+w\|

for each
v\in V
with image
\bar{v}\in V/W
.

By Proposition
1.6
, we can endow
\mathcal{N}(V)
with the
Goldman–Iwahori
metric
\dGI
(named after
[
GI
]
), defined by

(1.1)

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\right):=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\left|\log\|v\|-\log\|v\|^{\prime}\right|.

The exponential of
\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\right)
is thus the distortion betwen the two norms, i.e. the smallest constant
C\geq 1
such that

C^{-1}\|\cdot\|\leq\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\leq C\|\cdot\|.

The action of
\GL(V)
on
\mathcal{N}(V)
preserves
\dGI
.

Example 1.7
.

Assume
\dim V=1
, and pick a nonzero
v\in V
. Then
\|\cdot\|\mapsto\log\|v\|
defines an isometry
(\mathcal{N}(V),\dGI)\simeq\mathbb{R}
, and the action of
\GL(V)
on
\mathcal{N}(V)
is equivalent to the action of the additive value group
\Gamma_{K}=\log|K^{\times}|
on
\mathbb{R}
by translation.

The basic topological properties of
\mathcal{N}(V)
are as follows.

Proposition 1.8
.

The metric space
(\mathcal{N}(V),\dGI)
is complete. If
K
is local,
i.e.
its unit ball
K^{\circ}
is compact, then any closed bounded subset of
\mathcal{N}(V)
is compact.

Proof.

If
(\|\cdot\|_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{N}(V)
, then
\log\|v\|_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence for each nonzero
v\in V
. We easily conclude that
\|\cdot\|_{n}
converges in
\mathcal{N}(V)
, which proves the first assertion. Assume now that
K
is local.
After choosing a basis, we may assume that
V=K^{N}
, which we equip with the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. By the triangle inequality, each norm
\|\cdot\|
with
\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq C
restricts to a
C
-Lipschitz continuous function on the compact set
(K^{\circ})^{N}
. By the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, the closed balls of
\mathcal{N}(V)
(and hence any bounded closed subset) are thus compact.
∎

Remark 1.9
.

Conversely, if
\dim V>1
, one can show that
\mathcal{N}(V)
is locally compact only if
K^{\circ}
is compact.

1.3.
Diagonalizable norms

In order to treat in parallel the Archimedean and non-Archimedean cases, we will use the following terminology.

Definition 1.10
.

A norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
is
diagonalizable
if there exists a basis
(e_{i})
such that we have for all
a\in K^{N}
:

(i)

\|\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\|^{2}=\sum_{i}\|a_{i}e_{i}\|^{2}
(Archimedean case);

(ii)

\|\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\|=\max_{i}\|a_{i}e_{i}\|
(non-Archimedean case).

The basis
(e_{i})
is then said to be
orthogonal
for
\|\cdot\|
, and it is
orthonormal
if it further satisfies
\|e_{i}\|=1
. We denote by

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)\subset\mathcal{N}(V)

the set of diagonalizable norms.

Note that a norm admits an orthonormal basis iff it is diagonalizable and pure (cf. Definition
1.3
). In the Archimedean case, all norms are pure, and a norm is diagonalizable if and only if it derives from a Euclidian/Hermitian scalar product. In the non-Archimedean case,
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is a dense subset of
\mathcal{N}(V)
(see §
1.4
below), and
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)=\mathcal{N}(V)
for several important classes of fields
K
.

Example 1.11
.

If
K
is trivially valued, any norm
\|\cdot\|
is diagonalizable. Indeed,
\|\cdot\|
determines a flag of subspaces (cf. Example
1.5
), and a basis
(e_{i})
of
V
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|
if and only if
(e_{\sigma(i)})
is compatible with the flag, for some permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
.

More generally, we have:

Lemma 1.12
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean. The following properties are equivalent:

(i)

K
is maximally complete;

(ii)

every norm on a finite dimensional
K
-vector space is diagonalizable.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is proved in
[
BGR
, 2.4.2/3]
. Assume conversely that
K
is not maximally complete, and pick a nontrivial immediate extension
F/K
. For any choice of
a\in F\setminus K
, we claim that the restriction
\|\cdot\|
of the absolute value of
F
to
K+aK\simeq K^{2}
is not diagonalizable. Assume the contrary. Since
\|\cdot\|
takes values in
|F|=|K|
, it then admits an orthonormal basis
(e_{1},e_{2})
. Using
\widetilde{F}=\widetilde{K}
, we find a unit
u\in K^{\circ}
such that
e_{1}-ue_{2}\in L^{\circ\circ}
, i.e.
\|e_{1}-ue_{2}\|<1
, contradicting the orthonormality of
(e_{1},e_{2})
.
∎

In the Archimedean case, diagonalizable norms are of course preserved by restriction and quotient. This is also true in the non-Archimedean case (cf.
[
BGR
, 2.4.1/5]
):

Lemma 1.13
.

Let
0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an exact sequence of finite dimensional
K
-vector spaces. If
\|\cdot\|
is a diagonalizable norm on
V
, then the induced norms on
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
are also diagonalizable.

The following codiagonalization result is crucial for what follows. It will be proved in §
1.5
, after the basic facts on duality have been discussed.

Proposition 1.14
.

For any two diagonalizable norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
, there exists a basis
(e_{i})
of
V
that is orthogonal for both
\|\cdot\|
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
.

This can be used to give a simple description of the restriction of
\dGI
to
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
.

Lemma 1.15
.

If
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
are codiagonalized in a basis
(e_{i})
, then

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\max_{i}\left|\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|}{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}\right|.

If
K
is non-Archimedean, we have more generally

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\log\max\left\{\max_{i}\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|},\max_{i}\frac{\|e^{\prime}_{i}\|}{\|e^{\prime}_{i}\|^{\prime}}\right\}

whenever
(e_{i})
and
(e^{\prime}_{i})
are orthogonal bases for
\|\cdot\|
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
, respectively.

Proof.

Assume first that
K
is Archimedean. We trivially have

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\left|\log\frac{\|v\|}{\|v\|^{\prime}}\right|\geq m:=\max_{i}\left|\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|}{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}\right|

Consider conversely
v=\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}
with
a\in K^{N}
. Then

\|v\|^{\prime 2}=\sum_{i}|a_{i}|^{2}\|e_{i}\|^{\prime 2}\leq e^{2m}\sum_{i}|a_{i}|\|e_{i}\|^{2}=e^{2m}\|v\|^{2}.

By symmetry, this shows that
e^{-m}\|\cdot\|\leq\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\leq e^{m}\|\cdot\|
, i.e.
\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\leq m
. In the non-Archimedean case the result follows from Lemma
1.16
below.
∎

Lemma 1.16
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean. Let
\|\cdot\|
be a diagonalizable norm with orthogonal basis
(e_{i})
, and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
be any (ultrametric) seminorm on
V
. Then

\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|v\|^{\prime}}{\|v\|}=\max_{i}\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|}.

Proof.

As above, we trivially have
\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\|v\|^{\prime}/\|v\|\geq m:=\max_{i}\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}/\|e_{i}\|
, and
v=\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}
satisfies
\|v\|^{\prime}\leq\max_{i}|a_{i}|\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}\leq m\max_{i}|a_{i}|\|e_{i}\|=m\|v\|
.
∎

We now discuss in more detail the structure of the set
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
of diagonalizable norms. To each basis
{\bf e}=(e_{i})
of
V
is associated an injective map

\iota_{\bf e}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V),

which takes
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
to the unique norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\bf e},\lambda}
that is diagonalized in
(e_{i})
and such that
\|e_{i}\|_{\bf e,\lambda}=e^{-\lambda_{i}}
. The image

\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}:=\iota_{\bf e}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)

is thus the set of norms that are diagonalized in the given basis
{\bf e}
, and is called an
apartment
(or
flat
) of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
. By definition,
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)=\bigcup_{\bf e}\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
, and the Goldman–Iwahori metric
\dGI
can then be characterized as follows.

Proposition 1.17
.

The restriction of
\dGI
to
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is the unique metric such that each
\iota_{\bf e}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
becomes an isometric embedding with respect to the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof.

Each
\iota_{\bf e}
is an isometric embedding by Lemma
1.15
, and uniqueness follows from the fact that any two points of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
belong to the image of some
\iota_{\bf e}
, by codiagonalization (Proposition
1.14
).
∎

This picture will be generalized to any symmetric norm on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
(and in particular to the
\ell^{2}
-norm) in Section
3
, leading to the description of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
as a Riemannian symmetric space/Euclidian building. In the present setting, the general construction of retractions onto an apartment in building theory specializes as follows (compare
[
Ger81
]
).

Definition 1.18
.

Let
{\bf e}=(e_{i})
be a basis of
V
, with apartment
\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}=\iota_{\bf e}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
. The
Gram–Schmidt projection

\rho_{\bf e}:\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
is defined by sending a norm
\|\cdot\|
to the unique norm
\|\cdot\|_{\bf e}
that is diagonalized in
{\bf e}
and such that

\|e_{i}\|_{\bf e}=\inf_{a\in K^{N}}\left\|e_{i}+\sum_{j<i}a_{j}e_{j}\right\|

for
i=1,\ldots,N
.

Setting
W_{i}:=\Vect(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i})
defines a complete flag
W_{\bullet}
in
V
, and
\|\cdot\|
induces a subquotient norm on each graded piece
W_{i}/W_{i-1}
, and hence a diagonalizable norm on the graded object
\Gr V=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq N}W_{i}/W_{i-1}
. The norm
\|\cdot\|_{\bf e}
can then be described as the corresponding norm on
V
under the isomorphism
V\simeq\Gr V
defined by
(e_{i})
. It is straightforward to see that
\rho_{\bf e}:\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
is a retraction, i.e. restricts to the identity on
\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
.

The chosen terminology comes from the Archimedean case, where the Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization process associates to a Euclidian/Hermitian norm
\|\cdot\|
and a basis
(e_{i})
the orthogonal basis
(e^{\prime}_{i})
obtained by projection of each
e_{i}
orthogonal to
W_{i-1}
, which satisfies
\|e_{i}\|_{\bf e}=\|e^{\prime}_{i}\|
.

1.4.
Approximation by diagonalizable norms

The goal of this section is to study the closure in
\mathcal{N}(V)
of the set
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
of diagonalizable norms.

Theorem 1.19
.

The space of diagonalizable norms
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

If
K
is Archimedean, then
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is closed in
\mathcal{N}(V)
, and each norm
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}(V)
is at distance at most
\tfrac{1}{2}\log N
of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
.

(ii)

If
K
is non-Archimedean, then
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is dense in
\mathcal{N}(V)
.

Closedness in (i) follows from the fact that Euclidian/Hermitian norms are characterized by the pararallelogram law

\|u+v\|^{2}+\|u-v\|^{2}=2\|u\|^{2}+2\|v\|^{2},

and the second half of (i) can be deduced from the John ellipsoid theorem; one can also use the simpler Auerbach lemma, whose proof will be basically repeated below. Density in (ii) is equivalent to the existence of
\alpha
-cartesian bases in the sense of
[
BGR
, 2.6.1/3]
, which will be recovered below by imitating the Auerbach argument.

Denote by
V^{\vee}
the dual of
V
, and by
\det V^{\vee}=\bigwedge^{N}V^{\vee}
its determinant line. Viewing an element
\omega\in\det V^{\vee}
as a multilinear form on
V
, we define its
operator norm
as

\|\omega\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})\in(V\setminus\{0\})^{N}}\frac{|\omega(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})|}{\|v_{1}\|\cdot\ldots\cdot\|v_{N}\|}.

This supremum is indeed finite by equivalence of norms.

Lemma 1.20
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be a norm on
V
, and pick a nonzero
\omega\in\det V^{\vee}
. For each basis
(e_{i})
of
V
and all
a\in K^{N}
, we then have

\max_{i}\|a_{i}e_{i}\|\leq\left(\frac{\|\omega\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|e_{1}\|\cdot\ldots\cdot\|e_{N}\|}{|\omega(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})|}\right)\|\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\|.

Proof.

The dual basis
(e_{i}^{\vee})
satisfies

\langle e_{i}^{\vee},v\rangle=\frac{\omega(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},v,e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{N})}{\omega(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})},

and hence

\max_{i}|\langle e_{i}^{\vee},v\rangle|\|e_{i}\|\leq\left(\frac{\|\omega\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|e_{1}\|\dots\|e_{N}\|}{|\omega(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})|}\right)\|v\|,

which is equivalent to the desired result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.19
.

Assume first that
K
is Archimedean. As noted above, closedness in (i) follows from the characterization of diagonalizable norms in terms of the parallelogram law. Let
\|\cdot\|
be any norm on
V
, and fix a nonzero determinant
\omega\in\det V^{\vee}
. By compactness, we may choose a basis
(e_{i})
of
V
with
\|e_{i}\|=1
and
\|\omega\|_{\mathrm{op}}=|\omega(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})|
. For each
p\in[1,\infty]
, denote by
\|\cdot\|_{p}
the
\ell^{p}
-norm in the basis
(e_{i})
. Lemma
1.20
and the triangle inequality yield
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\leq\|\cdot\|\leq\|\cdot\|_{1}
. Since
N^{-1/2}\|\cdot\|_{1}\leq\|\cdot\|_{2}\leq N^{1/2}\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, it follows that
N^{-1/2}\|\cdot\|_{2}\leq\|\cdot\|\leq N^{1/2}\|\cdot\|_{2}
, and hence
\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|_{2})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\log N
, which proves (i) since
\|\cdot\|_{2}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
.

Assume now that
K
is non-Archimedean, and pick any norm
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}(V)
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a basis
(e_{i})
such that

\|\omega\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\frac{|\omega(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})|}{\|e_{1}\|\dots\|e_{N}\|},

and Lemma
1.20
yields

(1.2)

\|\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\|\leq\max_{i}\|a_{i}e_{i}\|\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\|

for all
a\in K^{N}
. Denoting by
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
the norm diagonalized in
(e_{i})
and such that
\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}=\|e_{i}\|
, we infer
\|\cdot\|\leq\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|\cdot\|
, hence
\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\right)\leq\log(1+\varepsilon)
, and
\|\cdot\|
is thus in the closure of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
.
∎

1.5.
Duality

To each norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
is associated a dual norm
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
on the dual vector space
V^{\vee}
, defined by the usual formula

\|\mu\|^{\vee}=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mu,v\rangle|}{\|v\|}.

Again, the supremum is finite by equivalence of norms.

Theorem 1.21
.

The duality map
\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathcal{N}(V^{\vee})
is an involutive isometry with respect to the Goldman–Iwahori distances.

Lemma 1.22
.

If
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is diagonalizable, then so is
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
. Further, if
(e_{i})
is an orthogonal basis for
\|\cdot\|
, then the dual basis
(e_{i}^{\vee})
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
, and
\|e_{i}^{\vee}\|^{\vee}=\|e_{i}\|^{-1}
.

Proof.

In the Archimedean case,
\|\cdot\|
is Euclidian/Hermitian, and the result is well-known. In the non-Archimedean case, the result is a simple consequence of Lemma
1.16
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.21
.

It is straightforward to see that
\|\cdot\|\mapsto\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
is
1
-Lipschitz, it is enough to show that
(\|\cdot\|^{\vee})^{\vee}=\|\cdot\|
for all
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}(V)
. In the Archimedean case, this is a consequence of the Hahn–Banach theorem. In the non-Archimedean case, it follows from Lemma
1.22
and the density of diagonalizable norms in
\mathcal{N}(V)
(Theorem
1.19
).
∎

Lemma 1.23
.

If
W\subset V
is a linear subspace, the canonical embedding
(V/W)^{\vee}\hookrightarrow V^{\vee}
identifies the dual of the quotient norm
\|\cdot\|_{V/W}
with the restriction of
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
.

Proof.

The image of
(V/W)^{\vee}
in
V^{\vee}
is the space
W^{\perp}
of linear forms
\mu\in V^{\vee}
that vanish on
W
. Denoting by
\bar{v}\in V/W
the image of
v\in V
and by
\tilde{\mu}\in V^{\vee}
the image of
\mu\in(V/W)^{\vee}
, we have by definition

\|\tilde{\mu}\|^{\vee}=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\tilde{\mu},v\rangle|}{\|v\|}

and

\|\mu\|_{V/W}^{\vee}=\sup_{v\in V-W}\frac{|\langle\tilde{\mu},v\rangle|}{\|\bar{v}\|_{V/W}}.

Since
\|\bar{v}\|_{V/W}=\inf_{w\in W}\|v+w\|\leq\|v\|
and
\langle\tilde{\mu},v\rangle=0
for
v\in W
, we trivially have
\|\tilde{\mu}\|^{\vee}\leq\|\mu\|_{V/W}^{\vee}
. Conversely, we have for each
v\in V-W
and
w\in W

\frac{|\langle\tilde{\mu},v\rangle|}{\|v+w\|}=\frac{|\langle\tilde{\mu},v+w\rangle|}{\|v+w\|}\leq\|\tilde{\mu}\|^{\vee},

hence

\frac{|\langle\tilde{\mu},v\rangle|}{\|\bar{v}\|_{V/W}}=\sup_{w\in W}\frac{|\langle\tilde{\mu},v\rangle|}{\|v+w\|}\leq\|\tilde{\mu}\|^{\vee},

and taking the supremum over
v
yields the inequality in the other direction
\|\mu\|_{V/W}^{\vee}\leq\|\tilde{\mu}\|^{\vee}
and we conclude.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the codiagonalization result promised in Proposition
1.14
.

Proof of Proposition
1.14
.

That any two diagonalizable norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
are codiagonalizable is a standard fact in the Archimedean case, and we henceforth assume that
K
is non-Archimedean. Our argument extends the classical one of
[
GI
]
, which treats the case of a local field, following a suggestion of Marco Maculan, whom we warmly thank. Recall that a direct sum decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus V_{r}
is
orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|
if

\left\|\sum_{i}v_{i}\right\|=\max_{i}\|v_{i}\|

for all
v_{i}\in V_{i}
. Given
v\in V
and a linear form
\mu\in V^{\vee}
with
\langle\mu,v\rangle\neq 0
, it is straightforward to check that the decomposition
V=Kv\oplus\Ker\mu
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|
if and only if

\frac{|\langle\mu,w\rangle|}{|\langle\mu,v\rangle|}\leq\frac{\|w\|}{\|v\|}

for all
w\in V
. Arguing by induction on
\dim V
, we will thus be done if we prove the existence of
v\in V
and
\mu\in V^{\vee}
with
\langle\mu,v\rangle\neq 0
such that

(1.3)

\frac{|\langle\mu,w\rangle|}{|\langle\mu,v\rangle|}\leq\frac{\|w\|}{\|v\|}\leq\frac{\|w\|^{\prime}}{\|v\|^{\prime}}

for all
w\in V
, since
V=Kv\oplus\ker\mu
will then be orthogonal for both
\|\cdot\|
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
. Let
(e^{\prime}_{i})
be an orthogonal basis for
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
. By Lemma
1.16
, we have

\sup_{w\in W\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|w\|}{\|w\|^{\prime}}=\frac{\|e^{\prime}_{i}\|}{\|e^{\prime}_{i}\|^{\prime}}

for some
i
, and
v:=e^{\prime}_{i}
therefore satisfies
\|w\|/\|v\|\leq\|w\|^{\prime}/\|v\|^{\prime}
for all
w\in V
. Let now
(e_{j})
be an orthogonal basis for
\|\cdot\|
. Since the dual basis
(e_{j}^{\vee})
is orthogonal for the dual norm
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
, we similarly get

\|v\|=\sup_{\mu\in V^{\vee}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mu,v\rangle|}{\|\mu\|^{\vee}}=\frac{|\langle e_{j}^{\vee},v\rangle|}{\|e_{j}^{\vee}\|^{\vee}}

for some
j
. It follows that
\mu:=e_{j}^{\vee}
satisfies

|\langle\mu,v\rangle|=\|\mu\|^{\vee}\|v\|\geq\frac{|\langle\mu,w\rangle|}{\|w\|}\|v\|

for all
w\in V\setminus\{0\}
, and (
1.3
) follows.
∎

1.6.
Ground field extension

Let
V
be a finite dimensional
K
-vector space
V
,
F/K
be a complete field extension, and set
V_{F}:=V\otimes_{K}F
. When
K
is Archimedean, the only nontrivial case is
K=\mathbb{R}
,
F=\mathbb{C}
, by the Gelfand–Mazur theorem.

Definition 1.24
.

The
ground field extension
of a norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
is the norm
\|\cdot\|_{F}
on
V_{F}
defined by

•

\|w\|_{F}:=\inf\sum_{i}|b_{i}|\|v_{i}\|
if if
K
is Archimedean;

•

\|w\|_{F}:=\inf\max_{i}|b_{i}|\|v_{i}\|
if
K
is non-Archimedean;

where the infimum ranges in both cases over all decompositions
w=\sum_{i}b_{i}v_{i}
with
b_{i}\in F
and
v_{i}\in V
.

Proposition 1.25
.

Let
F/K
be a complete field extension.

(i)

For any norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
, the restriction of
\|\cdot\|_{F}
to
V
coincides with
\|\cdot\|
, and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the maximal norm on
V_{F}
with this property.

(ii)

The map
\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathcal{N}(V_{F})

\|\cdot\|\mapsto\|\cdot\|_{F}
is an isometric embedding with respect to the Goldman–Iwahori distances.

(iii)

If
K=\mathbb{R}
and
F=\mathbb{C}
, then
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{C}}
is conjugation invariant, and any conjugation invariant norm
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V_{\mathbb{C}}
that coincides with
\|\cdot\|
on
V
satisfies

\tfrac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{C}}\leq\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{C}}.

(iv)

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean, and let
\|\cdot\|
be a diagonalizable norm with orthogonal basis
(e_{i})
. Then
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is also a diagonalizable norm with orthogonal basis
(e_{i})
, viewed as a basis of
V_{F}
, and
\|e_{i}\|_{F}=\|e_{i}\|
.

(v)

Assume further that
\tilde{F}=\tilde{K}
and

(1.4)

\left\{\|e_{i}\|/\|e_{i}\|\mid 1\leq i,j\leq N\right\}\cap|F^{\times}|=\{1\}.

Then
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the only norm that extends
\|\cdot\|
to
V_{F}
.

Note that (v) is direct generalization of
[
CMor18
, Lemma 1.12]
.

Proof.

(i) is immediate, and implies for any two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V

\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})\geq\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}|\log\|v\|-\log\|v\|^{\prime}|=\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}).

The converse inequality
\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})\leq\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
is straightforward from the definition, hence (ii).

To prove (iii), pick any conjugation invariant norm
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V_{\mathbb{C}}
that coincides with
\|\cdot\|
on
V
. For each
w\in V_{\mathbb{C}}
we then have
\|w\|^{\prime}=\|\bar{w}\|^{\prime}
, hence
\|\Rea w\|=\|\Rea w\|^{\prime}=\|(w+\bar{w})/2\|^{\prime}\leq\|w\|^{\prime}
,
\|\Ima w\|=\|\Ima w\|^{\prime}\leq\|w\|^{\prime}
. Since
w=\Rea w+i\Ima w
, we infer

\|w\|_{\mathbb{C}}\leq\|\Rea w\|+\|\Ima w\|\leq 2\|w\|^{\prime}.

To see (iv), pick
w\in V_{F}
, and write
w=\sum_{j}b_{j}e_{j}
with
b_{j}\in F
. By definition,
\|w\|_{F}\leq\max_{j}|b_{j}|\|e_{j}\|
. Conversely, pick any decomposition
v=\sum_{i}b_{i}v_{i}
with
b_{i}\in F
and
v_{i}\in V
, and write
v_{i}=\sum_{j}a_{ij}e_{j}
with
a_{ij}\in K
. Then
v=\sum_{j}c_{j}e_{j}
with
c_{j}=\sum_{i}b_{i}b_{ij}
, and we need to show that

\max_{j}|c_{j}|\|e_{j}\|\leq\max_{i}|b_{i}|\|v_{i}\|.

This follows indeed from
\|v_{i}\|=\max_{j}|a_{ij}|\|e_{j}\|
and
|c_{j}|\leq\max_{i}|b_{i}||a_{ij}|
.

Now assume that
\tilde{F}=\tilde{K}
and (
1.4
) hold, and pick an extension
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
of
\|\cdot\|
to
V_{F}
. Given a nonzero tuple
(b_{i})
in
F
, we need to show that

\|\sum_{i}b_{i}e_{i}\|^{\prime}=c:=\max_{i}|b_{i}|\|e_{i}\|.

After reindexing, we may assume that

c=|b_{1}|\|e_{1}\|=\dots=|b_{r}|\|e_{r}\|>|b_{r+1}|\|e_{r+1}\|\geq\dots

For
1\leq i\leq r
, we have
\|e_{i}\|/\|e_{1}\|=|b_{1}b_{i}^{-1}|\in|F^{\times}|
, and (
1.4
) thus implies
\|e_{i}\|=\|e_{1}\|
, and hence also
|b_{i}b_{1}^{-1}|=1
. Since
\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{F}
, we can pick a unit
u_{i}\in K^{\circ}
such that
|b_{i}b_{1}^{-1}-u_{i}|<1
.
Set
v_{1}:=b_{1}\sum_{i\leq r}u_{i}e_{i}
,
v_{2}=\sum_{i\leq r}(b_{i}-b_{1}u_{i})e_{i}
, and
v_{3}:=\sum_{i>r}b_{i}e_{i}
, so that
v_{1}+v_{2}+v_{3}=\sum_{i}b_{i}e_{i}
.
Then

\|v_{1}\|^{\prime}=|b_{1}|\|\sum_{i\leq r}u_{i}e_{i}\|^{\prime}=|b_{1}|\|\sum_{i\leq r}u_{i}e_{i}\|=|b_{1}|\max_{i\leq r}|u_{i}|\|e_{i}\|=c,

\|v_{2}\|^{\prime}<|b_{1}|\max_{i\leq r}\|e_{i}\|=c,

and

\|v_{3}\|^{\prime}\leq\max_{i>r}|b_{i}|\|e_{i}\|<|b_{1}|\|e_{1}\|=c,

and we infer as desired
\|\sum_{i}b_{i}e_{i}\|^{\prime}=\|v_{1}+v_{2}+v_{3}\|^{\prime}=c
.
∎

As in the proof of
[
CMor18
, Theorem 4.1]
, we infer:

Lemma 1.26
.

Assume that
K
is trivially valued, and let
(V_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i})_{i\in I}
be an at most countable family of norms on finite dimensional
K
-vector spaces. We may then find a complete extension
F/K
with
F
nontrivially valued such that for each
i\in I
,
(\|\cdot\|_{i})_{F}
is the only norm on
(V_{i})_{F}
that coincides with
\|\cdot\|_{i}
on
V_{i}
.

Proof.

For each
i
, pick an orthogonal basis
(e_{ij})_{j\in J_{i}}
for
(V_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i})
(see Example
1.11
), and consider the finite set

S_{i}:=\left\{\log\|e_{ij}\|-\log\|e_{ij^{\prime}}\|\mid j,j^{\prime}\in J_{i}\right\}.

Set
F:=K(\!(t)\!)
, pick
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and endow
F
with the valuation
v_{F}
equal to
\alpha
times the
t
-adic valuation. Then
\log|F^{\times}|=v_{F}(F^{\times})=\alpha\mathbb{Z}
and
\tilde{F}=\tilde{K}
. According to Proposition
1.25
(v), it will thus be enough to show that
\alpha
can be chosen so that
\alpha\mathbb{Z}\cap S_{i}=\{0\}
for all
i
. Now
\bigcup_{i}\mathbb{Q}S_{i}
is countable, and it is then enough to choose
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\setminus\bigcup_{i}\mathbb{Q}S_{i}
.
∎

1.7.
Lattice norms

In this section,
K
is non-Archimedean, with associated real-valued valuation
v_{K}=-\log|\cdot|
. As for any valuation ring,
K^{\circ}
has the property that every finitely generated ideal is principal, which implies that a
K^{\circ}
-module
M
is flat if and only if it is torsion-free. If
M
is further finitely generated, then it is free (since
K^{\circ}
is local).

Definition 1.27
.

A
lattice
3
3

3

In the densely valued case, the present notion of lattice is more restrictive than the one used in
[
CMor18
, §1.3.3]
.
of
V
is a finite
K^{\circ}
-submodule
\mathcal{V}
of
V
such that
\mathcal{V}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K=V

Any lattice of
V
is thus of the form
\mathcal{V}=\sum_{i}K^{\circ}e_{i}
with
(e_{i})
a basis of
V
. A lattice
\mathcal{V}
determines a
lattice norm

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
on
V
, by setting

\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}}:=\inf\left\{|a|\mid a\in K,\,v\in a\mathcal{V}\right\}.

Lemma 1.28
.

Let
(e_{i})
be a
K^{\circ}
-basis of a lattice
\mathcal{V}
of
V
. Then
(e_{i})
is an orthonormal basis of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{V}
coincides with the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

Pick
v\in V
, and write
v=\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}
with
a\in K^{N}
. Given
a\in K
, we then have
v\in a\mathcal{V}
if and only if
|a_{i}|\leq|a|
, and hence
\|v\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\max_{i}|a_{i}|
. This means that
(e_{i})
is orthonormal for
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, and also implies that
\mathcal{V}
is the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
.
∎

Lemma 1.29
.

Denote by
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{latt}}(V)\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
the set of lattice norms.

(i)

A norm is a lattice norm if and only if it is a pure diagonalizable norm,
i.e.
a norm that admits an orthonormal basis.

(ii)

If
K
is trivially valued, then
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{latt}}(V)
reduces to the trivial norm on
V
. If
K
is discretely valued, with uniformizing parameter
\pi_{K}
, then
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{latt}}(V)
is discrete and closed in
\mathcal{N}(V)=\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
. Further, the (closed) unit ball
B
of any norm
\|\cdot\|
is a lattice, whose associated lattice norm
\|\cdot\|_{B}
satisfies

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|_{B})\leq v_{K}(\pi_{K}).

(iii)

If
K
is densely valued,
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{latt}}(V)
is dense in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
, and hence also in
\mathcal{N}(V)
. Further, the unit ball
B
of a norm
\|\cdot\|
is a lattice if and only
\|\cdot\|
is a lattice norm.

(iv)

Let
\|\cdot\|
be the lattice norm determined by a lattice
\mathcal{V}
of
V
, and
F/K
be a complete extension. Then the ground field extension
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the lattice norm determined by the lattice
\mathcal{V}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}F^{\circ}
of
V_{F}=V\otimes_{K}F
.

Proof.

(i) is a direct consequence of Lemma
1.28
, and implies the first part of (ii). Assume that
K
is discretely valued. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}\neq\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
be two distinct lattice norms, and pick a joint orthogonal basis
(e_{i})
as in Proposition
1.14
. We then have
\mathcal{V}=\sum_{i}K^{\circ}\pi_{K}^{m_{i}}e_{i}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\sum_{i}K^{\circ}\pi_{K}^{m^{\prime}_{i}}e_{i}
for some integers
m_{i},m^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and hence

\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}})=\max_{i}\left|\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|_{\mathcal{V}}}{\|e_{i}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}\right|

=v_{K}(\pi_{K})\max_{i}|m_{i}-m^{\prime}_{i}|\geq v_{K}(\pi_{K}).

This shows that
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{latt}}(V)
is discrete and closed. Next, let
\|\cdot\|
be any diagonalizable norm, pick an orthogonal basis
(e_{i})
for
\|\cdot\|
, and write a given
v\in V
as
v=\sum_{i}u_{i}\pi_{K}^{n_{i}}e_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
u_{i}
a unit. Then
\|v\|\leq 1
if and only if
|\pi_{K}|^{n_{i}}\|e_{i}\|\leq 1
for all
i
, and we infer
B=\sum_{i}K^{\circ}\pi_{K}^{m_{i}}e_{i}
with
m_{i}:=\lceil\log\|e_{i}\|/v_{K}(\pi_{K})\rceil
. In particular,
B
is a lattice with basis
(\pi_{K}^{m_{i}}e_{i})
, and hence

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|_{B})=\max_{i}\left|\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|_{B}}{\|e_{i}\|}\right|=\max_{i}\left|m_{i}v_{K}(\pi_{K})-\log\|e_{i}\|\right|\leq v_{K}(\pi_{K}).

Assume finally that
K
is densely valued. That
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{latt}}(V)
is dense in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is easily seen by approximating the values of a given diagonalizable norm
\|\cdot\|
on an orthogonal basis
(e_{i})
by elements of the dense subset
|K^{\times}|
of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Similarly, any norm
\|\cdot\|
is determined by its closed unit ball
B
, via

\|v\|=\inf\left\{|a|\mid a\in K,\,v\in aB\right\}.

As a result,
\|\cdot\|
is a lattice norm if and only if
B
is a lattice. Finally, (iv) is a direct consequence of Lemma
1.28
and Proposition
1.25
.
∎

As an illustration of these considerations, we have:

Example 1.30
.

Let
K
be a densely valued non-Archimedean field, and
F/K
be a finite extension. If the ring extension
F^{\circ}/K^{\circ}
is finite, then
F/K
is necessarily unramified,
i.e.
|F|=|K|
. Indeed, the absolute value
|\cdot|_{L}
of
L
is then a lattice norm, by Lemma
1.29
(iii), and hence
|L|=|K|
.

2.
Determinants and relative spectra

The goal of this section is to investigate induced norms on the determinant line, leading to the notion of
relative volume
of two norms. We relate the latter to the relative spectrum via a Minkowski-type theorem.

As before,
V
denotes a finite dimensional vector space over a complete valued field
K
, and we set
N:=\dim V
.

2.1.
The determinant of a norm

The
determinant line
of
V
is
\det V:=\bigwedge^{N}V
. We have a natural isomorphism
\det(V^{\vee})\simeq(\det V)^{\vee}
, induced by the pairing

\langle v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{N},\mu_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mu_{N}\rangle=\det\left(\langle v_{i},\mu_{j}\rangle\right).

In particular, if
(e_{i})
is basis of
V
with dual basis
(e_{i}^{\vee})
, then

\left(e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}\right)^{\vee}=e_{1}^{\vee}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}^{\vee}.

Definition 2.1
.

To each norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
, we associate a norm
\det\|\cdot\|
on
\det V
by setting for
\tau\in\det V

\det\|\tau\|=\inf_{\tau=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{N}}\prod_{i}\|v_{i}\|,

where the infimum runs over all decompositions
\tau=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{N}
with
v_{i}\in V
.

We abuse the notation slightly by writing
\det\|\tau\|
for the value of
\det\|\cdot\|
on
\tau
. By construction,
\det\|\cdot\|
is the largest seminorm on
\det V
with the submultiplicativity property

(2.1)

\det\|v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{N}\|\leq\prod_{i}\|v_{i}\|

for all
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in V
. That it is actually a norm follows from the next result, which is readily checked.

Lemma 2.2
.

If we view the dual
\tau^{\vee}\in\det V^{\vee}
of a nonzero
\tau\in\det V
as a multilinear form on
V
, then
\left(\det\|\tau\|\right)^{-1}
coincides with the operator norm

\|\tau^{\vee}\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{N}\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\tau^{\vee}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})|}{\|v_{1}\|\dots\|v_{N}\|}.

From the definition, we immediately get:

Lemma 2.3
.

The map
\det:\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathcal{N}(\det V)
is
N
-Lipschitz continuous with respect to
\dGI
-metrics,
i.e.
we have

\dGI(\det\|\cdot\|,\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\leq N\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})

for any two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V
.

Computing the determinant of a norm is typically a hard problem in the Archimedean case.

Example 2.4
.

Consider the usual
\ell^{p}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{p}
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, and set
\tau:=e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}
, with
(e_{i})
the canonical basis. Then

•

\det\|\tau\|_{p}=1
for
p\in[1,2]
;

•

\det\|\tau\|_{p}<1
for
p>2
.

For
p=\infty
, determining the precise value of
\det\|\tau\|_{\infty}
amounts to maximizing the determinant of a
N\times N
-matrix with entries in
\{\pm 1\}
, and is known as the
Hadamard maximal determinant problem
. By
[
CL65
]
, we have for instance

N^{-\frac{N}{2}}\leq\det\|\tau\|_{\infty}\leq N^{-\frac{N}{2}\left(1-\frac{\log(4/3)}{\log N}\right)}.

The lower bound is achieved if and only if there exists an
\ell^{2}
-orthogonal basis with entries in
\{\pm 1\}
(which implies that
N
is a multiple of
4
), but the exact value
\det\|\tau\|_{\infty}
is unknown in the general case.

2.2.
Determinants of diagonalizable norms

As we shall see in this section, the determinant of a diagonalizable norm is very well-behaved. In the non-Archimedean case, this will extend to all norms, by density of diagonalizable norms.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
\|\cdot\|
is diagonalizable, then a basis
(e_{i})
of
V
satisfies

\det\|e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}\|=\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|

if and only if
(e_{i})
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|
.

Corollary 2.6
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean, and let
\mathcal{V}
be a lattice of
V
. Then
\det\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\|\cdot\|_{\det\mathcal{V}}
is the norm determined by the lattice
\det\mathcal{V}:=\bigwedge^{N}\mathcal{V}
of
\det V
.

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

When
K
is Archimedean, the result is equivalent to the classical Hadamard inequality for the determinant of a matrix. Assume now that
K
is non-Archimedean, and let
(e_{i})
be an orthogonal basis for
\|\cdot\|
. We need to show that each basis
(v_{i})
such that
v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{N}=e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}
satisfies
\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|\leq\prod_{i}\|v_{i}\|
. If we write
v_{i}=\sum_{j}a_{ij}e_{j}
with
a_{ij}\in K
, then
\det(a_{ij})=1
. Expanding out the determinant and using the ultrametric inequality, we get
\prod_{i}|a_{i\sigma(i)}|\geq 1
for some permutation
\sigma
. Since
\|\cdot\|
is diagonalized in
(e_{i})
, we have

\|v_{i}\|=\max_{j}|a_{ij}|\|e_{j}\|\geq\left|a_{i\sigma(i)}\right|\left\|e_{\sigma(i)}\right\|,

and we obtain as desired

\prod_{i}\|v_{i}\|\geq\prod_{i}\left|a_{i\sigma(i)}\right|\left\|e_{\sigma(i)\|}\right\|=\left(\prod_{i}|a_{i\sigma(i)}|\right)\left(\prod_{i}\|e_{\sigma(i)}\right)\geq\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|.

Conversely, any basis
(e_{i})
satisfying
\det\|e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}\|=\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|
, as a direct consequence of Lemma
1.20
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

If
\|\cdot\|
is a diagonalizable norm on
V
, then
\det\left(\|\cdot\|^{\vee}\right)=\left(\det\|\cdot\|\right)^{\vee}
under the canonical isomorphism
\det\left(V^{\vee}\right)\simeq\left(\det V\right)^{\vee}
.

Proof.

Let
(e_{i})
be an orthogonal basis for
\|\cdot\|
. By Lemma
1.22
, the dual basis
(e_{i}^{\vee})
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
, and
\|e_{i}^{\vee}\|^{\vee}=\|e_{i}\|^{-1}
. By Lemma
2.5
, we infer

\det\left\|e_{1}^{\vee}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}^{\vee}\right\|^{\vee}=\prod_{i}\left\|e_{i}^{\vee}\right\|^{\vee}=\left(\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|\right)^{-1}=\left(\det\|e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}\|\right)^{-1},

hence the result.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be a diagonalizable norm on
V
, and consider an exact sequence of vector spaces

0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0,

with induced norms
\|\cdot\|^{\prime},\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime}
on
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
. Under the canonical isomorphism

\det V\simeq\det V^{\prime}\otimes\det V^{\prime\prime},

we then have

\det\|\cdot\|=\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\otimes\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime}.

Proof.

Set
N^{\prime}:=\dim V^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime\prime}:=\dim V^{\prime\prime}
, and denote by
\pi:V\to V^{\prime\prime}
the given surjection. Pick nonzero
\tau^{\prime}\in\det V^{\prime}
,
\tau^{\prime\prime}\in\det V^{\prime\prime}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Definition
2.1
, we may then find
v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}\in V^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime\prime}}\in V^{\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime}=v^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}
,
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\pi(v^{\prime\prime}_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\pi(v^{\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime\prime}})
,

\prod_{i}\|v^{\prime}_{i}\|\leq(1+\varepsilon)\det\|\tau^{\prime}\|

and

\prod_{i}\|v^{\prime\prime}_{i}\|\leq(1+\varepsilon)\det\|\tau^{\prime\prime}\|

The isomorphism
\det V^{\prime}\otimes\det V^{\prime\prime}\simeq\det V
maps
\tau^{\prime}\otimes\tau^{\prime\prime}
to

v^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v^{\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime\prime}},

which satisfies

\det\|\tau^{\prime}\otimes\tau^{\prime\prime}\|=\det\|v^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v^{\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime\prime}}\|

\leq\prod_{i}\|v^{\prime}_{i}\|\prod_{i}\|v^{\prime\prime}_{j}\|\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}(\det\|\tau^{\prime}\|^{\prime})(\det\|\tau^{\prime\prime}\|^{\prime\prime}),

hence
\det\|\cdot\|\leq\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\otimes\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime}
. By Lemma
1.23
, we dually have

\det(\|\cdot\|^{\vee})\leq\det(\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime\vee})\otimes\det(\|\cdot\|^{\prime\vee}).

Since
\|\cdot\|
is diagonalizable, so are
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime}
, by Lemma
1.13
. By Lemma
2.7
, we thus have
(\det\|\cdot\|)^{-1}\leq(\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\otimes(\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime})^{-1}
, hence the result.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be a diagonalizable norm on
V
, and
F/K
be a complete field extension. Then
\det(\|\cdot\|_{F})=(\det\|\cdot\|)_{F}
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
1.25
together with Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

If
K
is non-Archimedean, Lemma
2.7
, Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
hold for all norms.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.19
, diagonalizable norms are dense in the set of all norms, and we conclude by continuity of
\det
(Lemma
2.3
).
∎

In the Archimedean case, both Lemma
2.7
and Lemma
2.8
fail in general for non-diagonalizable norms.

Example 2.11
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. The dual norm
\|\cdot\|^{\vee}
is the
\ell^{1}
-norm, and Example
2.4
thus shows that
\det(\|\cdot\|^{\vee})\neq(\det\|\cdot\|)^{\vee}
. Also, the exact sequence

0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0

with
V^{\prime}=Ke_{1}
,
V^{\prime\prime}=Ke_{2}
shows that
\det\|\cdot\|<(\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\otimes(\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime})
.

Finally, recall from §
1.3
that each basis
{\bf e}=(e_{i})
of
V
defines an apartment
\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}=\iota_{\bf e}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
and a Gram–Schmidt projection
\rho_{\bf e}:\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
. For later use, we show:

Lemma 2.12
.

For each diagonalizable norm
\|\cdot\|
, we have
\det\|\cdot\|=\det\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)
.

Proof.

Denote by
W_{i}=\Vect(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i})
the complete flag defined by
{\bf e}
. By Lemma
2.8
, we have

\det\|\cdot\|=\bigotimes_{i}\det\|\cdot\|_{W_{i}/W_{i-1}},

under the identification
\det V\simeq\bigotimes_{i}\det(W_{i}/W_{i-1})
. By definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\bf e}:=\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)
, we infer

\det\|e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}\|=\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|_{\bf e}=\det\|e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{N}\|_{\bf e},

where the second equality follows from Lemma
2.5
.
∎

2.3.
Relative volume

We introduce the following ’additive version’ of
[
Tem16
, 2.4.3]
.

Definition 2.13
.

The
relative volume
of two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V
is defined as the real number

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}):=\log\left(\frac{\det\|\cdot\|^{\prime}}{\det\|\cdot\|}\right).

Proposition 2.14
.

The relative volume satisfies the following properties.

(i)

cocycle formula:

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime})+\vol(\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}).

(ii)

homogeneity:

\vol(\|\cdot\|,e^{c}\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})+cN\text{ for all }c\in\mathbb{R}.

(iii)

monotonicity:

\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\leq\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime}\Longrightarrow\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\leq\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime\prime}).

(iv)

Lipschitz continuity:

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{1})-\vol(\|\cdot\|_{2},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{2})\right|\leq N\left(\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2})+\dGI(\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{2})\right).

(v)

if
F/K
is a complete field extension and
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
are two norms on
V
with ground field extensions
\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F}
to
V_{F}
, then

\vol(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})=\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})

in the diagonalizable or non-Archimedean case, and

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})-\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\right|\leq 2N\log N

in the general Archimedean case.

(vi)

if
0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0
is an exact sequence, the induced norms
\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime}},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{V^{\prime}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime\prime}}
,
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{V^{\prime\prime}}
satisfy

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime}},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{V^{\prime}}\right)+\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime\prime}},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{V^{\prime\prime}}\right)

in the diagonalizable or non-Archimedean case, and

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})-\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime}},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{V^{\prime}}\right)-\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime\prime}},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{V^{\prime\prime}}\right)\right|\leq 2N\log N

in general.

Proof.

The first three properties are obvious, and imply the fourth one as a formal consequence. In the diagonalizable and non-Archimedean case, (v) and (vi) are consequences of Lemma
2.9
, Lemma
2.8
and Corollary
2.10
. If
K
is Archimedean, Theorem
1.19
yields diagonalizable norms
\|\cdot\|_{0},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0}
with

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\log N,\,\,\,\dGI(\|\cdot\|^{\prime},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\log N.

Then

\vol((\|\cdot\|_{0})_{F},(\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0})_{F})=\vol(\|\cdot\|_{0},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0}),

while (iv) yields

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})-\vol(\|\cdot\|_{0},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0})\right|\leq N\log N

and

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})-\vol((\|\cdot\|_{0})_{F},(\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0})_{F})\right|\leq N\log N,

since ground field extension is an isometry for
\dGI
, by Proposition
1.25
. Thus

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})-\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\right|\leq\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{F},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{F})-\vol((\|\cdot\|_{0})_{F},(\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0})_{F})\right|

+\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{0},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{0})-\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\right|\leq 2N\log N.

The proof of (vi) in the general Archimedean case is similar.
∎

As we next show, in the Archimedean case, the relative volume is equivalent to the (logarithmic) volume ratio. The non-Archimedean case will be analyzed in the next section.

Proposition 2.15
.

Assume that
K
is Archimedean, and let
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
be two norms on
V
, with unit balls
B,B^{\prime}
.

(i)

If
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
are diagonalizable, then

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\frac{1}{[K:\mathbb{R}]}\log\left(\frac{\vol(B)}{\vol(B^{\prime})}\right),

where
\vol
is any choice of Haar measure on
V
.

(ii)

In the general case, we have

(2.2)

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})-\frac{1}{[K:\mathbb{R}]}\log\left(\frac{\vol(B)}{\vol(B^{\prime})}\right)\right|\leq 2N\log N.

Proof.

If
\|\cdot\|
,
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
are diagonalizable, we can pick an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})
for
\|\cdot\|
in which
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
is diagonalized, and the change-of-variable formula yields

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\log\prod_{i}\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}=\frac{1}{[K:\mathbb{R}]}\log\left(\frac{\vol(B)}{\vol(B^{\prime})}\right),

which proves (i). The proof of (ii) is entirely similar to that of (v) in Proposition
2.14
.
∎

Here again, the error term in (
2.2
) is generally nonzero in the Archimedean non-diagonalizable case.

Example 2.16
.

By Example
2.4
, the
\ell^{1}
and
\ell^{2}
norms
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfy
\vol(\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2})=0
, while the volume of the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{1}
is strictly smaller than that of
\|\cdot\|_{2}
.

2.4.
The content of a torsion module

We assume in this section that
K
is non-Archimedean and nontrivially valued, with associated valuation
v_{K}=-\log|\cdot|
. The next result was proved for instance in
[
Sch13
, Proposition 2.10]
(see also
[
Tem16
, Corollary 2.3.8]
).

Lemma 2.17
.

Every finitely presented torsion
K^{\circ}
-module
M
is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of cyclic modules

M\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}K^{\circ}/a_{i}K^{\circ}.

with
a_{i}\in K^{\circ\circ}
. Further,
r
and the sequence
v_{K}(a_{1}),\ldots,v_{K}(a_{r})
are uniquely determined by
M
, up to permutation.

Proof.

Pick a presentation
(K^{\circ})^{a}\to(K^{\circ})^{b}\to M\to 0
. The image
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
of
(K^{\circ})^{a}
in
\mathcal{V}:=(K^{\circ})^{b}
is finitely generated and torsion free, hence a free submodule. Since
\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is torsion,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
are both lattices in
V:=K^{b}
. We claim that
V
admits a basis
(e_{i})
such that
\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{i}K^{\circ}e_{i}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i}K^{\circ}a_{i}e_{i}
for some nonzero
a_{i}\in K^{\circ}
, which will yield as desired
M\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}K^{\circ}/a_{i}K^{\circ}
with
v_{K}(a_{i})>0
(since
K^{\circ}/a_{i}K^{\circ}=0
when
v_{K}(a_{i})=0
). Indeed, Proposition
1.14
yields a basis
(e_{i})
that jointly diagonalizes the lattice norms
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
. Since lattice norms take values in
|K|
, we can arrange that
\|e_{i}\|_{\mathcal{V}}=1
after multiplying each
e_{i}
by a scalar. Since
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
, we then have
\|e_{i}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}=|a_{i}^{-1}|
for some nonzero
a_{i}\in K^{\circ}
, and we get the claim by Lemma
1.28
. The uniqueness part is proved as in the usual structure theorem for torsion modules over PID’s.
∎

Definition 2.18
.

The
content
of a finitely presented torsion module
M
is defined as

\cont(M):=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{K}(a_{i})\in(0,+\infty).

Note that this is
-\log
of the content as defined in
[
Tem16
, 2.6.1]
. Alternatively,
\cont(M)
is obtained by applying
v_{K}
to the fractional ideal sheaf
\{a\in K\mid a\cdot\det M=0\}
.

Example 2.19
.

If
K
is discretely valued with uniformizing parameter
\pi_{K}
, then
\cont(M)=v_{K}(\pi_{K})\ell(M)
with
\ell(M)
the length of
M
.

The content is closely related to the relative volume. Indeed, as we saw during the proof of Lemma
2.17
, every finitely presented torsion module is the quotient of two lattices in the same vector space, and we have:

Lemma 2.20
.

If
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
are lattices in a finite dimensional vector space
V
, then

\cont(\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{\prime})=\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\right).

Assuming now that
K
is discretely valued, we conclude this section with an analogue of Proposition
2.15
, relating the relative volume to the virtual length used in
[
BG+16
]
. Recall that the virtual length
\ell(\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}
of two lattices
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
in a
K
-vector space is defined as

\ell(\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{\prime}):=\ell(\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime})-\ell(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}/\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime})

for any lattice
\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
contained in both
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
(cf.
[
BG+16
, Definition 4.1.1]
,
[
Ser
, III,§1]
).

Proposition 2.21
.

Assume that
K
is discretely valued with uniformizing parameter
\pi_{K}
, and let
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
be two norms on
V
. Denote by
B,B^{\prime}
their unit balls, and by
\|\cdot\|_{B},\|\cdot\|_{B^{\prime}}
the associated lattice norms. Then

\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{B},\|\cdot\|_{B^{\prime}}\right)=v_{K}(\pi_{K})\ell(B/B^{\prime})=\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})+O(N).

Note that the absolute value of
K
is normalized by
v_{K}(\pi_{K})=1
in
[
BG+16
]
.

Proof of Proposition
2.21
.

The first equality follows from Example
2.19
. By Lemma
1.28
, we further have
\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|_{B})\leq v_{K}(\pi_{K})
and
\dGI(\|\cdot\|^{\prime},\|\cdot\|_{B^{\prime}})\leq v_{K}(\pi_{K})
, and hence
\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\vol(\|\cdot\|_{B},\|\cdot\|_{B^{\prime}})+O(N)
by
N
-Lipschitz continuity of
\vol
.
∎

2.5.
Relative spectra

We introduce a general notion of relative spectrum of two norms, and relate it to relative volumes. In the Archimedean (resp. discretely valued) case, the results of this section can be traced back to Minkowski’s work on successive minima (resp. Mahler’s paper
[
Mah41
]
).

Definition 2.22
.

Let
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(V)
be two norms on
V
. We define the
relative spectrum
of
\|\cdot\|
with respect to
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
as the finite decreasing sequence

\lambda_{1}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})

defined by the minmax-type formulas

(2.3)

\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}):=\sup_{W\subset V,\,\dim W\geq i}\left(\inf_{w\in W\setminus\{0\}}\log\frac{\|w\|^{\prime}}{\|w\|}\right).

The following properties are immediate to check.

Lemma 2.23
.

For any two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V
we have:

(i)

\lambda_{1}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\left(\log\|v\|^{\prime}-\log\|v\|\right)
;

(ii)

\lambda_{N}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\inf_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\left(\log\|v\|^{\prime}-\log\|v\|\right)=-\lambda_{1}(\|\cdot\|^{\prime},\|\cdot\|)
;

(iii)

\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\max\left\{\lambda_{1}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}),\lambda_{1}(\|\cdot\|^{\prime},\|\cdot\|)\right\}
;

(iv)

for each
i
,
\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
is a
1
-Lipschitz continuous function of
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
with respect to the Goldman–Iwahori distance
\dGI
.

The next result justifies the chosen terminology.

Proposition 2.24
.

Assume that
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
are diagonalizable. Choose a basis
(e_{i})
of
V
in which both norms are diagonalized, as in Proposition
1.14
, and order it so that

\frac{\|e_{1}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{1}\|}\geq\dots\geq\frac{\|e_{N}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{N}\|}.

Then

\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|}.

Proof.

Set
W_{i}:=\Vect(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i})
,
W^{\prime}_{i}:=\Vect(e_{i},\ldots,e_{N})
, and observe that

\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|}=\inf_{w\in W_{i}\setminus\{0\}}\log\frac{\|w\|^{\prime}}{\|w\|}=\sup_{w\in W^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\{0\}}\log\frac{\|w\|^{\prime}}{\|w\|}.

By (
2.3
), the first equality yields
\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|}\leq\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
. On the other hand, each subspace
W\subset V
with
\dim W\geq i
satisfies
W\cap W^{\prime}_{i}\neq\{0\}
for dimension reason, and hence

\inf_{w\in W\setminus\{0\}}\log\frac{\|w\|^{\prime}}{\|w\|}\leq\sup_{w\in W^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\{0\}}\log\frac{\|w\|^{\prime}}{\|w\|}=\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|},

and using (
2.3
) again yields
\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\leq\log\frac{\|e_{i}\|^{\prime}}{\|e_{i}\|}
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the following analogue of Minkowski’s second theorem.

Theorem 2.25
.

Let
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
be two norms on
V
. If
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
are diagonalizable, or
K
is non-Archimedean, then

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}).

Otherwise,

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\right|\leq 2N\log N.

Proof.

Note that
\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
and
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
are both
N
-Lipschitz continuous in
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
, by Proposition
2.14
and Lemma
2.23
, respectively. When both norms are diagonalizable, Lemma
2.5
and Proposition
2.24
yield

\vol(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}).

If
K
is non-Archimedean, this propagates to all norms, by density of diagonalizable norms and (Lipschitz) continuity. Finally, the case of arbitrary norms in the Archimedean case is handled just as point (v) in Proposition
2.14
, by choosing diagonalizable norms at distance at most
\tfrac{1}{2}\log N
of
\|\cdot\|
,
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
.
∎

Example 2.26
.

By Example
2.4
, the
\ell^{1}
and
\ell^{2}
norms
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfy
\vol(\|\cdot\|_{2},\|\cdot\|_{1})=0
, while
\lambda_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{2},\|\cdot\|_{1})=\log\sqrt{2}
and
\lambda_{2}(\|\cdot\|_{2},\|\cdot\|_{1})=0
.

3.
Alternative metric structures on spaces of norms

The goal of this section, which stands somewhat apart from the rest of the paper, is to exploit the properties of determinants of norms to endow the space of diagonalizable norms with natural metric structures, recovering in particular the Bruhat–Tits metric in the non-Archimedean case.

In this section,
K
is again an arbitrary complete valued field, and
V
an
N
-dimensional
K
-vector space.

3.1.
The triangle inequality

The relative spectrum of two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(V)
defines a point
\lambda\left(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\right)
in the rational polyhedral cone

\mathcal{C}:=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\mid\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}\right\}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{N}/\mathfrak{S}_{N},

which is a Weyl chamber for the Weyl group
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
of
\GL_{N}
. Given an
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-invariant norm
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, set

\dist_{\chi}\left(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\right):=\chi\left(\lambda\left(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\right)\right).

The resulting function
\dist_{\chi}:\mathcal{N}(V)\times\mathcal{N}(V)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is symmetric, with
\dist_{\chi}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=0
if and only if
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
. The following result is inspired by Gerardin’s proof of
[
Ger81
, 2.4.7, Corollaire 2]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

For each
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-invariant norm
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\dist_{\chi}
satisfies the triangle inequality on
