\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
, and is characterized as the unique metric on
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
for which

\iota_{\bf e}:(\mathbb{R}^{N},\chi)\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V),\dist_{\chi})

is an isometric embedding for each basis
{\bf e}
of
V
.

By Proposition
1.17
, the Goldman–Iwahori metric
\dGI
on
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
corresponds to the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. By equivalence of norms on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, any metric
\dist_{\chi}
produced by Theorem
3.1
is Lipschitz equivalent to
\dGI
. Besides the latter, the most important case is the Euclidian metric
\dist_{2}
induced by the
\ell^{2}
-norm:

Example 3.2
.

When
K
is Archimedean,
\dist_{2}
coincides with the Riemannian metric of the symmetric space
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)\simeq\GL_{N}(K)/\mathrm{U}_{N}(K)
(see Theorem
3.7
below). When
K
is non-Archimedean,
(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V),\dist_{2})
is a realization of the Bruhat–Tits building of
\GL_{N}(K)
with its Euclidian metric, see for instance
[
Par00
, Chapter III]
. In both cases,
(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V),\dist_{2})
is a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
metric space.

Corollary 3.3
.

If
K
is non-Archimedean, the space
\mathcal{N}(V)
is complete with respect to the metric
\dist_{\chi}
, which is characterized as the unique compatible metric such that
\iota_{\bf e}:(\mathbb{R}^{N},\chi)\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{N}(V),\dist_{\chi})
is an isometric embedding for all bases
{\bf e}
. For
\chi=\ell^{2}
,
(\mathcal{N}(V),\dist_{2})
is a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
metric space.

Proof.

On
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
,
\dist_{\chi}
is equivalent to
\dist_{\infty}
and satisfies the triangle inquality. This is also the case on
\mathcal{N}(V)
, by density of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
. Since
\mathcal{N}(V)
is complete for
d_{\infty}
, it is also complete for
d_{\chi}
, and is thus the completion of
(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V),d_{\chi})
. Conversely, any metric on
\mathcal{N}(V)
with the stated property must coincide with
\dist_{\chi}
on the dense subset
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
, hence everywhere. For
\chi=\ell^{2}
, the Bruhat–Tits building
(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V),\dist_{2})
is a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
metric space, and this property is preserved under completion.
∎

As in the construction of the Euclidian metric on any Euclidian building, the key to the proof of Theorem
3.1
is to show that the Gram–Schmidt projections introduced in Definition
1.18
are distance-decreasing.

Lemma 3.4
.

For each basis
{\bf e}
of
V
, the Gram–Schmidt projection
\rho_{\bf e}:\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
satisfies

(3.1)

\dist_{\chi}\left(\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|),\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\right)\leq\dist_{\chi}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})

for all
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
.

We first recall some elementary facts.

Definition 3.5
.

Given
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\mid\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}\right\}
, one says that
\lambda
is
majorized by
\lambda^{\prime}
, written
\lambda\preceq\lambda^{\prime}
, if
\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda^{\prime}_{1}+\dots+\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
, with equality for
i=N
.

Lemma 3.6
.

We have
\lambda\preceq\lambda^{\prime}
if and only
\lambda
belongs to the convex envelope of the
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-orbit of
\lambda^{\prime}
, and then
\chi(\lambda^{\prime})\leq\chi(\lambda)
for any
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-invariant norm
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to see that any
\lambda
in the convex envelope of the
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-orbit of
\lambda^{\prime}
satisfies
\lambda\preceq\lambda^{\prime}
. As observed in
[
Rad52
]
, the converse is a simple consequence of the Hahn–Banach theorem. Assuming indeed that
\lambda\preceq\lambda^{\prime}
, it is enough to show that for each
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
there exists
\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
with

\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\lambda_{i}\leq\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\lambda^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}.

Choose
\sigma
such that
\mu_{\sigma(1)}\geq\dots\geq\mu_{\sigma(N)}
. Then

\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\lambda_{i}=\sum_{i}\mu_{\sigma(i)}\lambda_{\sigma(i)}=\sum_{i<N}\left(\mu_{\sigma(1)}-\mu_{\sigma(i+1)}\right)\left(\lambda_{\sigma(1)}+\dots+\lambda_{\sigma(i)}\right)+\mu_{\sigma(N)}\left(\lambda_{\sigma(1)}+\dots+\lambda_{\sigma(N)}\right)

\leq\sum_{i<N}\left(\mu_{\sigma(1)}-\mu_{\sigma(i+1)}\right)\left(\lambda^{\prime}_{1}+\dots+\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\right)+\mu_{\sigma(N)}\left(\lambda^{\prime}_{1}+\dots+\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\right)=\sum_{i}\mu_{\sigma(i)}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\lambda^{\prime}_{\sigma^{-1}(i)},

and it remains to set
\rho:=\sigma^{-1}
. Assume finally that
\lambda\preceq\lambda^{\prime}
, and hence
\lambda=\sum_{j}t_{j}\mu_{j}
with
\mu_{i}

\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-equivalent to
\lambda^{\prime}
,
t_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\sum_{j}t_{j}=1
, by what we just saw. By
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
-invariance of
\chi
, we infer

\chi(\lambda)\leq\sum_{j}t_{j}\chi(\mu_{i})=\sum_{j}t_{j}\chi(\lambda^{\prime})=\chi(\lambda^{\prime}).

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

By Lemma
3.6
, it will be enough to show that the relative spectrum
\lambda
(resp.
\mu
) of
\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
with respect to
\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|^{\prime})
(resp.
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|
) satisfy

\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{i}
for all
i
, with equality for
i=N
. Set
W:=\Vect(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i})
, and observe that the restriction
\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)_{W}
of
\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)
to
W
satisfies by definition

\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)_{W}=\rho_{{\bf e}_{W}}(\|\cdot\|_{W})

with
{\bf e}_{W}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i})
. By Lemma
2.12
, we thus have
\det(\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)_{W})=\det(\|\cdot\|_{W})
, and Theorem
2.25
yields

\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{i}=\vol\left(\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|)_{W},\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|^{\prime})_{W}\right)

=\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{W},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{W}\right)=\sum_{j\leq i}\lambda_{j}(\|\cdot\|_{W},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{W})

\leq\sum_{j\leq i}\lambda_{j}(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})=\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{i},

where the last inequality follows directly from (
2.3
), and is an equality when
i=N
, i.e.
W=V
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

By construction,
\iota_{\bf e}
is an isometric embedding with respect to
\chi
and
\dist_{\chi}
, and the latter therefore satisfies the triangle inequality on each apartment
\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}=\iota_{\bf e}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Pick three diagonalizable norms
\|\cdot\|_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
,
i=1,2,3
. We may then choose a basis
{\bf e}
with
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}\in\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
. Since
\dist_{\chi}
satisfies the triangle inequality on
\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
, we have

\dist_{\chi}\left(\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)=\dist_{\chi}\left(\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|_{1}),\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|_{2})\right)

\leq\dist_{\chi}\left(\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|_{1}),\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|_{3})\right)+\dist_{\chi}\left(\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|_{3}),\rho_{\bf e}(\|\cdot\|_{2})\right),

which shows that
\dist_{\chi}
satisfies the triangle inequality on
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
, by (
3.1
).
∎

3.2.
The Archimedean case: Finsler metrics

Assume that
K
is Archimedean, i.e.
K=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, and denote by
H(V)
the real vector space of all quadratic/Hermitian forms
h
on
V
. Each diagonalizable norm
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is then associated to a positive definite form
\gamma(v):=\|v\|^{2}
, thereby defining an embedding of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
as an open convex subset of
H(V)
. Further,
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is diffeomorphic to the symmetric space
\GL_{N}(K)/\mathrm{U}_{N}(K)
, with
\mathrm{U}_{N}(K)
the unitary/orthogonal group.

As we now show, the metric
\dist_{\chi}
constructed above is induced by a natural Finsler metric on
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
. Recall first that for each
h,\gamma\in H(V)
with
\gamma
positive definite, one can find a basis
{\bf e}=(e_{i})
of
V
which is orthonormal for
\gamma
and orthogonal for
h
, i.e.

\gamma\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}|a_{i}|^{2}

and

h\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}e_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}|a_{i}|^{2}

for all
a_{i}\in K
. The spectrum
(\lambda_{i})
is independent of the choice of
{\bf e}
up to ordering, hence defines a point
\lambda_{\gamma}(h)\in\mathcal{C}
, and we then have for any two
h,h^{\prime}\in H(V)

\lambda_{\gamma}(h+h^{\prime})\preceq\lambda_{\gamma}(h)+\lambda_{\gamma}(h^{\prime}).

This is indeed a simple consequence of the min-max principle, known as the
Ky Fan inequality
. Given a symmetric norm
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, it follows as in Lemma
3.6
that setting for each
\gamma\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)

|h|_{\chi,\gamma}:=\chi\left(\lambda_{g}(h)\right)

defines a norm on
H(V)
, and we thus get a continuous Finsler norm
|\cdot|_{\chi}
on the tangent bundle of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
.

Theorem 3.7
.

The metric
\dist_{\chi}
on
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
in Theorem
3.1
coincides with the length metric defined by the Finsler norm
|\cdot|_{\chi}
. In other words, for any two
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
,
\dist_{\chi}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
is the infimum over all smooth paths
(\gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
joining
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
of the corresponding length

\ell_{\chi}(\gamma):=\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\gamma}_{t}|_{\chi,\gamma_{t}}dt.

As a consequence, the distance
d_{\chi}
coincides with the one constructed in
[
Bha03
, §4]
and (for
\chi=\ell^{p}
) in
[
DLR20
]
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For each basis
{\bf e}
of
V
, the Gram–Schmidt projection
\rho_{\bf e}:\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
is a smooth map, and it satisfies
\rho_{\bf e}^{\star}|\cdot|_{\chi}\leq|\cdot|_{\chi}
.

Proof.

The smoothness of
\rho_{\bf e}
follows from its description in terms of the Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization process. Pick
\gamma\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
,
h\in H(V)
. As in the proof of Lemma
3.4
, it will be enough to show that
\lambda:=\lambda_{\rho_{\bf e}(\gamma)}(d_{\gamma}\rho_{\bf e}(h))
is majorized by
\mu:=\lambda_{\gamma}(h)
. Differentiating the identity

\vol(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=\vol(\rho_{\bf e}(\gamma),\rho_{\bf e}(\gamma^{\prime}))

with respect to
\gamma^{\prime}
shows that the trace
\Tr_{\gamma}(h)
satisfies

\Tr_{\gamma}(h)=\Tr_{\rho_{\bf e}(\gamma)}(d_{\gamma}\rho_{\bf e}(h)),

i.e.
\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{N}=\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{N}
. Arguing as in Lemma
3.4
, we apply this fact to the restrictions of
\gamma
and
h
to the span
W
of
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i})
for a given
i
, and get

\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{i}=\Tr_{\gamma|_{W}}(h|_{W})\leq\mu_{1}\dots+\mu_{i}

thanks to the min-max principle.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

Pick a basis
e
, and observe that the differential
d_{\lambda}\iota_{\bf e}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to H(V)
at
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfies for all
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

\lambda_{\iota_{\bf e}(\lambda)}(d_{\lambda}\iota_{\bf e}(\mu))=\mu\mod\mathfrak{S}_{N}.

As a result,
\iota_{\bf e}^{\star}|\cdot|_{\chi}
is the constant Finsler norm
\chi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and the
\chi
-length of any smooth path
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
joining
\gamma=\iota_{\bf e}(\lambda)
to
\gamma^{\prime}=\iota_{\bf e}(\lambda^{\prime})
thus satisfies

\ell_{\chi}(\gamma)\geq\chi(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda)=\dist_{\chi}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}),

with equality when
\gamma
is the image of the line segment
[\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}]
. If
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
is now a smooth path joining
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{diag}}(V)
only, the previous case applies to
\rho_{\bf e}\circ\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{A}_{\bf e}
, which combines with Lemma
3.8
(ii) to give
\ell_{\chi}(\gamma)\geq\ell_{\chi}(\rho_{\bf e}\circ\gamma)\geq\dist_{\chi}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
.
∎

Part II
Models and metrics

4.
Analytification and models

This section reviews some well-known facts on Berkovich analytifications and models, with an emphasis on the reduced fiber condition. We provide in particular a direct proof of a version of the Bosch–Lütkebohmert–Raynaud reduced fiber theorem for models.

As before,
K
denotes a complete valued field. All schemes over
K
(or
K^{\circ}
, when
K
is non-Archimedean) considered below are
separated
, unless otherwise specified.

4.1.
Analytification

To any scheme
X
of finite type over
K
, Berkovich functorially associates in
[
Ber
, §3.4]
an analytification
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, together with a continuous map
\ker:X^{\mathrm{an}}\to X
. In the present paper, we mostly only need the underlying topological space, which is Hausdorff and locally compact. As such, the analytifications of
X
and of the reduced scheme
X_{\red}
coincide, but it will nevertheless be useful for inductive arguments to allow
X
to be non-reduced.

Assume first that
X
is affine, i.e.
X=\Spec A
with
A
a finite type
K
-algebra. The topological space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is defined as the set of all multiplicative seminorms on
A
extending the given absolute value on
K
, endowed with the topology of pointwise convergence. The multiplicative seminorm associated to
x\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
is denoted by
f\mapsto|f(x)|
. The set of
f\in A
with
|f(x)|=0
is a prime ideal, the
kernel
of
x
, thereby defining a natural continuous map
\ker:X^{\mathrm{an}}\to X
. We thus have
|f(x)|=0
if and only if
f
vanishes at
\xi=\ker(x)
, and
f\mapsto|f(x)|
defines a norm on the residue field
\kappa(\xi)
.

Lemma 4.1
.

A function
f\in A
satisfies
|f|\equiv 0
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
if and only if
f
is nilpotent.

Proof.

For each closed point
\xi\in X
, the absolute value on
K
(uniquely) extends to the finite field extension
\kappa(\xi)
of
K
, and image of the kernel map therefore contains the set of closed points of
X
(in fact,
\ker
injects
X^{\mathrm{an}}
onto the set of closed points of
X
if
K
is Archimedean, while
\ker
maps
X^{\mathrm{an}}
onto
X
when
K
is non-Archimedean). As a consequence, a function
f\in A
with
|f|\equiv 0
vanishes at all closed points of
X
, and hence is nilpotent.
∎

Consider now an arbitrary
K
-scheme of finite type
X
, and cover it with finitely many affine open subschemes
U_{i}
. Since
X
is separated, each
U_{ij}=U_{i}\cap U_{j}
is affine, and
U_{ij}^{\mathrm{an}}
is homeomorphic to the inverse image of
U_{ij}
in both
U_{i}^{\mathrm{an}}
and
U_{j}^{\mathrm{an}}
. We can thus glue
U_{i}^{\mathrm{an}}
and
U_{j}^{\mathrm{an}}
together along their common open subset
U_{ij}^{\mathrm{an}}
to define the topological space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
with a continuous map
\ker:X^{\mathrm{an}}\to X
.

The GAGA theorem
[
Ber
, 3.4.8, 3.5.3]
guarantees that
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is Hausdorff (since we always assume
X
separated), locally compact, and
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is compact if and only if
X
is proper.

Example 4.2
.

If
K
is Archimedean, the Gelfand-Mazur theorem yields the following description of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
. When
K=\mathbb{C}
,
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is the usual analytification of
X
,
i.e.
X^{\mathrm{an}}=X(\mathbb{C})
with its Euclidian topology. When
K=\mathbb{R}
,
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is identified with the set of closed points of
X
,
i.e.
the quotient of
(X\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{an}}=X(\mathbb{C})
by complex conjugation.

Example 4.3
.

When
K
is non-Archimedean with valuation
v_{K}=-\log|\cdot|
,
X^{\mathrm{an}}
can be seen as a space of semivaluations on
X
,
i.e.
real valuations on the residue fields of points of
X
. More precisely, the bijective map
x\mapsto(\ker(x),v_{x})
with
v_{x}(f):=-\log|f(x)|
describes
X^{\mathrm{an}}
as the set of pairs
(\xi,v)
where
\xi\in X
is a scheme point and
v:\kappa(\xi)^{\star}\to\mathbb{R}
is a rank
1
valuation on the residue field
\kappa(\xi)
extending
v_{K}
.

4.2.
Smooth functions

In the Archimedean case, every point of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
admits a neighborhood
V
with a closed (analytic) embedding in a polydisc
\mathbb{D}^{r}
, and a smooth function
u:V\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as the restriction of a smooth function on
\mathbb{D}^{r}
, the definition being independent of the choice of closed embedding.

In the non-Archimedean case, Chambert-Loir and Ducros have introduced in
[
CLD12
]
a notion of
(p,q)
-form on any Berkovich analytic space (see also
[
Gub16
]
for the case of an analytification). In particular, a sheaf of
smooth functions
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is defined, basically prescribed by the following two natural requirements:

(i)

\log|f|
is smooth for each invertible analytic function
f
;

(ii)

if
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
are smooth functions on an open
V\subset X^{\mathrm{an}}
and
\chi
is a smooth function defined near the range of the map
V\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
with components
(u_{i})
, then
\chi(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r})
is smooth on
V
.

More explicitly, a function
u
on an open subset of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is smooth iff it is locally of the form

(4.1)

u=\chi\left(\log|f_{1}|,\ldots,\log|f_{r}|\right)

where the
f_{i}
are invertible analytic functions and
\chi
is a smooth function on an appropriate subset of
\mathbb{R}^{r}
. Note that this description of smooth functions also holds in the Archimedean case (using
\Rea z_{i}=\log|e^{z_{i}}|
).

4.3.
Models and reduction

In what follows,
K
is non-Archimedean (possibly trivially valued). Recall that the valuation ring
K^{\circ}
is Noetherian if and only if
K
is discretely or trivially valued. Let
X
be a
K
-scheme of finite type.

Definition 4.4
.

A
model
of
X
is a (separated) flat, finite type
K^{\circ}
-scheme
\mathcal{X}
together with an identification of
K
-schemes
\mathcal{X}_{K}:=\mathcal{X}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K\simeq X
.

The
special fiber
of a model
\mathcal{X}
is the
\widetilde{K}
-scheme of finite type
\mathcal{X}_{s}:=\mathcal{X}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}\widetilde{K}
. We say that a model
\mathcal{X}
is
proper
(resp.
projective
) if it is proper (resp. projective) as a
K^{\circ}
-scheme. This implies of course that
X
is proper (resp. projective) as a
K
-scheme.

Models of
X
form a category, in which a morphism of models
\mu:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
is a morphism of
K^{\circ}
-schemes compatible with the given identifications
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{K}\simeq X\simeq\mathcal{X}_{K}
. If two models
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{X}
admit a morphism
\mu:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
, then
\mu
is unique, by separatedness, and we then say that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}

dominates

\mathcal{X}
. We say that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}

properly dominates

\mathcal{X}
if
\mu
is proper.

In the trivially valued case, models of
X
are in 1–1 correspondence with automorphisms of
X
, and
\mathcal{X}=X
is thus the only model up to isomorphism.

Lemma 4.5
.

Any model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
is automatically finitely presented over
K^{\circ}
.

This follows from a general result of Raynaud–Gruson
[
RG71
, Théorème 3.4.6]
, and goes back to Nagata
[
Nag66
, Theorem 3]
. For the sake of completeness, we reproduce here a simple argument due to Antoine Ducros
[
Duc
]
, which is basically equivalent to that of Nagata.

Proof.

We claim that it is enough to prove the result when
\mathcal{X}
is projective over
K^{\circ}
. Indeed, arguing locally, we may first assume that
\mathcal{X}
is affine, and we get the claim by choosing a closed embedding in an affine space and passing to the schematic closure in the corresponding projective space. Pick a closed embedding
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}_{K^{\circ}}
, and denote by
I
the corresponding homogeneous ideal of
R:=K^{\circ}[t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}]
. Since both
(R/I)\otimes K
and
(R/I)\otimes\widetilde{K}
are Noetherian, we may choose a finitely generated homogeneous ideal
I^{\prime}\subset I
such that
R/I^{\prime}\to R/I
becomes an isomorphism after tensoring with either
K
or
\widetilde{K}
. This means that the finitely presented closed subscheme
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{P}^{N}_{K^{\circ}}
defined by
I^{\prime}
has the same special fiber and generic fiber as
\mathcal{X}
. If we can show that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is flat over
K^{\circ}
, it will coincide with the schematic closure of its generic fiber, which will prove that
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is finitely presented.
But
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is flat over
K^{\circ}
if and only if the finite type
K^{\circ}
-module
\mathcal{V}_{m}:=(R/I^{\prime})_{m}
is free for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, which is indeed the case since
\dim_{K}\mathcal{V}_{m}\otimes K=\dim_{\widetilde{K}}\mathcal{V}_{m}\otimes\widetilde{K}
, by choice of
I^{\prime}
.
∎

A model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
determines a compact subset
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}

4
4

4

The letter
\beth
(’bet’) is the second letter of the Hebrew alphabet. The chosen notation follows the lead of
[
Thu07
]
.
of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
and an anticontinuous
5
5

5

The inverse image of an open is closed.

reduction map

\red_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}^{\beth}\to\mathcal{X}_{s},

as follows. If
\mathcal{X}
is affine, i.e.
\mathcal{X}=\spec(\mathcal{A})
with
\mathcal{A}
a flat (i.e. torsion-free) finite type
K^{\circ}
-algebra, then

\mathcal{X}^{\beth}=\left\{x\in X^{\mathrm{an}}\mid|f(x)|\leq 1\text{ for all }f\in\mathcal{A}\right\},

and the reduction
\red_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
of
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
is the point of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
induced by the prime ideal
\{f\in\mathcal{A}\mid|f(x)|<1\}
. In the general case,
\mathcal{X}
is covered by finitely many affine open subschemes
\mathcal{U}_{i}
, whose generic fibers
U_{i}
give an affine open cover of
X
, and

\mathcal{X}^{\beth}=\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}\subset\bigcup_{i}U_{i}^{\mathrm{an}}=X^{\mathrm{an}}.

In the language of Example
4.3
,
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
consists of those semivaluations on
X
that admit a center on
\mathcal{X}
, and
\red_{\mathcal{X}}
maps a semivaluation to its center, which is necessarily on
\mathcal{X}_{s}
. By the valuative criterion of properness, we thus have:

Lemma 4.6
.

If a model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
properly dominates
\mathcal{X}
, then
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})^{\beth}=\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
. If
\mathcal{X}
is proper over
K^{\circ}
(and hence
X
is proper over
K
), then
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}=X^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Example 4.7
.

In the trivially valued case,
X
is the only model up to isomorphism, and
X^{\beth}
coincides with the construction of
[
Thu07
]
. In particular,
X^{\beth}\subset X^{\mathrm{an}}
, with equality iff
X
is proper.

The compact set
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
associated to a model
\mathcal{X}
can also be understood as (the underlying topological space of) the generic fiber in the sense of
[
Ber94
, §1]
of the formal completion
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
of
\mathcal{X}
. As above, it is enough to consider the case where
X=\spec(A)
and
\mathcal{X}=\spec(\mathcal{A})
are affine. For any two nonzero
a,a^{\prime}\in K^{\circ\circ}
, there exists
n\gg 1
with
a^{n}\in K^{\circ}a^{\prime}
, and the formal completion
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
a
is thus independent of the choice of
a\in K^{\circ\circ}
(set
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}=A
in the trivially valued case). The
K^{\circ}
-algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is flat and topologically of finite type, and
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}=\Spf(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
is thus an admissible formal
K^{\circ}
-scheme, whose generic fiber
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\eta}
is defined as the set of bounded multiplicative seminorms on the
K
-affinoid algebra
\widehat{A}:=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\otimes K
. Composing such a seminorm with the canonical map
A\to\widehat{A}
defines a continuous map
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\eta}\to\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
, which is easily see to be bijective by density of the image of
A
in
\widehat{A}
, and hence a homeomorphism, by compactness of
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\eta}
.

The following well-known result holds in fact for the reduction map of any admissible formal scheme (see for instance
[
GRW16
, §2.13]
).

Lemma 4.8
.

The reduction map
\red_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}^{\beth}\to\mathcal{X}_{s}
of any model
\mathcal{X}
is anticontinuous and surjective. Further, the preimage
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
of the set of generic points of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is a finite set.

We shall call
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
the set of
Shilov points
. When
\mathcal{X}
(and hence
X
) is affine,
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
is exactly the Shilov boundary of the
K
-affinoid domain
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
in the sense of
[
Ber
, 2.4.4]
, cf.
[
GM19
, Proposition A.3]
. Covering a general model with affine open subschemes, we get:

Lemma 4.9
.

For any model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
and
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)
, the sup-seminorm on
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
satisfies

\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}:=\sup_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}|f|=\max_{\Gamma(\mathcal{X})}|f|.

Example 4.10
.

If
K
is trivially valued and
\eta
is a generic point of
X=X_{s}
, the point of
X^{\beth}
corresponding to the trivial valuation on
\kappa(\eta)=\mathcal{O}_{X,\eta}
is the unique Shilov point mapping to
\eta
.

Example 4.11
.

Assume that
K
is nontrivially valued and
\mathcal{X}
is normal,
i.e.
integrally closed in
X
with
X
normal. For each generic point
\eta
of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
, the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X},\eta}
is a rank one valuation ring. This is of course well-known when
\mathcal{X}
is Noetherian (
i.e.
K
trivially or discretely valued), while the general case is proved in
[
Kna00
, Theorem 2.6.1]
. As observed in
[
GS15
, Proposition 2.3]
, one easily checks that the corresponding point of
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
is the unique Shilov point of
\mathcal{X}
mapping to
\eta
.

Example 4.12
.

If we merely assume that
\mathcal{X}
is integrally closed in
X
, it is still true that there is a unique Shilov point mapping to any given generic point
\eta
of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
. In the discretely valued case, this is proved in
[
CLT09
, Lemme 2.1]
. In the densely valued case, the assumption implies that
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is reduced by Theorem
4.19
below, which also implies that
\red_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}^{\beth}\to\mathcal{X}_{s}
coincides with the affinoid reduction map, and we conclude by
[
Ber
, Proposition 2.4.4]
.

4.4.
Sup-seminorm, integral closure and reduced fiber

The goal of this section is to review the relation between sup-seminorm, integral closure and reduced fiber.

Assume first that
K
is non-Archimedean and nontrivially valued,
X=\spec(A)
is affine and
\mathcal{X}=\spec(\mathcal{A})
is an affine model of
X
. Denote as above by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
the formal completion of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to any nonzero
a\in K^{\circ\circ}
, by
\widehat{A}:=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\otimes K
the associated
K
-affinoid algebra, and write
f\mapsto\widehat{f}
for the canonical map
A\to\widehat{A}
(which is not injective in general, cf. Example
4.14
below). The affinoid algebra
\widehat{A}
is equipped with the
sup-seminorm

\|\cdot\|_{\sup}
, defined by setting for
g\in\widehat{A}

\|g\|_{\sup}:=\sup_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}|g|=\max_{\Gamma(\mathcal{X})}|g|,

where the second equality holds by
[
Ber
, 2.4.4]
. The sup-seminorm on
\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
of
f\in A
as in Lemma
4.9
can thus be written as

\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}=\|\widehat{f}\|_{\sup}.

By
[
BGR
, 6.2.1/4]
and
[
Ber
, 3.4.3]
, we have:

Lemma 4.13
.

The sup-seminorm on
\widehat{A}
is a norm if and only if
\widehat{A}
is reduced. This holds in particular if
A
is reduced.

While
A\to\widehat{A}
has dense image, it is not injective in general, even when
A
is reduced:

Example 4.14
.

If
K
is discretely valued,
\mathcal{A}:=K
is of finite type of
K^{\circ}
, and hence a model of
A=K
, for which
\widehat{A}=\{0\}
(thanks to Antoine Ducros for this simple example).

Theorem 4.15
.

The unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\sup}
coincides with the integral closure
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
in
\widehat{A}
. Similarly, the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}
coincides with the integral closure
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}
in
A
, and the induced map
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
further has dense image.

Corollary 4.16
.

A given
f\in A
is integral over
\mathcal{A}
if and only if
\widehat{f}
is integral over
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and
\mathcal{A}
is integrally closed in
A
if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is integrally closed in
\widehat{A}
.

We start with a useful observation.

Lemma 4.17
.

For each
f\in A
, we have
f\in\mathcal{A}\Longleftrightarrow\widehat{f}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

We imitate
[
BL86
, Lemma 1.4]
. Pick a nonzero
a\in K^{\circ\circ}
such that
af\in\mathcal{A}
, and note that the canonical map
\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{A}/a\mathcal{A}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}/a\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. If
\widehat{f}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, we thus have
af=ag
for some
g\in\mathcal{A}
, and hence
f=g\in\mathcal{A}
after multiplying by
a^{-1}
in
A
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.15
.

That the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\sup}
is the integral closure of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is a reformulation of
[
BGR
, 6.3.4/1]
(and the remark that follows). Before dealing with the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}
, we first establish the density of the image of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
.

Let thus
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, so that
g^{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}b_{i}g^{n-i}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
for some
b_{i}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. Since
\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and
A\to\widehat{A}
have dense images, we can pick sequences
f_{j}\in A
and
a_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}
with
\widehat{f}_{j}\to g
and
\widehat{a}_{ij}\to b_{i}
as
j\to\infty
. As
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is open in
\widehat{A}
(for instance by Lemma
4.18
below), it follows that
\widehat{f}_{j}^{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\widehat{a}_{ij}\widehat{f}_{j}^{n-i}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
for all
j\gg 1
, i.e.
f_{j}^{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{ij}f_{j}^{n-i}\in\mathcal{A}
, by Lemma
4.17
. As a result,
f_{j}
is integral over
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
f_{j}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, which proves that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
has dense image.

It remains to show that an element
f\in A
with
\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}\leq 1
is integral over
\mathcal{A}
. Since
\|\widehat{f}\|_{\sup}\leq 1
, we already know that
\widehat{f}
belongs to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
has dense image and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is open in
\widehat{A}
, we find
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with
\widehat{f}-\widehat{f}^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.
f-f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, and hence
f\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(see also
[
CMor18
, Theorem 2.10]
) for a direct proof).
∎

Besides the sup-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}
,
A
is also equipped with a ’lattice seminorm’
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
, defined by

\|f\|_{\mathcal{A}}:=\inf\left\{|a|\mid a\in K,\,f\in a\mathcal{A}\right\}.

By Example
4.14
, this is again not a norm in general. However, similarly setting for
g\in\widehat{A}

\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}:=\inf\left\{|a|\mid a\in K,\,g\in a\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\right\},

does yield a norm on
\widehat{A}
:

Lemma 4.18
.

For each
g\in\widehat{A}
, the infimum defining
\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
is achieved. In particular,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is the closed unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
, and
K^{\circ\circ}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is its open unit ball.

Proof.

The result is true for the polynomial ring
\mathcal{B}:=K^{\circ}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]
, since
\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}
is then the Gauss norm on the Tate algebra
\widehat{B}=K\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\}
. In the general case, choose a surjection
\mathcal{B}:=K^{\circ}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}
for some
r\geq 1
, and observe that
\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
is the quotient seminorm of
\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}
with respect to the induced surjection
\rho:\widehat{B}\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{A}
. The kernel of
\rho
, being an ideal in a Tate algebra, is strictly closed
[
BGR
, 5.2.7/8]
. By definition, this means that for each
g\in\widehat{A}
, there exists
h\in\widehat{B}
such that
\rho(h)=g
and
\|h\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}=\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
. Since the desired result holds for
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
, we can then find
a\in K
with
|a|=\|h\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}=\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
, which implies that
h\in a\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
, and hence
g=\rho(h)\in a\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
∎

Theorem 4.19
.

As above, let
\mathcal{X}=\Spec\mathcal{A}
be an affine model of
X=\Spec A
. We then have
\|\cdot\|_{\sup}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
on
\widehat{A}
, and hence
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
on
A
. Consider further the following properties:

(i)

\mathcal{X}_{s}
is reduced;

(ii)

\|\cdot\|_{\sup}=\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
on
\widehat{A}
;

(iii)

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
on
A
;

(iv)

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is integrally closed in
\widehat{A}
;

(v)

\mathcal{A}
is integrally closed in
A
.

Then
(i)\Longleftrightarrow(ii)\Longleftrightarrow(iii)\Longrightarrow(iv)\Longleftrightarrow(v)
. If
K
is densely valued, we also have
(v)\Longrightarrow(i)
, and
(i)
—
(v)
are then equivalent.

Proof.

Pick a nonzero
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. By Lemma
4.18
,
\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
is in the value group
|K^{\times}|
, and we may thus assume that
\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}=1
after multiplying
g
by a nonzero scalar. We then have
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, hence
|g(x)|\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
, which proves that
\|g\|_{\sup}\leq 1
, and hence
\|\cdot\|_{\sup}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is reduced, and assume by contradiction that
g
as above satisfies
\|g\|_{\sup}<1
. By
[
BGR
, 6.2.3/2]
,
g
is topologically nilpotent, i.e.
g^{n}\to 0
. For
n\gg 1
, we thus have
\|g^{n}\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}<1
, i.e.
g^{n}\in K^{\circ\circ}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
; since
\mathcal{A}\otimes\widetilde{K}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\widetilde{K}
is reduced, this implies
g\in K^{\circ\circ}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, which contradicts
\|g\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}=1
. We have thus proved (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii), which trivially implies (iii) by composing with
A\to\widehat{A}
. If (iii) holds, then
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is power-multiplicative, i.e.
\|f^{n}\|_{\mathcal{A}}=\|f\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}
for each
f\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
f^{n}\in K^{\circ\circ}\mathcal{A}\Longleftrightarrow\|f^{n}\|_{\mathcal{A}}<1\Longleftrightarrow f\in K^{\circ\circ}\mathcal{A}
, which means that
\mathcal{A}\otimes\widetilde{K}\simeq\mathcal{A}/K^{\circ\circ}\mathcal{A}
is reduced.

Since
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}
) is the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
(resp.
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}})
, Theorem
4.15
shows that (ii) and (iii) respectively imply (iv) and (v), while Corollary
4.16
shows that (iv) and (v) are equivalent.

Assume finally that
K
is densely valued and that (v) holds. To prove (i), we need to show that each
f\in\mathcal{A}
such that
f^{n}\in a\mathcal{A}
for some
n\geq 1
and
a\in K^{\circ\circ}
actually satisfies
f\in K^{\circ\circ}\mathcal{A}
. Since
K
is densely valued, we can find
a^{\prime}\in K^{\circ\circ}
with
|a|^{1/n}\leq|a^{\prime}|<1
, and hence
a/a^{\prime n}\in K^{\circ}
. As a result,
g:=a^{\prime-1}f\in A
satisfies
g^{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, and hence
g\in\mathcal{A}
, since
\mathcal{A}
is integrally closed in
A
. We have thus shown as desired that
f=a^{\prime}g\in K^{\circ\circ}\mathcal{A}
(we are grateful to Walter Gubler for his help with this argument).
∎

We conclude this section with the following rather special case of the scheme-theoretic version of the Bosch–Lütkebohmert–Raynaud reduced fiber theorem
[
BLR95
, Theorem 2.1’]
. We provide some details for the convenience of the reader (see also
[
Ana73
, Théorème 1’, p.73]
).

Theorem 4.20
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean and nontrivially valued, and either discretely valued or algebraically closed. Let
X
be a reduced
K
-scheme of finite type, and pick a model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. The integral closure
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}
in
X
is then finite over
\mathcal{X}
, and hence a model of
X
as well. In the algebraically closed case,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{s}
is further reduced.

Note conversely that the existence of a model with reduced special fiber implies that
X
is reduced, by
[
EGA
, IV,12.1.1]
.

Proof of Theorem
4.20
.

If
K
is discretely valued, then
\mathcal{X}
is excellent, which implies that its integral closure in
X
is finite. Assume that
K
is algebraically closed. It is then densely valued, and the final point will thus follow from Theorem
4.19
.

The finiteness of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{X}
being local, we assume that
\mathcal{X}=\spec(\mathcal{A})
is affine and use the above notation. We will reduce the result to the Grauert–Remmert finiteness theorem, basically arguing as in
[
BL86
, Proposition 1.5]
and
[
Tem10
, Theorem 3.5.5, Step 3]
.

Since
A
is reduced,
\widehat{A}
is reduced as well by Lemma
4.13
, and
[
BGR
, 6.4.1/5]
thus shows that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is finite over
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}g_{i}
for a finite set
g_{i}\in\widehat{A}
, in which we include
1
for convenience. As in the proof of Lemma
4.17
, we can find for each
i
some
f_{i}\in A
with
g_{i}-\widehat{f}_{i}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and hence
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{f}_{i}
. By Corollary
4.16
, an element
f\in A
belongs to the integral closure
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}
in
A
if and only
\widehat{f}
belongs to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{f}_{i}
, which is also equivalent to
f\in\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}f_{i}
by Lemma
4.17
. We conclude as desired that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}f_{i}
is finite over
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

Theorem
4.20
fails in general over an arbitrary densely valued non-Archimedean field
K
. Let indeed
X=\spec A
be an affine reduced
K
-scheme,
\mathcal{X}=\spec\mathcal{A}
an affine model, and denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
the integral closure of
\mathcal{A}
in
A
. If
K
is densely valued and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is finite over
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:=\spec\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a model of
X
, and hence
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{s}
is reduced, by Theorem
4.19
. As a result, the sup-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\beth}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime\beth}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
takes values in
|K|
, a condition that is not satisfied in general when the group
|K^{\times}|
is not divisible.

5.
Fubini–Study metrics and model metrics

This section introduces Fubini–Study metrics, and compares them with model metrics. The main result is Theorem
6.4
, which compares the supnorms and lattice norms induced by a model metric, and relies on the reduced fiber theorem.

In what follows,
X
denotes a
projective

K
-scheme, where
K
is a complete valued field.

5.1.
Metrics

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. A
continuous metric

\phi
on
L
is a family of norms

|\cdot|_{\phi_{x}}:L_{x}:=L\otimes\mathcal{H}(x)\to[0,+\infty),\,x\in X^{\mathrm{an}},

such that for any local section
s
of
L
on an open
U\subset X
, the induced function
|s|_{\phi}
on
U^{\mathrm{an}}
is continuous. We use additive notation for metrics, which amounts to the following rules:

(i)

if
\phi,\psi
are continuous metrics on line bundles
L,M
, then
\phi\pm\psi
denotes the induced metric on
L\pm M=L\otimes M^{\pm 1}
, i.e.

|\cdot|_{\phi\pm\psi}=|\cdot|_{\phi}\otimes|\cdot|_{\psi}^{\pm 1};

(ii)

a continuous metric
\phi
on the trivial line bundle
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
is identified with the continuous function
-\log|1|_{\phi}
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
.

If
\phi,\psi
are two continuous metrics on the same line bundle
L
,
\phi-\psi
is thus a continuous function on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, which means that the space
\cz(L)
of continuous metrics on
L
is an affine space modeled on
\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
.

A
smooth metric

\phi
on
L
is a continuous metric such that
|s|_{\phi}
is smooth for any local trivialization
s
of
L
. In the non-Archimedean case, smoothness is understood in the sense of
[
CLD12
]
, see §
4.2
. The set of smooth metrics
C^{\infty}(L)\subset\cz(L)
is an affine space modeled on
C^{\infty}(X)
.

We occasionally use the notion of a
singular metric
on
L
, by which we mean a metric of the form
\phi=\psi+f
with
\psi\in\cz(L)
and
f:X^{\mathrm{an}}\to[-\infty,+\infty)
an arbitrary function. For a local trivialization
s
of
L
,
|s|_{\phi}=|s|_{\psi}e^{-f}
is thus allowed to be
+\infty
at certain points.

If
f
is bounded, we say that
\phi
is a
bounded metric
, defining an affine space
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(L)\supset\cz(L)
modeled on the space of bounded functions.

Example 5.1
.

Every section
s\in\Hnot(X,L)
defines a singular metric
\log|s|
on
L
, such that
\log|s|=\psi+\log|s|_{\psi}
for any
\psi\in\cz(L)
.

For each
m\geq 1
, every metric on
L
is of the form
m\phi
with
\phi
a metric on
L
. As a result, all of the above notions immediately extend to
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles.

5.2.
Fubini–Study metrics

The usual Fubini–Study metric on the tautological ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
over the projective space (also known as the Weil metric in the non-Archimedean context) generalizes in a natural way as follows (see Corollary
7.18
below for a more conceptual characterization).

Definition 5.2
.

A metric
\phi
on a line bundle
L
over
X
is called a
Fubini–Study metric
if there exists
m\geq 1
, a finite set of sections
(s_{i})
of
mL
without common zeroes, and constants
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(A)

\phi=\frac{1}{2m}\log\sum_{i}|s_{i}|^{2}e^{2\lambda_{i}}
(Archimedean case);

(NA)

\phi=\frac{1}{m}\max_{i}\{\log|s_{i}|+\lambda_{i}\}
(non-Archimedean case).

We say that
\phi
is
pure
if
\lambda_{i}=0
.

By definition,
L
admits a (pure) Fubini–Study metric iff
L
is
semiample
, i.e.
mL
is globally generated for some
m\geq 1
.

The notation
|s_{i}|=e^{\log|s_{i}|}
is understood as in Example
5.1
, and thus means that for any local trivializing section
\tau
of
L
, the corresponding functions
f_{i}:=s_{i}/\tau^{m}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}
satisfy

-\log|\tau|_{\phi}=\frac{1}{2m}\log\sum_{i}|f_{i}|^{2}e^{-2\lambda_{i}}\,\,\,\,\,\,(\text{resp. }\frac{1}{m}\max_{i}\{\log|f_{i}|+\lambda_{i}\}).

In the Archimedean case, one can replace
s_{i}
with
e^{\lambda_{i}}s_{i}
, and all Fubini–Study metrics are thus pure in that case.

Given a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}
, we say that a metric
\phi
as above is a
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metric
if
\lambda_{i}\in\Gamma
for all
i
. We denote by

\FS_{\Gamma}(L)\subset\cz(L)

the set of
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metrics on
L
, and by
\FS(L)=\FS_{\mathbb{R}}(L)
the set of all Fubini–Study metrics. The set of pure Fubini–Study metrics is thus
\FS_{\{0\}}(L)
.

Remark 5.3
.

While Fubini–Study metrics are smooth in the Archimedean case, they are instead piecewise linear when
K
is non-Archimedean (see §
5.4
below). But it is anyway easy to explicitly approximate a Fubini–Study metric by smooth metrics, cf. Theorem
7.14
.

The next result summarized the main properties of Fubini–Study metrics.

Proposition 5.4
.

If
L,L^{\prime}
are line bundles on
X
and
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}
is a subgroup, then:

(i)

\FS_{\Gamma}(L)+\FS_{\Gamma}(L^{\prime})\subset\FS_{\Gamma}(L+L^{\prime})
;

(ii)

\FS_{\Gamma}(L)=\FS_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(L)
with
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\mathbb{Q}(\Gamma+\Gamma_{K})
the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}
spanned by
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{K}
;

(iii)

\FS_{\Gamma}(mL)=m\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
;

(iv)

f^{\star}\FS_{\Gamma}(L)\subset\FS_{\Gamma}(f^{\star}L)
for any projective morphism
f:X^{\prime}\to X
;

(v)

if
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
, or
K
is nontrivially valued, then
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
is dense in
\FS(L)
with respect to uniform convergence.

When
K
is non-Archimedean, we further have:

(vi)

\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
is stable under max.

Proof.

Pick
m,m^{\prime}\geq 1
and finitely many sections without common
s_{i}\in\Hnot(mL)
,
s^{\prime}_{j}\in\Hnot(m^{\prime}L^{\prime})
such that
\phi=\frac{1}{2m}\log\sum_{i}|s_{i}|^{2}
,
\phi^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2m^{\prime}}\log\sum_{j}|s^{\prime}_{j}|^{2}
in the Archimedean case, and

\phi=\frac{1}{m}\max_{i}\{\log|s_{i}|+\lambda_{i}\}
,
\phi^{\prime}=\frac{1}{m}\max_{j}\{\log|s^{\prime}_{j}|+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\}
in the non-Archimedean case. In the former case,

mm^{\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime})=\log\left(\sum_{i}|s_{i}|^{2}\right)^{m^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{j}|s^{\prime}_{j}|^{2}\right)^{m},

which expands out as
mm^{\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime})=\log\sum_{\alpha}|\sigma_{\alpha}|^{2}
for a finite set of sections
\sigma_{a}\in\Hnot(mm^{\prime}L)
without common zeroes, proving that
\phi+\phi^{\prime}
is Fubini–Study. In the non-Archimedean case,

mm^{\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime})=\max_{i}\{\log|s_{i}^{m^{\prime}}|+m^{\prime}\lambda_{i}\}+\max_{j}\{\log|s^{\prime m}_{j}|+m\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\}

=\max_{i,j}\{\log|s_{i}^{m^{\prime}}s^{\prime m}_{j}|+m^{\prime}\lambda_{i}+m\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\},

which proves (i). The proof of (ii) and (iii) is similar, and (iv), (vi) follow directly from the definitions. To prove (v), note that the assumption implies that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}(\Gamma+\Gamma_{K})
is a nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}
. It is thus dense in
\mathbb{R}
, and it is then straightforward to check that
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)=\FS_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(L)
is dense in
\FS(L)=\FS_{\mathbb{R}}(L)
, simply by approximating the coefficients
\lambda_{i}
in Definition
5.2
.
∎

By Proposition
5.4
(ii), we can make sense of
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metrics on any
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
over
X
. As noted above, such metrics exist iff
L
is semiample. As we now show, Fubini–Study metrics always descend to an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
L
is a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
, then there exists a surjective morphism
f:X\to Y
to a projective
K
-scheme with
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
and an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
A
on
Y
such that
f^{\star}A=L
. Further,
f
and
(Y,A)
are unique up to isomorphism.

We call
A
the
Stein factorization
of
L
.

Proof.

After passing to a multiple, we may assume that
L
is a globally generated line bundle. We then have a morphism
h:X\to\mathbb{P}\Hnot(L)
such that
L=h^{\star}\mathcal{O}(1)
. By Stein factorization, we have
h=g\circ f
with
f:X\to Y
such that
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
and
g:Y\to\mathbb{P}\Hnot(L)
finite. Thus
A:=g^{\star}\mathcal{O}(1)
is ample, and
L=f^{\star}A
. By the projection formula, we have

\Hnot(Y,mA)\simeq\Hnot(X,mL)
for all
m
. This shows that
(Y,A)
is recovered from the graded ring
R(X,L)=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\Hnot(X,mL)
by the Proj construction, and hence is unique up to isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
L
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
. Denote by
X_{\mathrm{red}}\subset X
the reduction of
X
, and by
L_{\mathrm{red}}
the restriction of
L
. For all
m
sufficiently divisible, the restriction map
\Hnot(X,mL)\to\Hnot(X_{\mathrm{red}},mL_{\mathrm{red}})
is surjective.

Proof.

Use the notation of Lemma
5.5
. Since every germ on
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{\mathrm{red}}}
locally lifts to
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, we have
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X_{\mathrm{red}}}=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{\mathrm{red}}}
, hence
\Hnot(X_{\mathrm{red}},mL_{\mathrm{red}})\simeq\Hnot(Y_{\mathrm{red}},mA_{\mathrm{red}})
, by the projection formula. We are thus reduced to the case where
L
is ample, which follows from Serre vanishing.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
L
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
. Denote by
A
the Stein factorization of
L
as in Lemma
5.5
, and by
L_{\mathrm{red}}
the restriction of
L
to
X_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then

\FS_{\Gamma}(A)\simeq\FS_{\Gamma}(L)\simeq\FS_{\Gamma}(L_{\mathrm{red}}).

5.3.
Model metrics

In this section,
K
is non-Archimedean (possibly trivially valued). A
model
of a line bundle
L
on the projective
K
-scheme
X
is a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on a
projective
model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, together with an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathcal{X}_{K}}\simeq L
compatible with the given isomorphism
\mathcal{X}_{K}\simeq X
. When
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, each model of
L
is of the form
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}(D)
where
D
is a
vertical
Cartier divisor on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
, i.e.
\supp D\subset\mathcal{X}_{s}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
, and
\mathcal{X}
a projective model of
X
. Then we can find a projective model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
dominating
\mathcal{X}
and a model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
determined on
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Pick very ample line bundles
A_{1},A_{2}
on
X
such that
L=A_{1}-A_{2}
. Sections of
A_{i}
yields an embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N_{i}}_{K}
such that
A_{i}=\mathcal{O}(1)|_{X}
. The scheme theoretic closure of
X
in
\mathbb{P}^{N_{i}}_{K^{\circ}}
is thus a projective model
\mathcal{X}_{i}
of
X
, and
\mathcal{A}_{i}:=\mathcal{O}(1)|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}
is a model of
A_{i}
determined on
\mathcal{X}_{i}
. It remains to choose a projective model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
dominating
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
, and to define
\mathcal{L}
as the difference of the pullbacks to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
∎

A model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
defines a continuous metric
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
on
L
, as follows. Cover
\mathcal{X}
with finitely many open subschemes
\mathcal{U}_{i}
with a trivializing section
\tau_{i}
of
\mathcal{L}
. Since
\mathcal{X}
is in particular proper over
K^{\circ}
,
X^{\mathrm{an}}=\mathcal{X}^{\beth}
is covered by the compact sets
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}
. We may thus define a continuous metric
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
on
L^{\mathrm{an}}
by requiring that
|\tau_{i}|_{\phi_{\mathcal{L}}}=1
on
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}
. This is indeed well-defined, since any other trivializing section of
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is of the form
u_{i}\tau_{i}
with
u_{i}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
a unit, and hence
|u_{i}|\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}
.

Example 5.9
.

If
K
is trivially valued, the model metric defined by the unique model
(X,L)
is called the
trivial metric
of
L
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a model of
L
, with corresponding metric
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
.

(i)

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
\phi_{m\mathcal{L}}=m\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
.

(ii)

If a model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
dominates
\mathcal{X}
, then the pull-back
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
satisfies
\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

If
\tau
is trivializing section of
\mathcal{L}
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
, then
\tau^{m}
is a trivializing section of
m\mathcal{L}
, and the pull-back of
\tau
is a local trivialization of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
on the inverse image
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, which satisfies
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\beth}=\mathcal{U}^{\beth}
since
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is proper over
\mathcal{U}
(cf. Lemma
4.6
).
∎

If
L
is now a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
, a
\mathbb{Q}
-model
of
L
is defined as a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
such that
m\mathcal{L}
is a model of
mL
for some
m\geq 1
. By Lemma
5.10
(i), the metric
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}:=m^{-1}\phi_{m\mathcal{L}}
on
L
is independent of the choice of
m
.

Definition 5.11
.

A
model metric
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is a metric of the form
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-model of
L
. A
model function
is a model metric
\phi
on
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, identified with the continuous function
-\log|1|_{\phi}
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
.

A model function
f
is thus determined by a vertical
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor
D
on some projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. Every line bundle
L
on
X
admits a model metric
\phi
, and any other model metric on
L
is then of the form
\phi+f
with
f
a model function.

Theorem 5.12
.

Assume that
K
is either discretely (or trivially) valued, or algebraically closed. Let
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
be two models of a the same line bundle
L
, determined on models
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
X
. Then the model metrics
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
on
L
coincide iff the pullbacks of
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
to some model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}
dominating both
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}
’ are equal.

Proof.

If the pullbacks of
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
to some high enough model agree, then
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
, by Lemma
5.10
. Assume conversely that
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
. By Theorem
4.20
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
are dominated by a model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}
which is integrally closed in
X
. After pulling back
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}
, we may thus assume that
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are determined on
\mathcal{X}
integrally closed in
X
, and we then claim that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. To see thus, let
\mathcal{U}=\spec(\mathcal{A})
be an affine open subscheme of
\mathcal{X}
with trivializing sections
\tau\in\Hnot(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{L})
,
\tau^{\prime}\in\Hnot(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
. Since
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are both models of the same line bundle
L
, the restrictions of
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
to the generic fiber
U=\spec(\mathcal{A}_{K})
of
\mathcal{U}
satisfy
\tau^{\prime}|_{U}=u\tau|_{U}
with
u\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
a unit. As
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
coincide, the definition of model metrics yields
|u|\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{U}^{\beth}
. By Theorem
4.19
,
\mathcal{A}
coincides with the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{\sup}
on
\mathcal{A}_{K}
. As
\|u\|_{\sup}=\|u^{-1}\|_{\sup}=1
, it follows that
u
and
u^{-1}
belong to
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
u
is a unit in
\mathcal{A}
, and we conclude that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
.
∎

The next result describes the behavior of model metrics under pullback.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
L
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
, and
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
be the model metric determined by a
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
on a model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. For any projective morphism
f:Y\to X
, the induced metric
f^{\star}\phi
on
f^{\star}L
is also a model metric. More precisely, the family of projective models
\mathcal{Y}
of
Y
such that
f
extends to a
K^{\circ}
-morphism
f:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
is cofinal in all projective models, and we have
f^{\star}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\phi_{f^{\star}\mathcal{L}}
for any such model
\mathcal{Y}
.

If
f\colon Y\to X
is flat, then
\mathcal{Y}
can further be assumed to be flat over
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Pick a projective model
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
of
Y
, and denote by
\mathcal{Y}
the schematic (or flat) closure in
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\times_{K^{\circ}}\mathcal{X}
of the graph of
f
. Then
\mathcal{Y}
dominates
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
, and the projection
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
extends
f
. To see the last point, we may assume that
\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle. Let
(\mathcal{U}_{i})
be a finite open cover of
\mathcal{Y}
with trivializing sections
\tau_{i}\in\Hnot(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{L})
. Then
(f^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{i}))
is an open cover of
\mathcal{Y}
with trivializing sections
f^{\star}\tau_{i}
for
f^{\star}\mathcal{L}
, and the result easily follows.

For the last point, by the Raynaud–Gruson flattening theorem
[
RG71
]
, we can blow up
\mathcal{X}
and take the proper transform of
\mathcal{Y}
to obtain a flat morphism
f:\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of models. By Lemma
5.10
this does not change the metrics induced by the models.
∎

We are now in a position to compare model metrics and Fubini–Study metrics (see
[
CMor18
, Proposition 3.8]
for a related result).

Theorem 5.14
.

For a continuous metric
\phi
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
over
X
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\phi
is a pure Fubini–Study metric;

(ii)

\phi
is a model metric determined by a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
,
i.e.
m\mathcal{L}
is a globally generated line bundle when
m\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently divisible.

Proof.

The key observation is that the Weil metric
\log\max_{i}|z_{i}|
on
\mathbb{P}^{r}_{K}
with homogeneous coordinates
[z_{0}:\dots:z_{r}]
coincides with the model metric
\phi_{\mathcal{O}(1)}
determined by the canonical model of
(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathcal{O}(1))
over
K^{\circ}
.

Let first
\phi
be a pure Fubini–Study metric on
L
. After replacing
L
by a multiple, we may assume that
L
is globally generated and
\phi=\log\max_{i}|s_{i}|
with
(s_{i})
a basis of
\Hnot(L)
. These sections induce a morphism of
K
-schemes
f:X\to\mathbb{P}^{r}_{K}
with an identification
L=f^{\star}\mathcal{O}(1)
such that
\phi=f^{\star}\phi_{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. By Proposition
5.13
,
X
admits a projective model
\mathcal{X}
such that
f
extends to a morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{r}_{K^{\circ}}
, and
\phi
is the model metric defined by
(\mathcal{X},f^{\star}\mathcal{O}(1))
. Since
f^{\star}\mathcal{O}(1)
is globally generated, this proves (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). Conversely, let
\mathcal{L}
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-model of
L
. After passing to a multiple, we may assume that
\mathcal{L}
is a globally generated line bundle. Choosing a
K^{\circ}
-basis
(s_{i})
of the lattice
\Hnot(\mathcal{L})
yields a morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{r}_{K^{\circ}}
with
f^{\star}\mathcal{O}(1)=\mathcal{L}
, and hence
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=f^{\star}\phi_{\mathcal{O}(1)}=\log\max_{i}|s_{i}|
, by Proposition
5.13
.
∎

5.4.
PL metrics

In this section,
K
is non-Archimedean, and
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}
is an additive subgroup.

Definition 5.15
.

We say that a continuous metric
\phi
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is
\Gamma
-piecewise linear
, or
\Gamma
-PL
for short, if there exists (semiample)
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles
M,M^{\prime}
and
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metrics
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
on
M,M^{\prime}
such that
L=M-M^{\prime}
and
\phi=\psi-\psi^{\prime}
.

We denote by
\PL_{\Gamma}(L)
the set of
\Gamma
-PL metrics on
L
. A
\Gamma
-PL metric on
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is called a
\Gamma
-PL function, defining a subset
\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})\subset\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
.

When
\Gamma=\{0\}
, we speak of
pure PL metrics
and
pure PL functions
, respectively.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
L,L^{\prime}
be
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles on
X
. Then:

(i)

\PL_{\Gamma}(L)+\PL_{\Gamma}(L^{\prime})\subset\PL_{\Gamma}(L+L^{\prime})
;

(ii)

\PL_{\Gamma}(L)
is stable under max and min;

(iii)

\PL_{\Gamma}(mL)=m\PL_{\Gamma}(L)
for all nonzero
m\in\mathbb{Z}
;

(iv)

\PL_{\Gamma}(L)=\PL_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(L)
with
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}(\Gamma+\Gamma_{K})
;

(v)

\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspace of
\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
, and
\PL_{\Gamma}(L)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-affine space modeled on
\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
;

(vi)

pure PL metrics on
L
coincide with model metrics.

We refer to
[
GM19
, Theorem 1.1]
for an alternative description of (pure) PL metrics.

Proof.

(i)—(v) are direct consequences of Proposition
5.4
.

To prove (vi), pick a
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
, determined on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. Pick an ample line bundle
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{X}
, and denote by
A
its restriction to
X
. For
a\gg 1
,
\mathcal{L}+a\mathcal{A}
is then ample on
\mathcal{X}
. By Theorem
5.14
,
\phi_{\mathcal{L}+a\mathcal{A}}
and
\phi_{a\mathcal{A}}
are pure Fubini–Study metrics, and
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}+a\mathcal{A}}-\phi_{a\mathcal{A}}
is thus a pure PL metric.

Conversely, Theorem
5.14
implies that any pure Fubini–Study metric is a model metric. Every pure PL metric is thus also a model metric, which concludes the proof of (vi).
∎

For later use, we also note:

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
L
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle, and
\phi
be a
\Gamma
-PL metric on
L
. Then we can find
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metrics
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
on ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles
A,A^{\prime}
such that
L=A-A^{\prime}
and
\phi=\psi-\psi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By definition, we can find
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metrics
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles
M,M^{\prime}
such that
L=M-M^{\prime}
and
\phi=\psi-\psi^{\prime}
. Pick an ample line bundle
H
, and
a\gg 1
such that
aH-M
and
aH-L
are both ample, and pick
\tau\in\FS_{\Gamma}(aH-M)
. By Proposition
5.4
,
\psi+\tau
, and
\psi^{\prime}+\tau
are
\Gamma
-Fubini–Study metrics on the ample line bundles
A:=aH
,
A^{\prime}:=aH-L
, and
\phi=(\psi+\tau)-(\psi^{\prime}+\tau)
, which proves the result.
∎

The following result generalizes the well-known density of model functions
[
GM19
]
in the nontrivially valued case.

Theorem 5.18
.

If
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
, or if
K
is nontrivially valued, then
\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
is dense in
\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
.

Proof.

The assumption guarantees that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}(\Gamma+\Gamma_{K})
is nontrivial; replacing
\Gamma
with
\Gamma^{\prime}
, we may thus assume in all cases that
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
, by Proposition
5.16
(iv). By Proposition
5.16
,
\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspace of
\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
, stable under max. By the ’lattice version’ of the Stone–Weierstrass theorem, it will thus be enough to show that
\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
separates the points of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
.

To see this, fix
\gamma\in\Gamma\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
\rho\in\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
, and pick
x_{1}\neq x_{2}\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
and a very ample line bundle
L
. After replacing
L
with a multiple, we can find nonzero sections
s,s^{\prime}\in\Hnot(L)
such that
s^{\prime}
does not vanish at
x_{1},x_{2}
and
|s/s^{\prime}|(x_{1})\neq|s/s^{\prime}|(x_{2})
. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
set

\phi_{m}=\max\{\log|s|,\rho-m\gamma\},\quad\phi^{\prime}_{m}:=\max\{\log|s^{\prime}|,\rho-m\gamma\},\quad f_{m}:=\phi_{m}-\phi^{\prime}_{m}.

Then
\phi_{m},\phi^{\prime}_{m}\in\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
, and hence
f_{m}\in\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
. Since
\log|s^{\prime}|
is finite at
x_{1},x_{2}
,
\phi^{\prime}_{m}=\log|s^{\prime}|
at
x_{1},x_{2}
for all
m
large enough. Thus

f_{m}(x_{i})=\max\{\log|s/s^{\prime}|(x_{i}),\rho(x_{i})-m\gamma\}\to\log|s/s^{\prime}|(x_{i})

as
m\to\infty
, and
|s/s^{\prime}|(x_{1})\neq|s/s^{\prime}|(x_{2})
thus yields
f_{m}(x_{1})\neq f_{m}(x_{2})
for
m\gg 1
. This proves as desired that
\PL_{\Gamma}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
separates the points of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
.
∎

6.
The supnorm of a metric

In this section,
K
is an arbitrary complete valued field,
X
is a projective
K
-scheme, and
L
is a line bundle on
X
.

6.1.
Supnorms vs. lattice norms

The data of a bounded metric
\phi
on
L
defines a sup-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
on
\Hnot(L)
by setting

\|s\|_{\phi}:=\sup_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}|s|_{\phi}

for each
s\in\Hnot(L)
. If
X
is reduced, then
\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
is a norm, by Lemma
4.1
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
F/K
be a complete field extension, and assume that both
X
and
X_{F}
are reduced. Let
\phi
be a bounded metric on
L
, and denote by
\phi_{F}
the pullback of
\phi
to the base change
L_{F}
. Then
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}=\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
on
\Hnot(L)\hookrightarrow\Hnot(L_{F})=\Hnot(L)_{F}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}\leq(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}
on
\Hnot(L_{F})
.

If
K
is Archimedean, we further have

\tfrac{1}{2}(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}\leq(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}.

Proof.

Denoting by
p:X_{F}^{\mathrm{an}}\to X^{\mathrm{an}}
the surjective projection map, we plainly have for
s\in\Hnot(L)

\|s\|_{\phi_{F}}=\sup_{X_{F}^{\mathrm{an}}}|s|_{\phi}\circ p=\sup_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}|s|_{\phi}=\|s\|_{\phi}.

By Proposition
1.25
,
(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}
is the largest norm on
\Hnot(L)_{F}
that coincides with
\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
on
\Hnot(L)
, thus
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}\leq(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}
. If
K
is Archimedean, the only nontrivial case is
K=\mathbb{R}
,
F=\mathbb{C}
. The metric
\phi_{\mathbb{C}}
is conjugation invariant, thus
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{\mathbb{C}}}
is conjugation invariant as well, and hence
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{\mathbb{C}}}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{\mathbb{C}}
, by Proposition
1.25
again.
∎

The inequality
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}\leq(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}
of Lemma
6.1
is strict in general. One has indeed:

Lemma 6.2
.

Assume that
K
is discretely (nontrivially) valued, and let
F/K
be a finite Galois extension. The following are equivalent:

(i)

F/K
is tamely ramified,
i.e.
the residue field extension
\widetilde{F}/\widetilde{K}
is separable and the ramification order
\left[|F^{\times}|:|K^{\times}|\right]
is prime to the residue characteristic;

(ii)

for each bounded metric
\phi
on a line bundle
L
over a reduced projective
K
-scheme
X
such that
X_{F}
is reduced, we have
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}=(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}
.

Proof.

In the situation of (ii),
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}
is a Galois invariant norm on
\Hnot(L)_{F}
. If
F/K
is tamely ramified, the descent result of
[
Rou77
, Proposition 5.1.1]
(see also
[
Pra01
]
) implies that every Galois invariant norm is obtained by ground field extension, and hence (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii).

Conversely, for
X:=\spec F
,
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\phi=0
, the supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}
is the spectral norm on
F\otimes_{K}F
, and
[
RTW15
, §5.1]
thus says that
\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{F}}=(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})_{F}
if and only if
F
is tamely ramified.
∎

In the remainder on this section, we assume that
K
is non-Archimedean.

Lemma 6.3
.

\mathcal{L}
be a model of
L
determined on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, with associated set of Shilov points
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})\subset X^{\mathrm{an}}
. Denote by
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
the induced model metric on
L
, and recall that
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
denotes the norm on
\Hnot(L)
determined by the lattice
\Hnot(\mathcal{L})
. Then:

(i)

for each
s\in\Hnot(L)
, we have
\|s\|_{\phi}=\max_{\Gamma(\mathcal{X})}|s|_{\phi}
;

(ii)

\|\cdot\|_{\phi}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
;

(iii)

if
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is further reduced, then
\|\cdot\|_{\phi}=\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
.

Proof.

Cover
\mathcal{X}
with finitely many open subschemes
\mathcal{U}_{i}
with trivializing sections
\tau_{i}\in\Hnot(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{L})
. Denoting by
U_{i}
the generic fiber of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
, we have
s|_{U_{i}}=f_{i}\tau_{i}
with
f_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(U_{i})
, and
|s|_{\phi}=|f_{i}|
on
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}
, by definition of a model metric. By Lemma
4.9
, it follows that
\sup_{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}}|s|_{\phi}=\max_{\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{i})}|s|_{\phi}
, and (i) follows since
X^{\mathrm{an}}=\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}
and
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})=\bigcup_{i}\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{i})
.

In order to prove (ii) and (iii), let
s\in\Hnot(L)
be a nonzero section. Since
\|s\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
belongs to value group
|K^{\times}|
, we may then multiply
s
be a scalar and assume that
\|s\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}=1
, i.e.
s\in\Hnot(\mathcal{L})
but
s\notin K^{\circ\circ}\Hnot(\mathcal{L})
. Choose as above a finite cover
\mathcal{X}
by affine open subscheme
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\spec(\mathcal{A}_{i})
with a trivializing section
\tau_{i}
of
\mathcal{L}
, and write
s|_{U_{i}}=f_{i}\tau_{i}
with
f_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. On
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}
, we have
|s|_{\phi}=|f_{i}|\leq 1
, and hence
\|s\|_{\phi}\leq 1
, which proves (ii).

Finally, suppose that
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is reduced. To prove (iv), it is enough to show that a section
s\in\Hnot(L)
with
\|s\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}=1
satisfies
\|s\|_{\phi}=1
. Assume by contradiction that a nonzero section
\|s\|_{\phi}<1
. For each
i
, we then have
\sup_{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\beth}}|f_{i}|<1
, and hence
a_{i}^{-1}f_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
for some nonzero
a_{i}\in K^{\circ\circ}
, by Theorem
4.19
. Setting
a:=a_{i_{0}}
for an index
i_{0}
achieving
\max_{i}|a_{i}|
, we infer that
a^{-1}f_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
for all
i
, and hence
a^{-1}s\in\Hnot(\mathcal{L})
, a contradiction.
∎

The next result will be a key ingredient in the proof of Theorem
9.15
below.

Theorem 6.4
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically reduced, let
\mathcal{L}
be a model of
L
determined on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, and denote by
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
the induced model metric on
L
. Then the supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
and the lattice norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(m\mathcal{L}\right)}
on
\Hnot\left(mL\right)
satisfy

\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(m\mathcal{L}\right)}\leq C\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}.

for a constant
C>0
independent of
m
.

Proof.

Lemma
6.3
yields the left-hand inequality. Fix an algebraically closed complete field extension
F/K
, and denote by
(\mathcal{X}_{F},\mathcal{L}_{F})
the base change of
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L})
to
F^{\circ}
, which is thus a model of
(X_{F},L_{F})
. The pulled back metric
\phi_{F}
is the model metric determined by
\mathcal{L}_{F}
, and the ground field extension of
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
to
\Hnot(L_{F})=\Hnot(L)_{F}
coincides with the lattice norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L}_{F})}
, by Lemma
1.29
. By Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.3
, we infer

\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\leq\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)_{F}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L}_{F})}.

Since
X
is geometrically reduced,
X_{F}
is reduced, and Theorem
4.20
thus yields a model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
X_{F}
with a finite morphism
\mu:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}_{F}
such that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{s}
is reduced. By Lemma
5.10
, we have
\phi_{F}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}_{F}}=\phi_{\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F}}
, and hence
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F})}=\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}
, by Lemma
6.3
. All in all, we get

\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F})}=\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\leq\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)_{F}\leq(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})})_{F}=\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L}_{F})},

and it will thus be enough to show that
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L}_{F})}\leq C\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F})}
for a uniform constant
C>0
. By the projection formula, we have an injection of
K^{\circ}
-modules

\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},m\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F}\right)/\Hnot(m\mathcal{L}_{F})\hookrightarrow\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}_{F},\mathcal{F}(m\mathcal{L}_{F})\right),

where
\mathcal{F}:=\left(\mu_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\right)/\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{F}}
is a coherent module on
\mathcal{X}_{F}
supported in the special fiber (cf. Theorem
A.6
for coherence), and hence
a
-torsion for some nonzero
a\in K^{\prime\circ\circ}
. We thus have
a\Hnot(m\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F})\subset\Hnot(m\mathcal{L}_{F})
, and hence
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L}_{F})}\leq|a|^{-1}\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mu^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{F})}
, which yields the desired result.
∎

For later use, we also provide the following technical generalization of Theorem
6.4
. A bounded metric on a line bundle
L
over
X
induces for each
r\geq 1
a bounded metric
\phi^{\boxtimes r}
on the external tensor product
L^{\boxtimes r}
over
X^{r}:=X\times_{K}\dots\times_{K}X
(
r
times). If
\phi
is the model metric determined by a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
\mathcal{X}
, then
\phi^{\boxtimes r}
is the model metric determined by
\mathcal{L}^{\boxtimes r}
over
\mathcal{X}^{r}:=\mathcal{X}\times_{K^{\circ}}\dots\times_{K^{\circ}}\mathcal{X}
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically reduced, and let
\phi_{i}
be a finite family of model metrics on line bundles
L_{i}
, with
\phi_{i}
determined by a line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{i}
on a given model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. We can then find a constant
C\geq 1
such that for all integers
r,m_{i}\geq 1
, we have

\|\cdot\|_{(\sum_{i}m_{i}\phi_{i})^{\boxtimes r}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}^{r},(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i})^{\boxtimes r}\right)}\leq C^{r}\|\cdot\|_{(\sum_{i}m_{i}\phi_{i})^{\boxtimes r}}

as norms on
\Hnot(X^{r},(\sum_{i}m_{i}L_{i})^{\boxtimes r})
.

Note that
X^{r}
is reduced for each
r
, since
X
is geometrically reduced, so that
\|\cdot\|_{(\sum_{i}m_{i}\phi_{i})^{\boxtimes r}}
is indeed a norm.

Proof.

Use the notation in the proof of Theorem
6.4
. Since
F
is algebraically closed, so is its residue field
\widetilde{F}
; the
\widetilde{F}
-scheme
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{s}
is thus geometrically reduced, and

(\mathcal{X}^{\prime r})_{s}=(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\times_{F^{\circ}}\dots\times_{F^{\circ}}\mathcal{X}^{\prime})_{s}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{s}\times_{\widetilde{F}}\dots\times_{\widetilde{F}}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{s}

is therefore reduced. As above, we have

\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime r},(\mu^{r})^{\star}(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i,F})^{\boxtimes r}\right)}=\|\cdot\|_{(\sum_{i}m_{i}\phi_{i,F})^{\boxtimes r}}\leq\left(\|\cdot\|_{(\sum_{i}m_{i}\phi_{i})^{\boxtimes r}}\right)_{F}

\leq\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}^{r},(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i})^{\boxtimes r}\right)}\right)_{F}=\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}_{F}^{r},(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{F})^{\boxtimes r}\right)},

and

\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime r},(\mu^{r})^{\star}(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{F})^{\boxtimes r}\right)/\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}_{F}^{r},(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i,F})^{\boxtimes r}\right)\hookrightarrow\Hnot\left(\mathcal{X}_{F}^{r},\mathcal{F}_{r}(\sum_{i}m_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i,F})^{\boxtimes r}\right)

with
\mathcal{F}_{r}:=\left((\mu^{r})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime r}}\right)/\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{F}^{r}}
. It will thus be enough to show that
\mathcal{F}_{r}
is
a^{r}
-torsion, where
a\in F^{\circ\circ}
annihilates
\mathcal{F}=\left(\mu_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\right)/\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{F}}
as above. Let
\mathcal{U}=\spec(\mathcal{A})
be an affine open subset of
\mathcal{X}_{F}
. Since
\mu
is finite,
\mu^{-1}(\mathcal{U})=\spec(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
is also affine. Since
a\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}
, we get
a^{r}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes_{F^{\circ}}\dots\otimes_{F^{\circ}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}\otimes_{F^{\circ}}\dots\otimes_{F^{\circ}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, which implies as desired
a^{r}(\mu^{r})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime r}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{F}^{r}}
.
∎

6.2.
Supnorms and ground field extension

Even though supnorms are generally not compatible with ground field extension (see Lemma
6.2
), the following result shows that it becomes asymptotically true for large powers of a line bundle (thanks to Walter Gubler for his help with the argument).

Theorem 6.6
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically reduced. Let
F/K
be an arbitrary complete field extension,
\phi
a continuous metric on
L
, and denote by
\phi_{F}
its pull-back to
L_{F}
. Then

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F}\right)=o(m).

If
K
is Archimedean, or if
\phi
is a model metric, then
\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F}\right)=O(1)
.

Proof.

The Archimedean case follows directly from Lemma
6.1
. From now on,
K
is non-Archimedean. Assume that
\phi
is a model metric, and pick a model
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L})
of
(X,aL)
,
a\geq 1
, such that
\phi=a^{-1}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
. After passing to a higher model, we can also assume that
L
has a model
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{X}
(Lemma
5.8
). Pick
m\geq 1
and write
m=qa+r
with
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq r<a
. Denote by
\mathcal{X}_{F}
,
\mathcal{L}_{F}
and
\mathcal{M}_{F}
the base change of
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
to
F^{\circ}
. Since
m\phi=\phi_{q\mathcal{L}}+r\phi
and
\phi-\phi_{\mathcal{M}}
is bounded,

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F}}}\right)=O(1)\text{ and }\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M}}})_{F}\right)=O(1),

using again Proposition
1.25
. By Theorem
6.4
,

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F}}},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F})}\right)=O(1)\text{ and }\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M}}})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})})_{F}\right)=O(1).

By Lemma
1.28
,
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F})}=(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})})_{F}
, and it follows that

\dGI((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})\leq\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M}}})_{F}\right)+\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M}}})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(q\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})})_{F}\right)

+\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F})},\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F}}}\right)+\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{\phi_{q\mathcal{L}_{F}+r\mathcal{M}_{F}}},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\right),

which is thus bounded.

Let now
\phi
be an arbitrary continuous metric. Assume first that
K
is nontrivially valued, and pick
\varepsilon>0
. By Theorem
5.18
, we can find a model metric
\psi
on
L
such that
\sup|\phi-\psi|\leq\varepsilon
.
By invariance of
\dGI
under ground field extension (Proposition
1.25
), we infer

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F}\right)\leq\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi_{F}}\right)+\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\psi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})_{F}\right)

+\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F}\right)\leq C+2m\varepsilon.

Thus
\limsup_{m\to\infty}m^{-1}\dGI((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})\leq\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon>0
, and hence

\dGI((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})=o(m).

Assume next that
K
is trivially valued, and that
F
is nontrivially valued. By Lemma
1.26
, there exists a nontrivially valued extension
K^{\prime}/K
such that for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the ground field extension
(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{K^{\prime}}
to
\Hnot(mL)_{K^{\prime}}
is the unique norm that coincides with
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
on
\Hnot(mL)
, and hence
(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{K^{\prime}}=\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{K^{\prime}}}
, by Lemma
6.1
.

Choose a common non-Archimedean extension
F^{\prime}
of both
K^{\prime}
and
F
, and note that
(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F^{\prime}}=(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{K^{\prime}}})_{F^{\prime}}
. By Proposition
1.25
and the triangle inequality, we get

\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\right)=\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F^{\prime}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})_{F^{\prime}}\right)

=\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{K^{\prime}}})_{F^{\prime}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})_{F^{\prime}}\right)

\leq\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{K^{\prime}}})_{F^{\prime}},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F^{\prime}}}\right)+\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F^{\prime}}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})_{F^{\prime}}\right),

which is
o(m)
by the first part of the proof applied to the nontrivially valued fields
F
and
K^{\prime}
.

Assume finally that both
K
and
F
are trivially valued, and chose a nontrivially valued extension
F^{\prime}
of
F
. We similarly get

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F}\right)=\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})_{F^{\prime}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F^{\prime}}\right)

\leq\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}})_{F^{\prime}},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F^{\prime}}}\right)+\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F^{\prime}}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F^{\prime}}\right)

Since
F^{\prime}
is nontrivially valued, the previous step shows that the last two terms are
o(m)
, and we are done.
∎

7.
Plurisubharmonic metrics and envelopes

Following
[
BFJ16
,
BJ18a
]
, we introduce a general notion of plurisubharmonic metric, and compare it with other existing notions. In what follows,
X
denotes a projective scheme over a complete valued field
K
.

7.1.
Plurisubharmonic metrics

Assume first that
K
is Archimedean (the case
K=\mathbb{R}
merely consisting in working with conjugation-invariant objects). A (possibly singular) metric
\phi
on a line bundle
L
is
plurisubharmonic
(
psh
for short) if, for each local trivialization
\tau
of
L
, the function
-\log|\tau|_{\phi}
is psh, i.e. locally the restriction of a psh function in a polydisc. Note that
\phi
is psh iff its pullback to
L_{\mathrm{red}}
is psh. For any
m\geq 1
,
\phi
is psh iff
m\phi
is psh, and psh metrics thus make sense on
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles.

Theorem 7.1
.

Assume that
K
is Archimedean, and let
L
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle. Then every psh metric
\phi
on
L
is the limit of a decreasing sequence
(\phi_{j})
of Fubini–Study metrics on
L
.

Proof.

When
X
is smooth and
L
is ample, the result is due to Demailly, and is based on the deep Ohsawa–Takegoshi
L^{2}
-extension theorem (see for instance
[
GZ05
, Theorem 8.1]
and its proof). In the general case, le
A
be the Stein factoration of
(X,L)
as in Lemma
5.5
, i.e.
A
