is an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on a projective scheme
Y
, and
L=f^{\star}A
with
f:X\to Y
surjective and such that
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. By Zariski’s main theorem,
f:X(\mathbb{C})\to Y(\mathbb{C})
has connected fibers, and
\phi
thus descends to a (singular) usc metric on
A
. Relying on the deep Fornaess–Narasimhan theorem, one checks as in the final part of the proof of
[
Dem85
, Théorème 1.7]
that the induced metric on
A
is psh. As a result, we may assume wlog that
L
was ample to begin with. Passing to a multiple, we can even assume that
L
is a very ample line bundle, and hence
L=\mathcal{O}(1)|_{X}
for an embedding of
X
in a projective space
\mathbb{P}
. By
[
CGZ13
, Theorem B’]
, every psh metric
\phi
on
L
is then the restriction of a psh metric
\psi
on
\mathcal{O}(1)
. Since
\mathbb{P}
is smooth, Demailly’s result implies that
\psi
is the decreasing limit of a sequence
\psi_{j}
of Fubini–Study metrics on
\mathcal{O}(1)
. The restriction of each
\psi_{j}
to
X
is then a Fubini–Study metric on
L
(Proposition
5.4
(iv)), and the result follows.
∎

In view of Theorem
7.1
, it is natural to introduce as in
[
BJ18a
]
:

Definition 7.2
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean, and let
L
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle. We say that a (possibly singular) metric
\phi
on
L
is
plurisubharmonic
(
psh
for short) if
\phi
can be written as the pointwise limit of a decreasing net
\phi_{i}\in\FS(L)
of Fubini–Study metrics on
L
. We denote by
\PSH(L)
the set of psh metrics on
L
.

Given a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}
, nontrivial if
K
is trivially valued,
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
is dense in
\FS(L)
with respect to uniform convergence, by Proposition
5.4
(v); it would thus be equivalent to require
\phi_{i}\in\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
in the above definition.

As a direct consequence of Corollary
5.7
, we have:

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
A
be the Stein factorization of
L
. Then

\PSH(A)\simeq\PSH(L)\simeq\PSH(L_{\mathrm{red}}).

The following general result, borrowed from
[
BJ18a
, Proposition 5.6]
, ensures that
\PSH(L)
is closed under decreasing limits.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\cz(L)
be a family of continuous metrics on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
, closed under addition of a constant, and define
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
as the set of possibly singular metrics on
L
that can be written as decreasing limits of nets in
\mathcal{F}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
is closed under decreasing limits.

Proof.

Let
(\phi_{i})_{i\in I}
be a decreasing net in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
. For each
i\in I
choose a decreasing net
(\phi_{i,j})_{j\in J_{i}}
in
\mathcal{F}
converging to
\phi_{i}
. Let
A
be the set of triples
\alpha=(i,j,m)
with
i\in I
,
j\in J_{i}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. For each
\alpha=(i,j,m)
in
A
, set

\psi_{\alpha}:=\phi_{i,j}+m^{-1}\in\mathcal{F},

and define a partial order on
A
by

\alpha\geq\alpha^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow\psi_{\alpha}\leq\psi_{\alpha^{\prime}}.

We claim that
A
is directed, i.e. any two elements
a_{1}=(i_{1},j_{1},m_{1})
,
a_{2}=(i_{2},j_{2},m_{2})
in
A
can be dominated by a third. To see this, first pick
i\geq i_{1},i_{2}
, so that
\phi_{i}\leq\phi_{i_{1}}
and
\phi_{i}\leq\phi_{i_{2}}
, and set
m:=\max\{m_{1},m_{2}\}+1
. A simple variant of Dini’s lemma shows that
\phi_{i,j}+m^{-1}\leq\phi_{i_{k},j_{k}}+m_{k}^{-1}=\psi_{a_{k}}
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
for
k=1,2
and all
j\in J_{i}
large enough, which proves the claim. By construction,
(\psi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a decreasing net in
\mathcal{F}
such that
\psi_{\alpha}\to\phi
pointwise on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and hence
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

The space
\PSH(L)
is stable under finite max and decreasing limits.

Corollary 7.6
.

Pick a subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}
, nontrivial if
K
is trivially valued. For any semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
, the set
\cz\cap\PSH(L)
of continuous psh metrics on
L
is the closure of
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
with respect to uniform convergence.

Proof.

Let
\phi
be a continuous psh metric, and pick a decreasing net
(\phi_{i})
in
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
converging pointwise to
\phi
. By Dini’s lemma,
\phi_{i}\to\phi
uniformly on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and
\phi
is thus in the closure of
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
. Conversely, let
(\phi_{i})
be a sequence in
\FS_{\Gamma}(L)
converging unifornly to some (continuous) metric
\phi
. We can inductively construct a sequence of constants
c_{i}\to 0
such that
\phi_{i}+c_{i}
is decreasing, and it follows that
\phi=\lim_{i}(\phi_{i}+c_{i})
is psh.
∎

7.2.
Nef metrics

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean. A model metric
\phi
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is
nef
if
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
for some
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
which is
nef
, i.e.
\mathcal{L}\cdot C\geq 0
for each
\widetilde{K}
-projective curve
C\subset\mathcal{X}_{s}
. By
[
GM19
, Proposition 3.5]
, any
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of
L
such that
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
is then also nef.

Definition 7.7
.

A
nef metric
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is a continuous metric
\phi
that can be written as a uniform limit of nef model metrics.

This is usually known as a semipositive metric in the sense of S.W. Zhang, cf.
[
GM19
]
for a thorough discussion. When
K
is trivially valued, the trivial metric is the only nef metric.

Theorem 7.8
.

Assume that
K
is nontrivially valued, and let
L
be an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle. Then a continuous metric on
L
is nef iff it is psh (in the sense of Definition
7.2
).

A proof of this result is also sketched, in a slightly different language, in
[
CMor18
, Remark 3.18]
; we nevertheless provide a proof below, for completeness. By
[
GM19
, Corollary 1.4]
, we get:

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
L
be an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle, and pick a
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
. Then
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
is psh iff
\mathcal{L}
is nef.

Proof of Theorem
7.8
.

By Theorem
5.14
, pure Fubini–Study metrics on
L
coincide with model metrics associated to
\mathbb{Q}
-models
\mathcal{L}
of
L
that are semiample, and hence nef. Since
K
is nontrivially valued, Corollary
7.6
implies that every continuous psh metric is a uniform limit of pure Fubini–Study metrics, and hence is nef.

To prove the converse, let
\mathcal{L}
be a nef
\mathbb{Q}
-model of
L
, determined on a model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. Using again Theorem
5.14
, it will be enough to show that
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
is a uniform limit of model metrics associated to semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-models. Since
L
is ample, it admits an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-model
\mathcal{A}
on some projective model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
dominating
\mathcal{X}
(cf.
[
GM19
, Lemma 4.12]
). The pull-back
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is nef. For each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}:=(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{L}^{\prime}+\varepsilon\mathcal{H}
is thus ample on
\mathcal{X}_{s}
, and hence on
\mathcal{X}
, by
[
EGA
, IV.9.6.4]
. In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is a semiample model of
L
.
Since
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
(Lemma
5.10
),
\phi_{\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}}-\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\varepsilon(\phi_{\mathcal{H}}-\phi_{\mathcal{L}})
converges to
0
uniformly as
\varepsilon>0
, and we are done.
∎

Remark 7.10
.

We expect Theorem
7.8
and Corollary
7.9
to hold when
L
is merely semiample. For this, it would be enough to show that any nef model metric on
L
descends to the Stein factorization
A
as in Lemma
5.5
; this would for instance follow from a relative version of the local Hodge index theorem proved in
[
YZ16
, Theorem 2.1]
.

7.3.
Psh-regularizable metrics

The
curvature form
of a smooth metric
\phi
on a line bundle
L
is a closed
(1,1)
-form, which we denote by
dd^{c}\phi
(see
[
CLD12
, §6.4.1]
for the non-Archimedean case). For each trivializing section
\tau
of
L
over an open
U\subset X
, the function
\log|\tau|_{\phi}
is smooth, and
dd^{c}\phi=-dd^{c}\log|\tau|_{\phi}
on
U^{\mathrm{an}}
. Again, this makes sense for
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles as well.

Definition 7.11
.

A smooth metric
\phi
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is
semipositive
if its curvature form
dd^{c}\phi
is semipositive (see
[
CLD12
, §5.4]
for the non-Archimedean case).

Example 7.12
.

Let
L
be a line bundle,
s_{1},\dots s_{r}\in\Hnot(L)
be sections without common zeroes,
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\max_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}
a regularized max function. Then

\phi:=\mathrm{max}_{\varepsilon}\left\{\log|s_{1}|+\lambda_{1},\ldots,\log|s_{r}|+\lambda_{r}\right\}

is a smooth semipositive metric on
L
, see
[
CLD12
, 6.3.2]
.

Definition 7.13
.

A (possibly singular) metric
\phi
on a line bundle
L
is
psh-regularizable
if
\phi
can be written as the limit of a decreasing net
\phi_{i}
of smooth semipositive metrics.

If
\phi
is continuous then
\phi_{i}\to\phi
uniformly, by Dini’s lemma.

Theorem 7.14
.

Every psh metric
\phi
on a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is psh-regularizable.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.4
, it is enough to prove the result when
\phi
is a Fubini–Study metric. After replacing
L
with a multiple, there exists a basis
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{N})
of
\Hnot(L)
without common zeroes and constants
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi=\max\left\{\log|s_{1}|+\lambda_{1}\dots,\log|s_{N}|+\lambda_{N}\right\}
, and
\phi
is thus the uniform limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
of the smooth semipositive metrics

\phi_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{max}_{\varepsilon}\left\{\log|s_{1}|+\lambda_{1}\dots,\log|s_{N}|+\lambda_{N}\right\}

as in Example
7.12
.
∎

7.4.
The Fubini–Study operator

The following discussion is closely related to the point of view developed by Chen and Moriwaki in
[
CMor18
, §3.2]
.

Definition 7.15
.

Let
L
be a globally generated line bundle. We say that a subspace
V\subset\Hnot(L)
is
basepoint free
if sections in
V
generate
L
at all points. To each norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
, we then associate the metric
\FS(\|\cdot\|)
on
L
defined at each
x\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
as the quotient norm

\FS(\|\cdot\|)(x):=\log\sup_{s\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|s(x)|}{\|s\|}.

Recall that
\log|s|
denotes the singular metric attached to
s\in\Hnot(L)
, cf. Example
5.1
.

Theorem 7.16
.

For any norm
\|\cdot\|
on a basepoint free subspace
V\subset\Hnot(L)
, the metric
\FS(\|\cdot\|)
is continuous and psh.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}
be finitely many sections of
L
without common zeroes, and
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
V\subset\Hnot(L)
be the basepoint free subspace generated by the
s_{i}
. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
be the norm on
K^{r}
which is diagonal in the canonical basis
(e_{i})
and such that
\|e_{i}\|_{\lambda}=e^{-\lambda_{i}}
, and denote by
\|\cdot\|
the induced quotient norm on
V
with respect to the surjective map
K^{r}\to V
that sends
(e_{i})
to
(s_{i})
. Then:

(i)

\FS(\|\cdot\|)=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\sum_{i}|s_{i}|^{2}e^{2\lambda_{i}}
when
K
is Archimedean;

(ii)

\FS(\|\cdot\|)=\max_{i}(\log|s_{i}|+\lambda_{i})
when
K
is non-Archimedean.

Proof.

For each
s\in V
, we have
\|s\|=\inf\left\{\|a\|_{\lambda}\mid a\in K^{r},\,s=\sum_{i}a_{i}s_{i}\right\}
. Using this, one immediately checks that

\FS(\|\cdot\|)=\log\sup_{a\in K^{r}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\sum_{i}a_{i}s_{i}|}{\|a\|_{\lambda}}

pointwise on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
. In the Archimedean case, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields

\sup_{a\in K^{r}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\sum_{i}a_{i}s_{i}|^{2}}{\|a\|_{\lambda}^{2}}=\sum_{i}\frac{|s_{i}|^{2}}{\|e_{i}\|_{\lambda}^{2}}=\sum_{i}|s_{i}|^{2}e^{2\lambda_{i}}.

In the non-Archimedean case, Lemma
1.16
yields

\sup_{a\in K^{r}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\sum_{i}a_{i}s_{i}|}{\|a\|_{\lambda}}=\max_{i}\frac{|s_{i}|}{\|e_{i}\|_{\lambda}}=\max_{i}|s_{i}|e^{\lambda_{i}}.

The result follows.
∎

Corollary 7.18
.

For a continuous metric
\phi
on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\phi
is a Fubini–Study metric;

(ii)

for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
divisible enough, there exists a basepoint free subspace
V\subset\Hnot(mL)
and a diagonalizable norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
such that
\phi=m^{-1}\FS(\|\cdot\|)
.

Proof.

That (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i) follows directly from Lemma
7.17
. Assume conversely that
\phi
is Fubini–Study. For
m
divisible enough we can find sections
s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}
of
mL
without common zeroes such that

\phi=\tfrac{1}{2m}\log\sum_{i}|s_{i}|^{2}
in the Archimedean case, and
\phi=\tfrac{1}{m}\max_{i}(\log|s_{i}|+\lambda_{i})
with
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
in the non-Archimedean case. By Lemma
7.17
,
\phi=m^{-1}\FS(\|\cdot\|)
with
\|\cdot\|
the quotient norm on
V=\Vect(s_{i})
of some diagonalizable norm. By Lemma
1.13
,
\|\cdot\|
is diagonalizable, and we are done.
∎

Example 7.19
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean, and let
\mathcal{L}
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-model of a (semiample)
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
. For
m
divisible enough,
m\mathcal{L}
is a globally generated line bundle, and the lattice norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}
on
\Hnot(mL)
satisfies

\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=m^{-1}\FS\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}\right)

This follows indeed from Theorem
5.14
(and its proof).

Proof of Theorem
7.16
.

Let
V\subset\Hnot(L)
be a basepoint free subspace, and note first that for any two norms
\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
V
, we trivially have

(7.1)

\FS(\|\cdot\|)-\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\leq\FS(\|\cdot\|^{\prime})\leq\FS(\|\cdot\|)+\dGI(\|\cdot\|,\|\cdot\|^{\prime}).

When
K
is non-Archimedean, Theorem
1.19
shows that, for each norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
, there exists a sequence of diagonalizable norms
\|\cdot\|_{i}
such that
\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{i},\|\cdot\|)\to 0
. By Lemma
7.17
,
\FS(\|\cdot\|_{i})
is a Fubini–Study metric for all
i
, and
\FS(\|\cdot\|_{i})
converges uniformly to
\FS(\|\cdot\|)
, by (
7.1
). This proves that
\FS(\|\cdot\|)
is continuous and psh.

In the Archimedean case, the unit ball
B\subset V
of
\|\cdot\|
is compact, and

\FS(\|\cdot\|)=\sup_{s\in B}\log|s|

pointwise. Using this, it is easy to see that
\FS(\|\cdot\|)
is continuous and psh.
∎

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be a norm on a basepoint free subspace
V\subset\Hnot(L)
. Let
F/K
be a complete field extension, and denote by
\|\cdot\|_{F}
the ground field extension of
\|\cdot\|
to
V_{F}\subset\Hnot(L)_{F}=\Hnot(L)_{F}
. Then
\FS(\|\cdot\|_{F})
is the pullback to
L_{F}
of
\FS(\|\cdot\|)
.

Proof.

In the Archimedean case, the only nontrivial case is
K=\mathbb{R}
,
F=\mathbb{C}
. Denote by
B\subset V
and
B_{\mathbb{C}}\subset V_{\mathbb{C}}
the unit balls of
\|\cdot\|
,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{C}}
. We need to show that
\sup_{s\in B}\log|s|=\sup_{s\in B_{\mathbb{C}}}\log|s|
, which is an easy consequence of Proposition
1.25
. Assume now that
K
is non-Archimedean. As above, it is enough to prove the result when
\|\cdot\|
is diagonalizable. Let
(s_{i})
be an orthogonal basis of
\Hnot(L)
. The pullback
(s^{\prime}_{i})
of
(s_{i})
to
\Hnot(L_{F})
is orthogonal for
\|\cdot\|_{F}
with
\|s^{\prime}_{i}\|_{F}=\|s_{i}\|
, and Lemma
7.17
thus yields

\FS(\|\cdot\|)=\max_{i}\{\log|s_{i}|-\log\|s_{i}\|\},\,\,\,\,\FS(\|\cdot\|_{F})=\max_{i}\{\log|s^{\prime}_{i}|-\log\|s^{\prime}_{i}\|_{F}\},

which proves the result.
∎

Definition 7.21
.

Let
L
be a globally generated line bundle, and denote by
L_{\mathrm{red}}
its restriction to
X_{\mathrm{red}}
. For each bounded metric
\phi
, we set

\FS(\phi):=\FS(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})
, where
\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
is the supnorm induced by
\phi
on
\Hnot(L_{\mathrm{red}})
.

Theorem 7.22
.

If
\phi
is a Fubini–Study metric on a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
, then
\phi=m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
for all
m
sufficiently divisible.

Lemma 7.23
.

For each bounded metric
\phi
on a globally generated line bundle
L
, we have

(i)

\FS(\phi)\leq\phi
;

(ii)

if
\phi=\FS(\|\cdot\|)
for some norm
\|\cdot\|
on a basepoint free subspace
V\subset\Hnot(L)
, then
\FS(\phi)=\phi
;

(iii)

\|\cdot\|_{\FS(\phi)}=\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
as norms on
\Hnot(L_{\mathrm{red}})
;

(iv)

for any other bounded metric
\phi^{\prime}
on
L
we have

(7.2)

\sup\left|\FS(\phi)-\FS(\phi^{\prime})\right|\leq\sup|\phi-\phi^{\prime}|.

Proof.

For any nonzero
s\in\Hnot(L_{\mathrm{red}})
, the definitional inequality
|s|_{\phi}\leq\|s\|_{\phi}
yields
\log|s|-\phi\leq\log\|s\|_{\phi}
, and

\FS(\phi)=\sup_{s\in\Hnot(L_{\mathrm{red}})\setminus\{0\}}\left(\log|s|-\log\|s\|_{\phi}\right)\leq\phi,

proving (i). Assume
\phi=\FS(\|\cdot\|)
for a norm
\|\cdot\|
on a basepoint free subspace
V\subset\Hnot(L)
. For each
s\in V
, we have
\log|s|-\log\|s\|\leq\phi
, i.e.
\|s\|_{\phi}\leq\|s\|
. Thus

\FS(\|\cdot\|_{\phi})\geq\sup_{s\in V\setminus\{0\}}\left(\log|s|-\log\|s\|\right)=\phi,

and hence (ii). Since
\FS(\phi)\leq\phi
, each nonzero
s\in\Hnot(L_{\mathrm{red}})
satisfies
\|s\|_{\phi}\leq\|s\|_{\FS(\phi)}
. On the other hand,
\log|s|-\log\|s\|_{\phi}\leq\FS(\phi)
, hence
|s|_{\FS(\phi)}\leq\|s\|_{\phi}
, which proves (iii).

Finally, we trivially have
\phi\leq\phi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\FS(\phi)\leq\FS(\phi^{\prime})
and
\FS(\phi+c)=\FS(\phi)+c
for
c\in\mathbb{R}
, which formally imply (
7.2
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.22
.

By Corollary
7.18
, for all
m
sufficiently divisible we have
\phi=m^{-1}\FS(\|\cdot\|)
with
\|\cdot\|
a norm on a basepoint free subspace
V\subset\Hnot(mL)
, and Lemma
7.23
(ii) thus yields
\phi=m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
.
∎

7.5.
Envelopes and ground field invariance

In what follows,
L
is a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
.

Definition 7.24
.

Let
\phi
be a bounded metric on
L
.

(i)

The
psh envelope
of
\phi
is defined as the pointwise upper envelope

(7.3)

\env(\phi):=\sup\{\psi\in\PSH(L)\mid\psi\leq\phi\}.

(ii)

The
regular psh envelope
of
\phi
is

(7.4)

\qq(\phi):=\sup\{\psi\in\FS(L)\mid\psi\leq\phi\}.

Since any continuous psh metric on
L
is a uniform limit of Fubini–Study metrics, it would be equivalent to require
\psi
continuous and psh in (ii). Trivially,
\qq(\phi)
is lsc,
\qq(\phi)\leq\env(\phi)\leq\phi
, and the operators
\env
and
\qq
are monotone increasing and
1
-Lipschitz with respect to the supnorm. They are related as follows.

Proposition 7.25
.

For any bounded metric
\phi
on
L
we have
\qq(\phi)=\env(\phi_{\star})
, with
\phi_{\star}\leq\phi
denoting the lsc regularization of
\phi
.

Proof.

A continuous metric
\psi
satisfies
\psi\leq\phi
iff
\psi\leq\phi_{\star}
, and hence
\qq(\phi)=\qq(\phi_{\star})
. We may thus assume from the start that
\phi
is lsc, and we then need to show that any psh metric
\psi
on
L
such that
\psi\leq\phi
satisfies
\psi\leq\qq(\phi)
. By Definition
7.2
, there exists a decreasing net
(\psi_{j})
of Fubini–Study metrics on
L
with
\psi_{j}\to\phi
. Pick
\varepsilon>0
. By lower semicontinuity of
\phi-\psi_{j}
and a simple compactness argument (Dini’s lemma), we thus have
\psi_{j}\leq\phi+\varepsilon
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
for
j
large enough. Thus
\psi_{j}\leq\qq(\phi)+\varepsilon
, and hence
\psi\leq\qq(\phi)+\varepsilon
, which yields the result.
∎

To
L
we attach the semigroup

\mathbb{N}(L)=\left\{m\in\mathbb{N}\mid mL\text{ is a globally generated line bundle}\right\}.

Theorem 7.26
.

For any bounded metric
\phi
on
L
we have

(7.5)

\lim_{\mathbb{N}(L)\ni m,\,m\to\infty}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)=\qq(\phi),

and the convergence is uniform iff
\qq(\phi)
is continuous.

Proof.

Pick
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}(L)
. For any two
s\in\Hnot(mL_{\mathrm{red}})
,
s^{\prime}\in\Hnot(m^{\prime}L_{\mathrm{red}})
, we have

(7.6)

\|s\cdot s^{\prime}\|_{(m+m^{\prime})\phi}\leq\|s\|_{m\phi}\|s^{\prime}\|_{m^{\prime}\phi}.

This implies
\FS((m+m^{\prime})\phi)\geq\FS(m\phi)+\FS(m^{\prime}\phi)
, and hence

\lim_{\mathbb{N}(L)\ni m,\,m\to\infty}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)=\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}(L)_{>0}}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi),

by Fekete’s lemma. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}(L)_{>0}
,
m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
is continuous and psh, by Theorem
7.16
. By Lemma
7.23
,
m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)\leq\phi
, and hence
m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)\leq\qq(\phi)
, which proves that

\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}(L)_{>0}}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)\leq\qq(\phi).

Conversely, pick
\psi\in\FS(L)
such that
\psi\leq\phi
. By Theorem
7.22
, we find
m\in\mathbb{N}(L)_{>0}
such that
\psi=m^{-1}\FS(m\psi)
. Thus
\psi\leq\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}(L)_{>0}}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
, and hence

\qq(\phi)\leq\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}(L)_{>0}}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi).

∎

Corollary 7.27
.

Let
\phi
be a bounded metric on
L
, and pick
m\in\mathbb{N}
such
mL
is a line bundle. Then

\|\cdot\|_{\phi}=\|\cdot\|_{\qq(\phi)}=\|\cdot\|_{\env(\phi)}

as norms on
\Hnot(mL_{\mathrm{red}})
.

Proof.

For each
s\in\Hnot(mL)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\|s^{k}\|_{km\phi}=\|s\|_{m\phi}^{k}
. After replacing
m
with a multiple, we may thus assume that
mL
is globally generated. Then

m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)\leq\qq(\phi)\leq\env(\phi)\leq\phi,

and we are done since
\|\cdot\|_{\FS(m\phi)}=\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
on
\Hnot(mL_{\mathrm{red}})
, by Lemma
7.23
.
∎

Definition 7.28
.

Let
L
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle. We say that
continuity of envelopes
holds for
L
if for any continuous metric
\phi
on
L
,
\env(\phi)=\qq(\phi)
is continuous.

By Theorem
7.26
and Dini’s lemma,
\env(\phi)
is continuous iff
m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
converges uniformly as
m\to\infty
.

Lemma 7.29
.

Continuity of envelopes is equivalent to the following property: for any family
(\phi_{\alpha})
of psh metrics on
L
, uniformly bounded above, the usc upper envelope
\left(\sup_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\right)^{\star}
is psh.

Proof.

Suppose first that continuity of envelopes holds, and let
\phi
be a continuous metric on
L
. Since each
\FS(m\phi)
is continuous,
\env(\phi)=\sup_{m}m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
is lsc, and its usc regularization
\env(\phi)^{\star}
is the decreasing limit of a net of asymptotically Fubini–Study metrics
\psi_{j}
. Since
\env(\phi)\leq\phi
and
\phi
is continuous,
\lim_{j}\psi_{j}=\env(\phi)^{\star}\leq\phi
, and (a small variant of) Dini’s lemma therefore yields
\psi_{j}\leq\phi+\varepsilon_{j}
for some constants
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0
. It follows that
\psi_{j}=\env(\psi_{j})\leq\env(\phi)+\varepsilon_{j}
, and hence
\env(\phi)^{\star}\leq\env(\phi)
in the limit, which proves that
\env(\phi)
is usc.

Conversely, asssume that
\env(\phi)
is continuous for each continuous metric
\phi
, and let
(\phi_{\alpha})
be a family of psh metrics, uniformly bounded above. The metric
\psi:=\left(\sup_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\right)^{\star}
, being usc, can be written as the limit of a decreasing net of continuous metrics
\tau_{j}
. For each
\alpha,j
, we have
\phi_{\alpha}\leq\tau_{j}
, and hence
\phi_{\alpha}\leq\env(\tau_{j})
, which in turn yields
\psi\leq\env(\tau_{j})\leq\tau_{j}
. We have thus written
\psi
as the limit of the decreasing net of psh metrics
\env(\tau_{j})
, which shows that
\psi
is psh.
∎

In the Archimedean case, the equivalent formulation of Lemma
7.29
is a classical property, assuming
X
to be normal. It is thus natural to conjecture:

Conjecture 7.30
.

Continuity of envelopes holds for any semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
over a
normal
projective variety
X
.

As of this writing, continuity of envelopes in the non-Archimedean case has been established when
X
is smooth and one of the following is satisfied:

•

X
is a curve, as a consequence of A. Thuillier’s work
[
Thu05
]
(see
[
GJKM19
]
);

•

K
discretely or trivially valued, of residue characteristic
0

[
BFJ16
,
BJ18a
]
, building on
multiplier ideals and the Nadel vanishing theorem;

•

K
is discretely valued of characteristic
p
,
(X,L)
is defined over a function field of
transcendence degree
d
, and resolution of singularities is assumed in dimension
n+d

[
GJKM19
]
, replacing multiplier ideals with test ideals.

We conclude this section with the following application of Theorem
7.26
.

Theorem 7.31
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically reduced. Let
\phi
be a continuous metric on a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
over
X
. Pick a complete field extension
F/K
, and denote
\phi_{F}
the pullback of
\phi
to
L_{F}
. Then
\phi
is psh iff
\phi_{F}
is psh.

Proof.

By definition, the pullback of a Fubini–Study metric on
L
is a Fubini–Study metric on
L_{F}
, and hence
\phi
psh implies
\phi_{F}
psh. Assume conversely that
\phi_{F}
is psh. By Theorem
7.26
,

\varepsilon_{m}:=\sup_{X_{F}^{\mathrm{an}}}\left|m^{-1}\FS(m\phi_{F})-\phi_{F}\right|

tends to
0
as
m\in\mathbb{N}(L)
tends to
\infty
; we need to show that
\phi_{m}:=m^{-1}\FS(m\phi)
converges uniformly to
\phi
. By Lemma
7.20
, we have

(\phi_{m})_{F}=m^{-1}\FS\left(\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)_{F}\right).

Thus

\sup_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}\left|\phi_{m}-\phi\right|=\sup_{X^{\mathrm{an}}_{F}}\left|(\phi_{m})_{F}-\phi_{F}\right|\leq\sup_{X^{\mathrm{an}}_{F}}\left|(\phi_{m})_{F}-m^{-1}\FS(m\phi_{F})\right|+\varepsilon_{m}

=m^{-1}\sup_{X^{\mathrm{an}}_{F}}\left|\FS\left(\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)_{F}\right)-\FS\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\right)\right|+\varepsilon_{m}\leq m^{-1}\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F})+\varepsilon_{m},

by (
7.1
). By Theorem
6.6
,
m^{-1}\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F})\to 0
, and are done.
∎

Part III
Asymptotics of relative volumes

8.
Monge–Ampère measures and Deligne pairings

In this section, we review some basic properties of Monge–Ampère operators, and use them to define metrics on Deligne pairings (Theorem
8.16
), following Deligne’s program
[
Del87
]
. In what follows,
X
is as before an
n
-dimensional projective scheme over a complete valued field
K
.

8.1.
Mixed Monge–Ampère measures

The (algebraic) fundamental class of
X
is the
n
-dimensional cycle

[X]=\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}[X_{\alpha}],

where the
X_{\alpha}
denote the
n
-dimensional irreducible components of
X
(with their reduced structure) and
m_{\alpha}
is the length of the local ring of
X
at the generic point of
X_{\alpha}
. The intersection number of
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
on
X
is defined as

(L_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n}):=\deg\pi_{\star}\left(c_{1}(L_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot c_{1}(L_{n})\cdot[X]\right),

with
\pi:X\to\Spec K
the structure morphism.

Now let
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
be smooth metrics on
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
, and recall that
dd^{c}\phi_{i}
denotes the curvature form of
\phi_{i}
, a smooth, closed
(1,1)
-form on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
(cf.
[
CLD12
, §6.4.1]
for the non-Archimedean case). The smooth
(n,n)
-form
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}
determines a Radon measure

(8.1)

dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}=\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X_{\alpha}}

on the topological space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, the
mixed Monge–Ampère measure
of the
\phi_{i}
, with

\delta_{X}=\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}\delta_{X_{\alpha}}
denoting the analytic fundamental class of
X
(see
[
CLD12
, §3.7]
for the non-Archimedean case).

Remark 8.1
.

When
K=\mathbb{R}
, we define
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
as the image of the corresponding measure on
X(\mathbb{C})=X_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{an}}
by the projection map
X_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{an}}\to X^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Example 8.2
.

Assume that
n=0
,
i.e.
X=\spec A
with
A
a finite
K
-algebra. The previous construction produces a positive measure
\delta_{X}
on the finite set
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, which is described as follows. We have a product decomposition
A=\prod_{i}A_{i}
into local finite
K
-algebras
A_{i}
corresponding to the connected components of
X
. The (reduced) irreducible components of
X
are given by
X_{i}=\spec K_{i}
, where the residue field
K_{i}
of
A_{i}
is a finite extension of
K
, and the unique extension of the absolute value of
K
to
K_{i}
defines a point
x_{i}\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
. The current
\delta_{X}=\sum_{i}m_{i}\delta_{X_{i}}
is a measure on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and the requirement that
\delta_{X_{i}}
has total mass
\deg\pi_{\star}[X_{i}]=[K_{i}:K]
yields
\delta_{X_{i}}=[K_{i}:K]\delta_{x_{i}}
. As
m_{i}[K_{i}:K]=\dim_{K}A_{i}
, we conclude that

\delta_{X}=\sum_{i}(\dim_{K}A_{i})\delta_{x_{i}}.

Proposition 8.3
.

For each tuple
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
of smooth metrics on
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
, the mixed Monge–Ampère measure
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

it is a symmetric, multilinear function of the
\phi_{i}
, and a positive Radon measure if the
\phi_{i}
are semipositive,
i.e.
dd^{c}\phi_{i}\geq 0
;

(ii)

\int dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}=(L_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n})
;

(iii)

if
L_{0}=L_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
(and hence
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
are smooth functions on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
), then

(8.2)

\int\phi_{0}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}=\int\phi_{1}\,dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}.

(iv)

for any complete field extension
F/K
, we have

p_{\star}\left(dd^{c}\phi_{1,F}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n,F}\wedge\delta_{X_{F}}\right)=dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X},

where
\phi_{i,F}
is the pullback of
\phi_{i}
to
L_{i,F}
and
p:X_{F}^{\mathrm{an}}\to X^{\mathrm{an}}
is the canonical projection.

Proof.

(i) is straightforward. (ii) is classical in the Archimedean case, and proved in
[
CLD12
, Proposition 6.4.3]
in the non-Archimedean case. (iii) is a consequence of the Stokes formula (see
[
CLD12
, Théorème 3.12.2]
in the non-Archimedean case). In the Archimedean case, (iv) is nontrivial only when
K=\mathbb{R}
,
F=\mathbb{C}
, in which case it holds by definition (see Remark
8.1
). In the non-Archimedean case, it is implicit in
[
CLD12
]
, and can be checked as follows. Pick a smooth, compactly supported function
f
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and consider the smooth, compactly supported
(n,n)
-form
\alpha:=f\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}
. According to
[
Gub16
, Proposition 5.13]
, there exists a Zariski open subset
U\subset X
with a closed embedding
U\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m,K}^{r}
and a compactly supported smooth
(n,n)
-superform on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
such that
\alpha
is obtained by pulling-back
\eta
by the tropicalization map
\trop:U^{\mathrm{an}}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and

\int f\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}=\int_{X}\alpha

is defined as the integral of
\eta
on the tropical cycle
\trop(U)
. Unravelling the definitions, it is clear that the pull-back
\alpha_{F}
of
\alpha
to
X_{F}^{\mathrm{an}}
is simply the pull-back of
\eta
by the tropicalization map
\trop_{F}:U_{F}^{\mathrm{an}}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
. The construction of the tropical cycle of an algebraic variety being invariant under ground field extension, we have
\trop_{F}(U_{F})=\trop(U)
as tropical cycles, and we conclude as desired that
\int_{X}\alpha=\int_{X_{F}}\alpha_{F}
.
∎

Recall from §
7.3
that a continuous metric on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
is psh-regularizable iff it is a uniform limit of smooth semipositive metrics on
L
. If
L
is semiample, then any continuous psh metric (in the sense of Definition
7.2
, i.e. a uniform limit of Fubini–Study metrics) is psh-regularizable, by Theorem
7.14
.

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
be
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles on
X
. The measure-valued operator

(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n})\mapsto dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X},

defined so far on smooth metrics, admits a unique continuous extension to tuples of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics on the
L_{i}
, with respect to uniform convergence for tuples of metrics, and the weak topology on Radon measures.

As a direct consequence of Proposition
8.3
, we get:

Proposition 8.5
.

For each tuple of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics
(\phi_{i})
,

dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}=\sum_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X_{\alpha}}

is a positive Radon measure on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, of total mass
(L_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n})
. It is further symmetric and multiadditive as a function of the
\phi_{i}
.

Theorem
8.4
follows from the classical Bedford–Taylor theory
[
BT76
]
in the Archimedean case, and from its analogue in the non-Archimedean case
[
CLD12
, Corollaire 5.6.5]
. In the present global setting, one can however provide a simple direct argument, based on the following global version of the classical Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality that will be put to use again a bit later.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
be line bundles on
X
, and suppose given a pair of smooth, semipositive metrics
\phi_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{i}
on each
L_{i}
. Then

\left|\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}-\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}\right|

\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup|\phi_{i}-\phi^{\prime}_{i}|

with
C:=2\max_{1\leq i\leq n}(L_{0}\cdot L_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{L_{i}}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n})
.

Proof.

For brevity of notation, set
T:=dd^{c}\phi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
. By (
8.2
),

\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T-\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T=\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}(\phi_{1}-\phi^{\prime}_{1})\wedge T

=\int(\phi_{1}-\phi_{1}^{\prime})\,dd^{c}(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\wedge T=\int(\phi_{1}-\phi_{1}^{\prime})\,dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge T+\int(\phi_{1}-\phi^{\prime}_{1})\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T.

Since
dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge T
and
dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T
are both positive measures of mass
(L_{0}\cdot L_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n})
, we infer

\left|\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T-\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T\right|\leq 2(L_{0}\cdot L_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n})\sup|\phi_{1}-\phi^{\prime}_{1}|.

We conclude by symmetry and multilinearity.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.4
.

Assume first that
K
is non-Archimedean, and pick
f\in\PL_{\mathbb{R}}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
. By Lemma
5.17
, we can find a pair of Fubini–Study metrics on an ample line bundle
L_{0}
such that
f=\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0}
. By Theorem
7.14
,
\phi_{0}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
are uniform limits of smooth, semipositive metrics on
L_{0}
. Lemma
8.6
thus shows that

(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n})\mapsto\int f\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}

is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the supnorm on tuples of smooth, semipositive metrics (with Lipschitz constant depending on
f
), and hence extends continuously to tuples of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics. For each tuple of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics
(\phi_{i})
, the linear form on
\PL_{\mathbb{R}}(X^{\mathrm{an}})

f\mapsto\int f\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
is positive, hence continuous, and it thus extends to a positive linear form on
\cz(X)
, by density of
\PL_{\mathbb{R}}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
.

In the Archimedean case, the argument is similar, arguing with a smooth function
f
instead. It is in fact even simpler, since
f
can directly be written as a difference of smooth psh metrics on some ample line bundle.
∎

In order to exploit the multilinearity of the previous construction, it is convenient to introduce the following terminology.

Definition 8.7
.

We say that a continuous metric
\phi
on a line bundle
L
is
dpsh
if it can be written as a difference of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics.

A dpsh metric is thus the same as an
approachable metric
in the sense of
[
CLD12
, §6.3]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Every line bundle
L
admits a smooth, dpsh metric.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.14
, any ample line bundle admits a smooth, semipositive metric, and
the result follows since
L
can be written as a difference of ample line bundles.
∎

Example 8.9
.

If
K
is Archimedean, every smooth metric is dpsh.

While this is probably true in the non-Archimedean case as well, it is less obvious, since strictly positive
(1,1)
-forms do not exist globally in this context.

Example 8.10
.

If
K
is non-Archimedean, every
\mathbb{R}
-PL metric is dpsh, by Lemma
5.17
.

The operator
(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n})\mapsto dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
, being symmetric and multiadditive on tuples of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics, extends to a symmetric, multilinear operator on continuous dpsh metrics, with values in signed Radon measure. Note that Proposition
8.3
still holds for such metrics.

Example 8.11
.

If
K
is non-Archimedean and
\phi_{1},\dots\phi_{n}
are
\mathbb{R}
-PL metrics, then
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
has finite support. By ground field invariance, we may indeed assume that
K
is algebraically closed, and that its value group is large enough to ensure that the
\phi_{i}
become pure after base change. By Proposition
5.16
,
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
are then induced by
\mathbb{Q}
-models
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
of
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
determined on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. By linearity with respect to the fundamental class
[X]
, we may further assume that
X
is (geometrically) irreducible, which allows us to assume that
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is reduced, by Theorem
4.20
. Each irreducible component
Y
of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
then determines a unique Shilov point
x_{Y}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
, and

(8.3)

dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}=\sum_{Y}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{Y}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{Y})\delta_{x_{Y}},

by
[
CLD12
, Proposition 6.9.2]
(compare
[
Gub07
, Proposition 3.11]
).

8.2.
A Poincaré–Lelong formula

We start with the following integrability result.

Theorem 8.12
.

Let
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
be continuous, psh-regularizable metrics on line bundles
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
, and let
s\in\Hnot(X,L)
be a regular section of a line bundle
L
, equiped with any continuous metric
\phi
. Then
\log|s|_{\phi}
is integrable with respect to
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
, and

\int\log|s|_{\phi}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}

depends continuously on the
\phi_{i}
with respect to uniform convergence.

Lemma 8.13
.

We can find two ample line bundles
A,A^{\prime}
, a decreasing sequence
(\psi_{j})
of smooth, semipositive metrics on
A
, and a smooth, semipositive metric
\psi^{\prime}
on
A^{\prime}
such that
L=A-A^{\prime}
and
\psi_{j}-\psi^{\prime}\to\log|s|
pointwise.

Proof.

Pick a very ample line bundle
A^{\prime}
such that
A:=L+A^{\prime}
is also very ample. Let
(s_{\alpha})
and
(s^{\prime}_{\beta})
be bases of
\Hnot(A)
and
\Hnot(A^{\prime})
, respectively, and set

\psi_{j}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\left(\sum_{\beta}|s\cdot s^{\prime}_{\beta}|^{2}+e^{-j}\sum_{\alpha}|s_{\alpha}|^{2}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\psi^{\prime}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\left(\sum_{\beta}|s^{\prime}_{\beta}|^{2}\right).

These metrics are smooth and semipositive (both in the Archimedean and non-Archimedean cases), and

\log|s|+\psi^{\prime}=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\left(\sum_{\beta}|s\cdot s^{\prime}_{\beta}|^{2}\right)

is the decreasing limit of
\psi_{j}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.12
.

Given any other metric
\phi^{\prime}
on
L
,
\log|s|_{\phi}-\log|s|_{\phi^{\prime}}=\phi^{\prime}-\phi
is a continuous function on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and Theorem
8.12
for
\phi
or
\phi^{\prime}
are thus equivalent, by Theorem
8.4
. By Lemma
8.8
, we may therefore assume that
\phi
is smooth dpsh.

Write
\log|s|
as the decreasing limit of a sequence of smooth dpsh metrics
\psi_{j}-\psi^{\prime}
as in Lemma
8.13
, so that

f_{j}:=\psi_{j}-\psi^{\prime}-\phi\to f:=\log|s|_{\phi},

where
\psi_{j}
and
\psi^{\prime}+\phi
are metrics on a fixed (ample) line bundle
A
. By Lemma
8.6
, there exists
C>0
only depending on the
L_{i}
and
A
such that for any other tuple
\phi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\phi^{\prime}_{n}
of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics on
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
we have

(8.4)

\left|\int f_{j}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}-\int f_{j}\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}\right|\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup|\phi_{i}-\phi^{\prime}_{i}|.

By monotone convergence, we infer

\displaystyle-C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup|\phi_{i}-\phi^{\prime}_{i}|+\int f\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}\leq\int f\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\int f\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}+C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup|\phi_{i}-\phi^{\prime}_{i}|

in
[-\infty,+\infty)
. It thus remains to show that
f=\log|s|_{\phi}
is integrable with respect to
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
for
some
choice of continuous, psh-regularizable metrics
(\phi_{i})
, which holds as soon as the
\phi_{i}
are smooth (see
[
CLD12
, 4.6.2]
for the non-Archimedean case, where we could alternatively take the
(\phi_{i})
to be model metrics, since

dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
is then supported in a finite set of Shilov points, by Example
8.11
).
∎

We are now in a position to state the following version of the Poincaré–Lelong formula, which plays a key role in the proof of Theorem A.

Theorem 8.14
.

Let
\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}
be continuous dpsh metrics on line bundles
L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}
, and set for brevity
T:=dd^{c}\phi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}
. Suppose also given a regular section
s\in\Hnot(X,L)
of a line bundle
L
, with divisor
Z
, a continuous dpsh metric
\phi
on
L
, and a continuous dpsh function
f
. Then

(8.5)

\int\log|s|_{\phi}\,dd^{c}f\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int f\,T\wedge\delta_{Z}-\int f\,dd^{c}\phi\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}.

Note that the first integral is well-defined, by Theorem
8.12
and multilinearity.

Proof.

Given any other continuous dpsh metric
\phi^{\prime}
on
L
, we have
\log|s|_{\phi}-\log|s|_{\phi^{\prime}}=\phi^{\prime}-\phi
, hence

\int\log|s|_{\phi}\,dd^{c}f\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}-\int\log|s|_{\phi^{\prime}}\,dd^{c}f\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)\,dd^{c}f\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\int f\,dd^{c}\phi^{\prime}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}-\int f\,dd^{c}\phi\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

by integration by parts. As a result, the desired formula for
\phi
is equivalent to that for
\phi^{\prime}
, and we may thus assume from the start that
\phi
is smooth, by Lemma
8.8
.

Assume next that
f
and
\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}
are also smooth. The Poincaré–Lelong formula (cf.
[
CLD12
, Theorem 4.6.5]
in the non-Archimedean case) implies that

dd^{c}\log|s|_{\phi}\wedge\delta_{X}=\delta_{Z}-dd^{c}\phi\wedge\delta_{X}

in the sense of currents, which yields (
8.5
). Assume finally that
f
and
\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}
are merely continuous dpsh. We can then find line bundles
M,M_{2},M^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,M_{n},M^{\prime}_{n}
and continuous, psh-approchable metrics
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
on
M
,
\psi_{i},\psi^{\prime}_{i}
on
M_{i}
,
M^{\prime}_{i}
such that
f=\psi-\psi^{\prime}
,
L_{i}=M_{i}-M^{\prime}_{i}
and
\phi_{i}=\psi_{i}-\psi^{\prime}_{i}
. Choose sequences
(\tau_{j})
,
(\tau^{\prime}_{j})
,
(\psi_{i,j})
and
(\psi^{\prime}_{i,j})
of smooth, semipositive metrics on
M
,
M_{i}
and
M^{\prime}_{i}
such that
\tau_{j}\to\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}_{j}\to\tau_{j}
,
\psi_{i,j}\to\psi_{i}
and
\psi^{\prime}_{i,j}\to\psi^{\prime}_{i}
uniformly. Set
f_{j}:=\tau_{j}-\tau^{\prime}_{j}
,
\phi_{i,j}:=\psi_{i,j}-\psi^{\prime}_{i,j}
, and
T_{j}:=dd^{c}\phi_{2,j}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n,j}
. Theorem
8.12
and multilinearity yield

\lim_{j}\int\log|s|_{\phi}\,dd^{c}f_{j}\wedge T_{j}\wedge\delta_{X}=\int\log|s|_{\phi}\,dd^{c}f\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

By Theorem
8.4
, we have weak convergence of measures

T_{j}\wedge\delta_{Z}\to T\wedge\delta_{Z},\,\,\,\,dd^{c}\phi\wedge T_{j}\wedge\delta_{X}\to dd^{c}\phi\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

and hence

\int f_{j}T_{j}\to\int_{Z}fT\,\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\,\int f_{j}\,dd^{c}\phi\wedge T_{j}\wedge\delta_{X}\to\int f\,dd^{c}\phi\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

by uniform convergence
f_{j}\to f
. Thus (
8.5
) in the smooth case implies the general case.
∎

8.3.
Metrics on Deligne pairings

As a special case of a general construction discussed extensively in Appendix
A
, the
Deligne pairing
associates to an
(n+1)
-tuple of line bundles
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
on an
n
-dimensional projective
K
-scheme
X
a line bundle
\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle
on
\spec K
, i.e. a one-dimensional
K
-vector space.

Following F. Ducrot’s approach
[
Duc05
]
, we view the Deligne pairing as the
(n+1)
-st iterated difference of the determinant of cohomology. In our situation, the determinant of cohomology of a line bundle
L
on
X
can simply be described as

\lambda(L)=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\det H^{i}(L),

and

\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\sum_{I\subset\{0,\ldots,n\}}(-1)^{n+1-|I|}\lambda\left(\sum_{i\in I}L_{i}\right).

This pairing is functorial, symmetric, multilinear, and the data of a regular section
s\in\Hnot(X,L_{0})
with zero divisor
Z
induces a canonical isomorphism

(8.6)

\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z}\rangle.

Example 8.15
.

When
n=0
, a regular section
s
of
L_{0}
is a trivializing section of
L_{0}
, and (
8.6
) thus yields a generator
\langle s_{0}\rangle\in\langle L_{0}\rangle
. Any other trivializing section of
L_{0}
is of the form
us_{0}
with
u\in A^{\times}
a unit, and

(8.7)

\langle us_{0}\rangle=N_{A/K}(u)\langle s_{0}\rangle

where
N_{A/K}(u)\in K^{\times}
is the
norm
of
u
,
i.e.
the determinant of the endomorphism of the
K
-vector space
A
given by multiplication by
u
. In other words, the Deligne pairing coincides with norm functor when
n=0
.

In line with Deligne’s program
[
Del87
]
and the work of Elkik
[
Elk89
]
, we prove:

Theorem 8.16
.

There exists a unique way to associate to each tuple of continuous dpsh metrics
\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}
on line bundles
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
over an
n
-dimensional projective
K
-scheme
X
a metric
\langle\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle
on
\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle
, such that the following holds:

(i)

the pairing
(\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n})\mapsto\langle\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle
is symmetric and multilinear;

(ii)

if
s\in\Hnot(L_{0})
is a regular section with zero divisor
Z
, the following
restriction formula
holds:

(8.8)

\langle\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\langle\phi_{0}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z}\rangle-\int\log|s|_{\phi_{0}}dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}

as metrics on
\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z}\rangle
.

Note that (ii) implies the
change of metric formula

(8.9)

\langle\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle-\langle\phi^{\prime}_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\int(\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0})dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}

for all continuous dpsh metrics
\phi_{0},\phi^{\prime}_{0}
on
L_{0}
and
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
on
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
. This follows indeed by applying (
8.8
) to the dpsh metrics
\phi_{0}-\phi^{\prime}_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
on
\mathcal{O}_{X},L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
and the regular section
s=1\in\Hnot(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}
be continuous dpsh metrics on line bundles
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
. Let
s_{0}\in\Hnot(L_{0})
,
s_{1}\in\Hnot(L_{1})
be regular sections with divisors
Z_{0},Z_{1}
, and assume that
s_{1}|_{Z_{0}}
and
s_{0}|_{Z_{1}}
are also regular. Then:

(i)

the induced isomorphisms

\langle L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1}|_{Z_{0}},L_{2}|_{Z_{0}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}}\rangle=\langle L_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle

and

\langle L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1},L_{0},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle

=\langle L_{0}|_{Z_{1}},L_{2}|_{Z_{1}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{1}}\rangle=\langle L_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle

coincide;

(ii)

setting for brevity
T:=dd^{c}\phi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}
, we have

\int\log|s_{0}|_{\phi_{0}}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int\log|s_{0}|_{\phi_{0}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\int\log|s_{1}|_{\phi_{1}}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{0}}-\int\log|s_{1}|_{\phi_{1}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}.

When
X
,
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
are smooth over an Archimedean field
K
and the metrics are smooth, the result is a special case
[
Elk89
, Lemma I.1.2]
. The proof we propose here follows standard monotone regularization arguments in pluripotential theory (compare for instance
[
Dem
, Proposition III.4.9]
).

Proof.

To prove (i), recall from Theorem
A.22
that the restriction isomorphism

\langle L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1}|_{Z_{0}},L_{2}|_{Z_{0}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}}\rangle

is induced by (
8.6
) and the restriction isomorphism

\lambda(L)-\lambda(L-Z_{0})=\lambda_{Z_{0}}(L|_{Z_{0}}),

valid for any line bundle
L
on
X
. Thus

\langle L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},L_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle

is induced by

\lambda(L)-\lambda(L-Z_{0})-\lambda(L-Z_{1})+\lambda(L-Z_{0}-Z_{1})=\lambda_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}(L),

whose symmetry with respect to
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
implies (i).

We turn to (ii). By Lemma
8.8
and multilinearity, we may assume that
\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}
are smooth and semipositive, and also that
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
are smooth dpsh.

For
i=0,1
, set
f_{i}:=\log|s_{i}|_{\phi_{i}}
. As in the proof of Theorem
8.12
, we can choose an (ample) line bundle
H_{i}
, a decreasing sequences of smooth, semipositive metrics
(\psi_{i,j})_{j}
on
H_{i}
and a smooth, semipositive metric
\psi^{\prime}_{i}
such that
f_{i,j}:=\psi_{i,j}-\psi^{\prime}_{i}
converges to
f_{i}
as
j\to\infty
. By integration by parts,

\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int f_{1,j}\,dd^{c}f_{0,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

and we will thus be done if we prove that

(8.10)

\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\to\int f_{0}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int f_{0}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

and

\int f_{1,j}dd^{c}f_{0,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\to\int f_{1}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{0}}-\int f_{1}\,dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

the first of these being enough, by symmetry. Pick indices
j\geq k
. Then
f_{0,j}\leq f_{0,k}
, and hence

\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int f_{0,j}dd^{c}\psi_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}-\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

\leq\int f_{0,k}\,dd^{c}\psi_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}-\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\int f_{0,k}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}+\int(f_{0,k}-f_{0,j})\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}.

Letting first
j\to\infty
, we infer

\limsup_{j}\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\leq\int f_{0,k}\,dd^{c}f_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}+\int(f_{0,k}-f_{0})\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\int f_{0,k}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int f_{0,k}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}+\int(f_{0,k}-f_{0})\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

and letting next
k\to\infty
yields

\limsup_{j}\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\leq\int f_{0}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int f_{0}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

by monotone convergence. For the converse, pick now indices
j\leq k
. Then

\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int f_{1,j}\,dd^{c}f_{0,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int f_{1,j}\,dd^{c}\psi_{0,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}-\int f_{1,j}\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

\geq\int f_{1,k}\,dd^{c}\psi_{0,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}-\int f_{1,j}\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=\int f_{1,k}\,dd^{c}f_{0,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}+\int(f_{1,k}-f_{1,j})\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}f_{1,k}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}+\int(f_{1,k}-f_{1,j})\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}.

As
k\to\infty
,
dd^{c}f_{1,k}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\to dd^{c}f_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}=T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}+dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}
in the sense of currents, while
\int f_{1,k}\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\to\int f_{1}\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}
, by monotone convergence. Thus

\int f_{0,j}dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\geq\int f_{0,j}T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int f_{0,j}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}+\int(f_{1}-f_{1,j})\,dd^{c}\psi^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

and hence

\liminf_{j\to\infty}\int f_{0,j}dd^{c}f_{1,j}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}\geq\int f_{0}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int f_{0}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X},

using again monotone convergence.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.16
.

We argue by induction on
n
. Assume
n=0
, i.e.
X=\spec A
with
A
finite, flat over
K
. We can then pick a trivializing section
s\in\Hnot(X,L_{0})
, and (
8.8
) yields

(8.11)

\log|\langle s\rangle|_{\langle\phi_{0}\rangle}=\int\log|s|_{\phi_{0}}\delta_{X}.

In view of (
8.7
), (
8.11
) defines a metric
\langle\phi_{0}\rangle
on
\langle L_{0}\rangle
iff

(8.12)

\int\log|u|\,\delta_{X}=\log|N_{A/K}(u)|

To see this, we may assume that
A
is local, by Example
8.2
. Then

\int\log|u|\,\delta_{X}=(\dim_{K}A)\log|u(x)|,

with
x
is the unique point of
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, corresponding to the absolute value on the residue field
K^{\prime}
of
A
. A standard computation gives
N_{A/K}(u)=N_{K^{\prime}/K}(u|_{K^{\prime}})^{m}
with
m=\dim_{K^{\prime}}A
the length of
A
(see for instance
[
Mor14
, Lemma 1.16.2]
). It is also well-known that
|N_{K^{\prime}/K}(u)|=|u|_{K^{\prime}}^{[K^{\prime}:K]}=|u(x)|^{[K^{\prime}:K]}
, and (
8.12
) follows since
\dim_{K}A=m[K^{\prime}:K]
.

We now consider a tuple of continuous dpsh metrics on line bundles
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
on a projective
K
-scheme
X
of dimension
n
, and argue basically as in the proof of
[
Elk89
, Théorème I.1.1]
. Given a regular section
s_{0}\in\Hnot(L_{0})
with divisor
Z_{0}
, we force the restriction formula by setting temporarily

(8.13)

\langle(\phi_{0},s_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle:=\langle\phi_{1}|_{Z_{0}},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z_{0}}\rangle-\int\log|s_{0}|_{\phi_{0}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X},

as metrics on
\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1}|_{Z_{0}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}}\rangle
, where the right-hand side is well-defined by induction and Theorem
8.12
. By induction, this pairing is multilinear and symmetric in
(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n})
.

Assume next given a regular section
s_{1}\in\Hnot(L_{1})
, with divisor
Z_{1}
, such that
s_{1}|_{Z_{0}}
is also regular. We are going to show that

(8.14)

\langle(\phi_{0},s_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\langle(\phi_{1},s_{1}),\phi_{0},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle

as metrics on
\langle L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1},L_{0},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle
. We first claim that
s_{0}|_{Z_{1}}
is regular as well. Indeed, the sequence
(s_{0},s_{1})
is regular at each point of
Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}
, and hence so is
(s_{1},s_{0})
, by general properties of regular sequences in a Noetherian local ring. This means that
s_{0}|_{Z_{1}}
is regular at each point of
Z_{0}
, and also trivially at each point not in
Z_{0}=\mathrm{div}(s_{0})
, proving the claim. By induction, the restriction formula applies to
Z_{0}
, and yields

\langle\phi_{1}|_{Z_{0}},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z_{0}}\rangle=\langle\phi_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle-\int\log|s_{1}|_{\phi_{1}}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{0}}

with
T:=dd^{c}\phi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}
. Using the commutativity property of Lemma
8.17
, we infer

\langle(\phi_{0},s_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle

=\langle\phi_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle-\int\log|s_{1}|_{\phi_{1}}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{0}}-\int\log|s_{0}|_{\phi_{0}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\langle\phi_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle-\int\log|s_{0}|_{\phi_{0}}\,T\wedge\delta_{Z_{1}}-\int\log|s_{1}|_{\phi_{1}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\langle\phi_{0}|_{Z_{1}},\phi_{2}|_{Z_{1}},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z_{1}}\rangle-\int\log|s_{1}|_{\phi_{1}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{0}\wedge T\wedge\delta_{X}

=\langle(\phi_{1},s_{1}),\phi_{0},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle

as metrics on

\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{2}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{0}\cap Z_{1}}\rangle=\langle L_{0}|_{Z_{1}},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z_{1}}\rangle=\langle L_{1},L_{0},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle,

using again induction, which proves (
8.14
).

Assume now that
L_{0}
admits two regular sections
s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}\in\Hnot(L_{0})
, with divisors
Z_{0},Z^{\prime}_{0}
. We claim that

\langle(\phi_{0},s_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\langle(\phi_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle

as metrics on
\langle L_{0}.\dots,L_{n}\rangle
. By linearity with respect to
\phi_{1}
, we may assume that
L_{1}
is ample. For
m\gg 1
, we then find a regular section
s_{1}\in\Hnot(mL_{1})
such that
s_{1}|_{Z_{0}}
and
s_{1}|_{Z^{\prime}_{0}}
are also regular, since this amounts to requiring that
s_{1}
is nonzero at each of the finitely many associated points of
X
,
Z_{0}
and
Z^{\prime}_{0}
. Applying (
8.14
) twice, we infer

\langle(\phi_{0},s_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=m^{-1}\langle(m\phi_{1},s_{1}),\phi_{0},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\langle(\phi_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle

which proves the claim. Given a tuple of continuous dpsh metrics
\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}
on line bundles
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
such that
L_{0}
admits a regular section, we can thus set

\langle\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle:=\langle(\phi_{0},s_{0}),\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle

for any choice of regular section
s_{0}\in\Hnot(L_{0})
. If
\phi^{\prime}_{0}
is a continuous dpsh metric on another line bundle
L^{\prime}_{0}
admitting a regular section, then
L_{0}+L^{\prime}_{0}
also admits a regular section, and (
8.14
) shows that

\langle\phi_{0}+\phi^{\prime}_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\langle\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle+\langle\phi^{\prime}_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle.

Consider finally a continuous dpsh metric
\phi_{0}
on an arbitrary line bundle
L_{0}
. After adding to
L_{0}
a sufficiently large multiple of an ample line bundle, we can write
L_{0}=M_{0}-M^{\prime}_{0}
with
M_{0},M^{\prime}_{0}
both admitting a regular section. Since
M^{\prime}_{0}
admits a continuous dpsh metric
\psi^{\prime}_{0}
, we get
\phi_{0}=\psi_{0}-\psi^{\prime}_{0}
with
\psi_{0}:=\phi_{0}+\psi^{\prime}_{0}
continuous dpsh on
M_{0}
, and we set

\langle\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle:=\langle\psi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle-\langle\psi^{\prime}_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle.

By the previous additivity property, this is independent of the choice of
\psi_{0},\psi^{\prime}_{0}
, and multiadditive with respect to
\phi_{0}
. By (
8.14
), the pairing
\langle\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle
is symmetric with respect to
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
, and hence with respect to any permutation of
\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}
, being already symmetric with respect to
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
by induction. Finally, the pairing satisfies by construction the restriction formula, and we are done.
∎

8.4.
The non-Archimedean case

We assume in this section that
K
is non-Archimedean, possibly trivially valued. By the change of metric formula (
8.9
), the Deligne pairing is basically determined by its values on model metrics, which are described by the following result.

Theorem 8.18
.

Let
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
be line bundles on a projective
K
-scheme
X
of dimension
n
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
be models of
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
, determined on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, and denote by
\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}
the corresponding model metrics. Then

(8.15)

\langle\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle=\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle}.

Here
\langle\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle
is the Deligne pairing with respect to the flat projective morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\Spec K^{\circ}
, cf. Appendix
A
. Since Deligne pairings commute with base change,
\langle\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle
is a model of
\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle
, and
\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle}
denotes the corresponding model metric.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
n
. Assume first
n=0
, and hence
\mathcal{X}=\spec\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{A}
finite free over
K^{\circ}
. As recalled in Lemma
A.19
, any line bundle on
\mathcal{X}
is trivial in a neighborhood of the special fiber of
\mathcal{X}
, and hence trivial on
\mathcal{X}
. A trivializing section
s\in\Hnot(\mathcal{L}_{0})
induces a trivializing section
\langle s\rangle=N_{\mathcal{X}/K^{\circ}}(s)
of
\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle=N_{\mathcal{X}/K^{\circ}}(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, as well as a trivializing section
s\in\Hnot(X,L_{0})
, such that
|s|_{\phi_{0}}=1
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
. By (
8.11
), we infer

\log|\langle s\rangle|_{\langle\phi_{0}\rangle}=\int\log|s|_{\phi_{0}}\delta_{X}=0,

which is equivalent to
\langle\phi_{0}\rangle=\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle}
.

Let now
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
be line bundle on a projective
K
-scheme
X
of dimension
n
, and
\mathcal{L}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
be models of
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
determined on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. Since any line bundle on
\mathcal{X}
can be written as a difference of ample line bundles, we may assume that
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is ample, by linearity. After passing to a large enough multiple, we assume that there exists a relatively regular section
s\in\Hnot(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, by Proposition
A.15
. Denote by
\mathcal{Z}
its divisor, with generic fiber
Z
. By the restriction property of Deligne pairings on models,
s
induces an isomorphism

\langle\mathcal{L}_{0},\cdots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Z}},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{\mathcal{Z}}\rangle

of models of
\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle=\langle L_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z}\rangle
. By induction, we get

\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}_{0},\cdots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle}=\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Z}},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{\mathcal{Z}}\rangle}=\langle\phi_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,\phi_{n}|_{Z}\rangle

=\langle\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{n}\rangle+\int\log|s|_{\phi_{0}}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X},

using the restriction property of Theorem
8.16
. It remains to show that the right-hand integral vanishes, which follows from the fact that
dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}\wedge\delta_{X}
is supported on
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
(cf. Example
8.11
) together with Lemma
8.19
below.
∎

Lemma 8.19
.

If
s
is a relatively regular section of a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on a model
\mathcal{X}
, then
|s|_{\phi_{\mathcal{L}}}=1
on
\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
.

Proof.

Since
s
doesn’t vanish at the associated points of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
,
s
is nonzero on the reduction
\xi:=\red_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
of any Shilov point
x\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
defined by
\mathcal{X}
. In other words,
s
defines a trivializing section of
\mathcal{L}
on an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\xi
, and we infer
|s|_{\phi_{\mathcal{L}}}\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{U}^{\beth}
, by definition of the model metric
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

Using Theorem
8.18
, we relate Deligne pairings and Gubler’s intersection theory on models
[
Gub98
]
.

Definition 8.20
.

If
D
is a vertical Cartier divisor and
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
are line bundles on a projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, we define their
intersection number
as the real number

(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}):=\phi_{\langle\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}(D),\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle}.

Here the right-hand side is a model function on
\spec K
, identified with its value on the unique point of
\spec K
. Equivalently,
(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n})=v_{K}(f)
for any choice of generator
f
of the free
K^{\circ}
-module

\langle\mathcal{O}(D),\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\rangle\hookrightarrow K.

In particular,
(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n})
belongs to the (additive) value group
\Gamma_{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
.

As a special case of Theorem
8.18
, we have

(8.16)

(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n})=\int\phi_{D}\,dd^{c}\phi_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}\wedge\delta_{X}.

On the other hand, in
[
Gub98
, §3]
Gubler associates to every vertical Cartier divisor
D
on
\mathcal{X}
an
n
-dimensional cycle
\cyc(D)
with coefficients in
\Gamma_{K}
on the finite type
\tilde{K}
-scheme
\mathcal{X}_{s}
, such that the following holds:

(i)

if
\mu:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
is a morphism of projective models, then
\cyc(D)=\mu_{\star}\cyc(\mu^{\star}D)
;

(ii)

if
F/K
is a non-Archimedean extension and
D_{F}
is the pull-back of
D
to the base change of
\mathcal{X}
to
F^{\circ}
, then
\cyc(D_{F})=\cyc(D)_{\widetilde{F}}
;

(iii)

if
\mathcal{X}_{s}
is reduced, every irreducible component
Y
of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
determines a unique Shilov point
x_{Y}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{X})
, and

\cyc(D)=\sum_{Y}\phi_{D}(x_{Y})[Y].

Corollary 8.21
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
be line bundles on a model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, and
D
be a vertical Cartier divisor on
\mathcal{X}
. Then

\left(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}\right)=\left(\cyc(D)\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}\right).

Proof.

By (
8.16
),
\left(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}\right)
is invariant under ground field extension, and we may thus assume that
K
is algebraically closed and nontrivially valued. By multilinearity, we may further assume that
X
is (geometrically) reduced. After replacing
\mathcal{X}
with a higher model, we can arrange for
\mathcal{X}_{s}
to be reduced, by Theorem
4.20
. By (
8.3
), we then have

\left(D\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}\right)=\int\phi_{D}\,dd^{c}\phi_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dd^{c}\phi_{n}

=\sum_{Y}\phi_{D}(x_{Y})\left(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{Y}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{Y}\right)=\left(\cyc(D)\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}\right).

∎

8.5.
General metrics on the norm functor

We extend the previous discussion on metrics on Deligne pairings to the setting of general finite flat morphisms
f:Y\to X
. In this setting, we will write abreviate
N=N_{X/Y}
when there is no risk for ambiguity.

The formula (
8.11
) provides a general definition of a metric
N(\phi)
on
N(L)
, provided we are given a metric
\phi
on
L
. Namely, for
x\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and a section
s
of
H^{0}(L|_{f^{-1}(U)})
for some open neighborhood
U
of
X
such that
U^{\mathrm{an}}
is an open neighborhood of
x
, we define

(8.17)

\log|N(s)|_{N(\phi)}(x)=\int_{X}\log|s|_{\phi}\delta_{[f^{-1}(x)]}.

Here
[f^{-1}(x)]
denotes the fundamental cycle of
f^{-1}(x)
, defined in a way analogous to Example
8.2
. The definition extends in a natural way to
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles.

We have the following proposition.

Proposition 8.22
.

Suppose
f:Y\to X
is a finite flat morphism of projective geometrically reduced
K
-schemes, and
L
an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
Y
. If
\phi
is a continuous psh metric on
L
, then so is
N(\phi)
on
N(L)
.

Proof.

Arguing on the components of
X
and
Y
, we may assume wlog that
f
has constant degree
e>1
. The claim is standard in the Archimedean setting since it is straightforward to verify that the curvature of
N(L)
is the direct image under
f
of the curvature of
L
.

Suppose now that
K
is non-trivially valued and non-Archimedean. As it follows from (
8.17
), the map
\phi\mapsto N(\phi)
is Lipschitz continuous. By Theorem
7.8
we can hence restrict ourselves to the case when
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
, for a nef model
\mathcal{L}
of
L
on a model
\mathcal{Y}
. By multiplicativity of the norm we may assume they are actual line bundles. By Proposition
5.13
(and its proof) we can assume
f
extends to a proper and flat morphism
f:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
of models. Flatness together with properness implies that the fiber dimension is locally constant, and hence
f
is quasi-finite. As
f
is automatically finitely presented these facts taken together imply it is also finite by
[
EGA
, IV.8.11.1]
. We can thus consider the norm of line bundles
\mathcal{L}
which are models of
N(L).

An application of the Riemann–Roch theorem for singular curves shows that
N(\mathcal{L})
is nef if
\mathcal{L}
is. We claim that
N(\phi_{\mathcal{L}})=\phi_{N(\mathcal{L})}
(compare with the first part of the proof of Theorem
8.18
), in which case we conclude that
N(\phi_{\mathcal{L}})
is a nef metric. To prove the claim, pick a point
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
x
, and a trivialization
\tau
of
\mathcal{L}
on
f^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
. Since
|\tau|_{\phi_{\mathcal{L}}}(y^{\prime})=1
for
y^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(x^{\prime})
for
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\beth}
, it follows from definition (
8.17
) that
N(\phi_{\mathcal{L}})
is the model metric induced by
N(\mathcal{L})
.

The trivially valued case can be deduced from the non-trivially valued case, in a straightforward application of Lemma
1.26
and Theorem
7.31
.
∎

Remark 8.23
.

It is possible to prove that the norm functor maps continuous metrics to continuous metrics.

Proposition 8.24
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a finite, flat morphism of geometrically reduced projective
K
-schemes. Suppose we are given an ample line bundle
L
on
Y
with a continuous metric
\phi
. Then
P(f^{\star}\phi)=f^{\star}P(\phi)
. In particular, if
P(\phi)
is continuous, so is
P(f^{\star}\phi)
.

Proof.

In the Archimedean case, this is a special case of
[
BB10
, Proposition 2.9]
. The statement in the trivially valued case follows from the non-Archimedean non-trivially valued one by comparing with a non-trivially valued field extension as in the previous lemma. We will henceforth suppose
K
is non-Archimedean non-trivially valued.

It follows from Proposition
5.13
and Lipschitz continuity of
f^{\star}
that
f^{\star}\left(C^{0}\cap\PSH(L)\right)\subseteq C^{0}\cap\PSH(f^{\star}L)
, from which we infer the inequality
f^{\star}P(\phi)\leq P(f^{\star}\phi)
.

To show the reverse inequality we show that any continuous psh metric
\psi
on
f^{\star}L
with
\psi\leq f^{\star}\phi
admits a majoration
\psi\leq f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}\leq f^{\star}\phi
, where
\psi_{\infty}
is continuous psh. Since
P
is non-decreasing we conclude that

\psi\leq f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}\leq f^{\star}P(\phi)\leq P(f^{\star}\phi)

which implies the statement by taking supremum over all continuous psh metrics
\psi.

∎

Lemma 8.25
.

Suppose that
f:Y\to X
is a finite flat morphism of connected projective geometrically reduced
K
-schemes, of degree
e
, and
\psi
is a continuous psh metric on
f^{\star}L
. Inductively define
\psi_{0}=\psi
and
\psi_{k}=\max\left\{\frac{1}{e}f^{\ast}\phi_{N({\psi_{k-1}})},\psi\right\}
. Then all
\psi_{k}
are continuous psh, and converge uniformly to
f^{\ast}\psi_{\infty}
, where

\psi_{\infty}(x)=\sup_{y^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(x)}\psi(y^{\prime})

is a continuous psh metric on
\frac{1}{e}N(L)
.

Proof.

The fact that all the metrics
\psi_{k}
are continuous psh follows from Proposition
8.22
together with the statement that continuous psh metrics are closed under max. Without loss of generality we suppose that
e>1
. Here we can define

f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}(x)=\sup_{y^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(x)}\left(\psi-f^{\star}\phi\right)(x)+\phi(f(x))

for an auxiliary metric
\phi
on
L
. It is independent of
\phi
, and
\psi_{\infty}
defines a metric on
\frac{1}{e}N(L)
.

By construction we have

0\leq\left(f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}-\psi_{k}\right)(y)\leq f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}(y)-\left[\frac{1}{e}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(f(y))}{\psi_{k-1}}(y^{\prime})\right].

Here the sum is computed with taking the multiplicities of the points into account. For any
x\in X^{\mathrm{an}}
, there is always at least one
y\in f^{-1}(x)
such that
\psi_{k}(y)=\operatorname{max}\{\psi_{k-1},\psi\}(y)=f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}(y)
. It follows that an upper bound
M_{k}
of
f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}-\psi_{k}
can be chosen so that
M_{k}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{e}\right)M_{k-1}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{e}\right)^{k}M_{0}
. We conclude that
\psi_{k}\to f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}
uniformly so that
f^{\star}\psi_{\infty}
is continuous psh, and hence so must
\psi_{\infty}
be.
∎

9.
Asymptotics of relative volumes

This section introduces graded norms and their relative volumes, reviews the basic properties of the Monge–Ampère energy functional, and then proves Theorem A, the main result of this paper.

As before,
K
denotes a complete valued field. Throughout this section,
X
is a
geometrically reduced
6
6

6

If
K
is perfect (e.g. of characteristic
0
), this is equivalent to being reduced.
projective
K
-scheme, and
n:=\dim X
.

9.1.
Relative volumes of graded norms

Let
L
be any line bundle on
X
, and consider the graded
K
-algebra
R=R(X,L)
with graded pieces
R_{m}:=\Hnot(X,mL)
. Set also

N_{m}:=\dim R_{m}=\hnot(X,mL).

Definition 9.1
.

A
graded norm

\|\cdot\|_{\bullet}
on the graded algebra
R
is defined as a sequence of norms
\|\cdot\|_{m}
on the graded pieces
\Hnot(mL)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, which is

(i)

submultiplicative
,
i.e.

\|s\cdot s^{\prime}\|_{m+m^{\prime}}\leq\|s\|_{m}\cdot\|s^{\prime}\|_{m^{\prime}}

for all
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in R_{m}
,
s^{\prime}\in R_{m^{\prime}}
;

(ii)

bounded
,
i.e.

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)=O(m).

for some (hence any) bounded metric
\phi
on
L
.

Example 9.2
.

Each bounded metric
\phi
on
L
determines a
graded supnorm

\|\cdot\|_{\bullet\phi}
, with graded pieces
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
.

Example 9.3
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a model of
L
, then the lattice norms
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}
form a graded sequence, denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\bullet\mathcal{L})}
. Boundedness is a consequence of Theorem
6.4
.

Graded norms are preserved under ground field extension:

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
F/K
be a complete field extension. For any graded norm
\|\cdot\|_{\bullet}
on
R=R(X,L)
, the sequence of ground field extensions
(\|\cdot\|_{m})_{F}
forms a graded norm on
R_{F}=R(X_{F},L_{F})
. Further,

(9.1)

\vol\left((\|\cdot\|_{m})_{F},(\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})_{F}\right)=\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})+o(m^{n+1}).

Proof.

Submultiplicativity of the sequence
(\|\cdot\|_{m})_{F}
follows from directly from Definition
1.24
. To see that the sequence is bounded, pick any continuous metric
\phi
on
L
. By Proposition
1.25
,

\dGI((\|\cdot\|_{m})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F})=\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})=O(m),

and it is thus enough to show that
\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\right)=O(m)
, which follows from Theorem
6.6
. Finally, (
9.1
) is a consequence of Proposition
2.14
combined with the estimate

(9.2)

N_{m}\log N_{m}=O(m^{n}\log m)=o(m^{n+1}),

∎

The next result is basically due to Chen and Maclean
[
CMac15
]
, following the strategy of Witt Nyström
[
WN14
]
, itself finding its roots in the work of Zaharjuta
[
Zah75
]
. For the convenience of the reader, we review the argument below.

Theorem 9.5
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically integral. Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
, and pick two graded norms
\|\cdot\|_{\bullet},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet}
on
R=R(X,L)
. Then
m^{-(n+1)}\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})
admits a limit in
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 9.6
.

The
relative volume
of the graded norms
\|\cdot\|_{\bullet}
,
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet}
is defined as

\vol(\|\cdot\|_{\bullet},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet}):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m}).

Proof of Theorem
9.5
.

Observe first that the result is trivial when
L
is not big. Set indeed
N_{m}:=\hnot(mL)
. By the Lipschitz property of relative volumes (Proposition
2.14
),

\left|\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})\right|\leq N_{m}\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})=O(mN_{m}).

If
L
is not big, then
N_{m}=o(m^{n})
, and hence
m^{-(n+1)}\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})\to 0
.

We henceforth assume that
L
is big. By Lemma
9.4
, we may further pass to a complete field extension and assume that
K
is algebraically closed.

We now follow the approach of
[
WN14
,
CMac15
]
, which combines the strategy of Zaharjuta
[
Zah75
]
, relying on a multivariate version of Fekete’s Lemma on subadditive sequences, with the Okounkov body construction
[
LM09
,
KK12
]
. Pick a regular point
p\in X(K)
, a regular sequence
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
in
\mathcal{O}_{X,p}
, and consider the valuation
\nu:K(X)^{\times}\to(\mathbb{Z}^{n},\mathrm{lex})
defined as follows: by Cohen’s structure theorem, every
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,p}
admits a formal power series expansion
f=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}f_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}
,
f_{\alpha}\in K
, and we set

\nu(f):=\min\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\mid f_{\alpha}\neq 0\},

where the min is understood with respect to the lexicographic order. Note that
\nu
is trivial on
K
, and has center
p
on
X
.

The valuation
\nu
can be naturally evaluated on sections
s\in R_{m}=\Hnot(mL)
, by setting
\nu(s):=\nu(f)
with
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,p}
the function corresponding to
s
in some choice of local trivialization of
L
at
p
(the definition being independent of the choice of trivialization). This induces an
\mathbb{N}^{n}
-filtration on
R_{m}
, with the key property that its graded pieces

\gr^{\alpha}R_{m}=\frac{\{s\in R_{m}\mid\nu(s)\geq\alpha\}}{\{s\in R_{m}\mid\nu(s)>\alpha\}}

have dimension at most
1
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
. Thus

\Gamma_{m}:=\nu\left(R_{m}\setminus\{0\}\right)\subset\mathbb{N}^{n}

is finite, of cardinality
N_{m}=\dim R_{m}
, and
\gr^{\alpha}R_{m}
is one-dimensional for each
\alpha\in\Gamma_{m}
(see for instance
[
Bou14
, Lemme 2.11]
). The fact that
L
is big implies that the semigroup

\Gamma:=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\{m\}\times\Gamma_{m}\right)\subset\mathbb{N}^{n+1}

generates
\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
as a group (cf.
[
Bou14
, Proposition 3.3]
). Further,
\Gamma_{m}
grows linearly with
m
, i.e. there exists
C>0
such that

(9.3)

|\alpha|:=\sum_{i}|\alpha_{i}|\leq Cm\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Gamma_{m}.

Indeed, the closure
\bigcup_{m\geq 1}m^{-1}\Gamma_{m}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a convex body
\Delta(L)
, the
Okounkov body
of
L
with respect to
\nu
.

Next fix a local trivialization
\tau
of
L
at
p
. For each
\alpha\in\Gamma_{m}
, one can find a section
s\in\Hnot(mL)
with Taylor expansion
s=z^{\alpha}+\text{higher order terms}
(in the trivialization
\tau^{m}
). The class of
s
in
\gr^{\alpha}R_{m}
is independent of the choice of
s
, and hence a canonical generator
s_{m,\alpha}
of
\gr^{\alpha}R_{m}
. The norm
\|\cdot\|_{m}
induces a subquotient norm on
\gr^{\alpha}\Hnot(mL)
, characterized by

(9.4)

\|s_{m,\alpha}\|_{m}=\inf\{\|s\|_{m}\mid s\in\Hnot(mL),\,s=z^{\alpha}+\text{higher order terms}\},

This gives rise to a superadditive function
\Phi:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
, defined by

\Phi(m,\alpha):=-\log\|s_{m,\alpha}\|_{m}.

We similarly have a superadditive function
\Phi^{\prime}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
attached to
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet}
, and a repeated application of Proposition
2.14
(vi), combined with the estimate
N_{m}\log N_{m}=o(m^{n+1})
, yields

\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma_{m}}\left(\Phi(m,\alpha)-\Phi^{\prime}(m,\alpha)\right)+o(m^{n+1}),

and hence

\frac{1}{m^{n+1}}\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})=\frac{1}{m^{n}}\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma_{m}}\left(m^{-1}\Phi(m,\alpha)-m^{-1}\Phi^{\prime}(m,\alpha)\right)+o(1).

Now
\left|m^{-1}\Phi(m,\alpha)-m^{-1}\Phi^{\prime}(m,\alpha)\right|\leq m^{-1}\dGI(\|\cdot\|_{m},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{m})=O(1)
and
|\Gamma_{m}|=N_{m}\sim\frac{m^{n}}{n!}\vol(L)
. By the general convergence result of
[
CMac15
, Theorem 4.3]
(see also
[
WN14
, Theorem 1.3]
), it remains to check that

\sup_{m\geq 1,\,\alpha\in\Gamma_{m}}m^{-1}\Phi(m,\alpha)<\infty,

which will then also hold for
\Phi^{\prime}
, by symmetry. By (
9.3
) and (
9.4
), this is equivalent to the existence of a uniform constant
C>0
such that
