(9.5)

\log\|s\|_{m}\geq-C(m+|\alpha|)

for all
s\in R_{m}
with Taylor expansion
s=z^{\alpha}+\text{higher order terms}
. Thanks to the boundedness property of
\|\cdot\|_{\bullet}
, it is enough to check this when
\|\cdot\|_{\bullet}=\|\cdot\|_{\bullet\phi}
is the graded supnorm attached to a continuous metric
\phi
, which we now prove along the lines of
[
WN14
, Lemma 5.4]
. By the
K
-analytic inverse function theorem, the coordinates
(z_{i})
at
p
induce a
K
-analytic isomorphism of an open neighborhood
U
of
p
in
X(K)
with an open polydisc
\mathbb{D}(r)^{n}
in
K^{n}
. The section
s
induces an analytic function
f
on
U
, with
s=f\tau^{m}
, and hence
\log|s|_{m\phi}=\log|f|+m\log|\tau|_{\phi}
. The function
\log|\tau|_{\phi}
being locally bounded, it will be enough to show that any analytic function
f
on
\mathbb{D}(r)^{n}
such that

f=z^{\alpha}+\text{higher order terms}

satisfies
\sup_{\mathbb{D}(r)^{n}}|f|\geq r^{|\alpha|}
, which follows from a repeated application of the maximum principle in one variable.
∎

By (
9.1
), relative volumes are invariant under ground field extension:

Proposition 9.7
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically integral. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\bullet}
,
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be graded norms on
R
, and
F/K
be a complete field extension. Then

\vol\left((\|\cdot\|_{\bullet})_{F},(\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet})_{F}\right)=\vol(\|\cdot\|_{\bullet},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{\bullet}).

9.2.
Relative volumes of metrics

We come back here to the general setting of Section
9
, and thus assume that
X
is geometrically reduced. Using Theorem
9.5
, we prove:

Theorem 9.8
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically reduced, and let
L
be a line bundle on
X
.

(i)

The
volume

\vol(L):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n}}\hnot(mL)

exists in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

(ii)

For any two bounded metrics
\phi,\psi
on
L
, the
relative volume

\vol(L,\phi,\psi):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})

exists in
\mathbb{R}
.

Denote further by
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
the set of top-dimensional irreducible components of
X
, and let
\phi_{\alpha}
,
\psi_{\alpha}
be the pullbacks of
\phi,\psi
to
L_{\alpha}:=L|_{X_{\alpha}}
. Then

(9.6)

\vol(L)=\sum_{\alpha}\vol\left(L_{\alpha}\right)

and

(9.7)

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\sum_{\alpha}\vol(L_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha}).

Lemma 9.9
.

Using the notation of Theorem
9.8
, we have

(9.8)

\hnot(X,mL)=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\hnot(X_{\alpha},mL_{\alpha})+o(m^{n})

and

(9.9)

\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{\alpha}},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi_{\alpha}})+o(m^{n+1}).

Proof.

Set
Y:=\coprod_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
, and denote by
\mu:Y\to X
the canonical morphism. For each
m
, we have an exact sequence

0\to\Hnot(X,mL)\to\Hnot(Y,m\mu^{\star}L)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}\Hnot(X_{\alpha},mL)\to\Hnot(X,\mathcal{F}(mL))

with
\mathcal{F}:=\mu_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{Y}/\mathcal{O}_{X}
. Note that
\mu
is an isomorphism over the complement of
\bigcup_{\alpha\neq\beta}X_{\alpha}\cap X_{\beta}
, which has dimension at most
n-1
. Thus
\supp\mathcal{F}
has dimension at most
n-1
, and hence
\hnot(X,\mathcal{F}(mL))=O(m^{n-1})
, which proves (
9.8
). Under the identification
\Hnot(Y,m\mu^{\star}L)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}\Hnot(X_{\alpha},mL_{\alpha})
, the supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{m\mu^{\star}\phi}
corresponds to
\max_{\alpha}\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{\alpha}}
, and a repeated application of Proposition
2.14
, combined with (
9.2
), yields (
9.9
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.8
.

By (
9.1
), we may assume that
K
is algebraically closed. By Lemma
9.9
, it is enough to prove the result with
X
replaced by any of its top-dimensional irreducible component. We may thus assume that
X
is (geometrically) integral, in which case the result follows from Theorem
9.5
.
∎

We each
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we note the obvious homogeneity property

(9.10)

\vol(mL,m\phi,m\psi)=m^{n+1}\vol(L,\phi,\psi).

As a result, we can make sense of relative volumes of metrics on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
. As usual, we say that
L
is
big
if
\vol(L)>0
.

Corollary 9.10
.

Let
L
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
.

(i)

L
is big iff
L|_{X_{\alpha}}
is big for some top-dimensional irreducible component
X_{\alpha}
of
X
.

(ii)

If
L
is nef, then
\vol(L)=(L^{n})
.

Proof.

By homogeneity, we may assume that
L
is an honest line bundle. (i) is then a direct consequence of (
9.6
). Assume that
L
is nef. Since
X_{\alpha}
is irreducible, it is well-known that the volume of the nef line bundle
L|_{X_{\alpha}}
is given by
\vol(L|_{X_{\alpha}})=(L|_{X_{\alpha}})^{n}=c_{1}(L)^{n}\cdot[X_{\alpha}]
. By (
9.6
), we get

\vol(L)=\sum_{\alpha}c_{1}(L)^{n}\cdot[X_{\alpha}]=c_{1}(L)^{n}\cdot[X]=(L^{n}).

∎

Proposition 9.11
.

The following properties hold for all bounded metrics on a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
:

(i)

cocycle formula:

\vol(L,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})+\vol(L,\phi_{2},\phi_{3})+\vol(L,\phi_{3},\phi_{1})=0
;

(ii)

monotonicity:

\phi\leq\phi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\vol(L,\phi,\psi)\leq\vol(L,\phi^{\prime},\psi)
;

(iii

scaling:

\vol(L,\phi+c,\psi)=\vol(L,\phi,\psi)+\vol(L)c
for
c\in\mathbb{R}
;

(iv)

Lipschitz continuity:

\left|\vol(L,\phi,\psi)-\vol(L,\phi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\right|\leq\vol(L)\left(\sup|\phi-\phi^{\prime}|+\sup|\psi-\psi^{\prime}|\right).

(v)

birational invariance:
for any projective birational morphism
\mu:X^{\prime}\to X
, we have

\vol(\mu^{\star}L,\mu^{\star}\phi,\mu^{\star}\psi)=\vol(L,\phi,\psi);

(vi)

envelopes:
if
L
is semiample, then the psh envelopes
\env(\phi)
,
\env(\psi)
satisfy

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\vol(\env(\phi),\env(\psi)).

In particular, if
L
is not big, i.e.
\vol(L)=0
, then
\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=0
for all bounded metrics on
L
.

Proof.

We may assume that
L
is a line bundle, by homogeneity. Properties (i) and (ii) follow immediately from the analogous properties for relative volumes of norms. Further,
\|\cdot\|_{m(\phi+c)}=e^{-mc}\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
, hence

\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m(\phi+c)},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})=\vol(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})+m\hnot(mL)c,

by Proposition
2.14
. Since
n!\hnot(mL)/m^{n}\to\vol(L)
, this yields (iii), and (iv) is a formal consequence of (i), (ii) and (iii). To see (v), note that the embedding

\Hnot(X,mL)\hookrightarrow\Hnot(X^{\prime},m\mu^{\star}L)

is an isometry with respect to the supnorms. By the projection formula, the quotient

W_{m}:=\Hnot(X^{\prime},m\mu^{\star}L)/\Hnot(X,mL)

injects into
\Hnot(X,\mathcal{F}(mL))
, where
\mathcal{F}:=\mu_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/\mathcal{O}_{X}
. The support of the latter sheaf is contained in the image of the exceptional locus of
\mu
, and hence has dimension at most
n-1
, and hence

\dim W_{m}=o(m^{n})
. By Proposition
2.14
, we infer

\left|\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\mu^{\star}\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\mu^{\star}\psi}\right)-\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi}\right)\right|

\leq(\dim W_{m})\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\mu^{\star}\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\mu^{\star}\psi}\right)+O(N_{m}\log N_{m})

\leq(\dim W_{m})m\sup|\phi-\psi|+O(N_{m}\log N_{m})=o(m^{n+1}),

which implies (v). Assume finally that
L
is semiample. Corollary
7.27
shows that
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}=\|\cdot\|_{m\env(\phi)}
and
\|\cdot\|_{m\psi}=\|\cdot\|_{m\env(\psi)}
for all
m
, hence (vi).

∎

Proposition 9.12
.

Let
\phi,\psi
be
continuous
metrics on
L
,
F/K
a complete field extension, and
\phi_{F},\psi_{F}
the pullbacks of
\phi,\psi
to the base change
L_{F}
. Then

\vol(L_{F},\phi_{F},\psi_{F})=\vol(L,\phi,\psi).

Proof.

By Theorem
6.6
, we have

\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}}\right)=o(m),\,\,\,\,\dGI\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})_{F},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi_{F}}\right)=o(m).

By the Lipschitz property of relative volumes and Lemma
9.4
, we infer

\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{F}},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi_{F}}\right)=\vol\left((\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_{F},(\|\cdot\|_{m\psi})_{F}\right)+o(m^{n+1})

=\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{m\psi}\right)+o(m^{n+1}),

and the result now follows.
∎

9.3.
Monge–Ampère energy

In this section,
X
is geometrically reduced, and
L
is a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
. Since
L
is in particular nef, we have

V:=\vol(L)=(L^{n}),

by Corollary
9.10
.

Definition 9.13
.

Let
\phi,\psi
be continuous psh metrics on
L
. We define the
relative Monge–Ampère energy
7
7

7

Note that the present normalization, which is more convenient for the purpose of this paper, is not uniform accross the literature.
of
\phi,\psi
as

(9.11)

\en(\phi,\psi):=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\int(\phi-\psi)(dd^{c}\phi)^{j}\wedge(dd^{c}\psi)^{n-j}\wedge\delta_{X}.

Recall that
\phi-\psi
is a continuous function on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, which may thus be integrated against each positive Radon measure
(dd^{c}\phi)^{j}\wedge(dd^{c}\psi)^{n-j}\wedge\delta_{X}
. By (
8.9
), we have

(9.12)

\en(\phi,\psi)=\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\langle\phi^{n+1}\rangle-\langle\psi^{n+1}\rangle\right).

Given a continuous psh metric
\psi
, the functional
\phi\mapsto\en(\phi,\psi)
is characterized as the unique antiderivative of the Monge–Ampère operator
\phi\mapsto(dd^{c}\phi)^{n}
that vanishes at
\psi
, in the sense that

(9.13)

\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\en((1-t)\phi+t\phi^{\prime},\psi)=\int_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)(dd^{c}\phi)^{n}

for any two continuous psh metrics
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
, see
[
BJ18a
, §3.8]
.

Proposition 9.14
.

The Monge–Ampère energy satisfies the following properties.

(i)

cocycle formula:
\en(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})+\en(\phi_{2},\phi_{3})+\en(\phi_{3},\phi_{1})=0
;

(ii)

monotonicity:
\phi\leq\phi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\en(\phi,\psi)\leq\en(\phi^{\prime},\psi)
;

(iii)

scaling:
\en(\phi+c,\psi)=\en(\phi,\psi)+Vc
for
c\in\mathbb{R}
;

(iv)

homogeneity:
\en(a\phi,a\psi)=a^{n+1}\en(\phi,\psi)
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

(v)

Lipschitz continuity:

\left|\en(\phi,\psi)-\en(\phi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\right|\leq V\left(\sup|\phi-\phi^{\prime}|+\sup|\psi-\psi^{\prime}|\right).

(vi)

for each complete field extension
F/K
we have

\en(\phi_{F},\psi_{F})=\en(\phi,\psi).

(vii)

birational invariance: for any projective birational morphism
\mu:X^{\prime}\to X
we have

\en(\mu^{\star}\phi,\mu^{\star}\psi)=\en(\phi,\psi).

Proof.

(i) follows from (
9.13
), and (ii)— (iv) follow directly from (
9.11
), using that

\int(dd^{c}\phi)^{j}\wedge(dd^{c}\psi)^{n-j}\wedge\delta_{X}=(L^{n})=V.

(v) is a formal consequence of (ii) and (ii), and (vi) follows from (
9.11
) and the compatibility of mixed Monge–Ampère measures with ground field extension, cf. Proposition
8.3
. Finally, (vii) is a consequence of (
9.11
) and the projection formula
\mu_{\star}\delta_{X^{\prime}}=\delta_{X}
.
∎

9.4.
Proof of Theorem A

The following result corresponds to Theorem A in the introduction. It was first established in
[
BB10
]
when
K
is Archimedean, and in
[
BG+16
]
when
K
is discretely valued.

Theorem 9.15
.

Let
X
be a geometrically reduced projective
K
-scheme, and
L
be a semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
X
. For any two continuous psh metrics
\phi,\psi
on
L
, we then have

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\en(\phi,\psi).

Corollary 9.16
.

Let
\phi,\psi
be arbitrary continuous metrics on
L
. Let
\nu:\tilde{X}\to X
be the normalization morphism, and assume that Conjecture
7.30
holds. Then

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\en\left(\env(\nu^{\star}\phi),\env(\nu^{\star}\psi)\right).

Proof.

By Proposition
9.11
,
\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\vol(\nu^{\star}\phi,\nu^{\star}\psi)=\vol\left(\env(\nu^{\star}\phi),\env(\nu^{\star}\psi)\right)
. Since
\tilde{X}
is normal, Conjecture
7.30
implies that
\env(\nu^{\star}\phi)
and
\env(\nu^{\star}\psi)
are continuous and psh, and Theorem
9.15
yields the result.

∎

Lemma 9.17
.

Assume that
K
is non-Archimedean, and let
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
be models of
L
, with associated model metrics
\phi_{\mathcal{L}},\phi_{\mathcal{M}}
and graded norms
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\bullet\mathcal{L})}
,
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\bullet\mathcal{M})}
(cf. Example
9.3
). Then

\vol\left(L,\phi_{\mathcal{L}},\phi_{\mathcal{M}}\right)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}\right).

Proof.

By Theorem
6.4
, we have

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{\mathcal{L}}},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}\right)=O(1),\,\,\,\,\,\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{\mathcal{M}}},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}\right)=O(1).

By Lipschitz continuity of relative volumes of norms (Proposition
2.14
), this yields

\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{\mathcal{L}}},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_{\mathcal{M}}}\right)=\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}\right)+O(N_{m}),

which implies the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.15
.

We claim that it is enough to prove the result when
K
is algebraically closed and nontrivially valued,
X
is irreducible, and
L
is an ample line bundle. The first condition can be reached by passing to an appropriate complete field extension of
K
, by invariance of relative volumes and the Monge–Ampère energy under ground field extension (Proposition
9.12
and Proposition
9.14
). By Lemma
9.4
, we may further assume that
X
is irreducible and
L
is big. By Lemma
5.5
, there exists a birational morphism
f:X\to Y
and an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
A
, unique up to isomorphism, such that
L=f^{\star}A
and
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, and
\phi,\psi
descend to continuous psh metrics on
A
. By birational invariance of relative volumes (Proposition
9.11
) and of the Monge–Ampère energy (Proposition
9.14
), we may thus replace
L
with
A
and assume that
L
is ample. By homogeneity (
9.10
), we may finally assume that
L
is an honest (ample) line bundle, which concludes the proof of the claim.

Consider first the Archimedean case, i.e.
K=\mathbb{C}
. By birational invariance of relative volumes and of the Monge–Ampère energy, we can replace
X
with a resolution of singularities. Then
X
is smooth,
L
is big and semiample, and the result is then a special case of
[
BB10
, Theorem A]
(which deals with an arbitrary big line bundle).

We now assume that
K
is non-Archimedean. We claim that it is enough to prove Theorem
9.15
when
\phi,\psi
are model metrics determined by ample models
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}
of
L
on some model
\mathcal{X}
. By Theorem
7.8
,
\phi
and
\psi
are uniform limits of model metrics determined by nef
\mathbb{Q}
-models
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
of
L
. Thanks to the Lipschitz continuity of relative volumes (Proposition
9.11
) and of the Monge–Ampère energy (Proposition
9.14
), it is thus enough to prove the result for such model metrics. After pulling-back to a higher model, we can assume that
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
are determined on the same model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
, and also that
L
further extends to an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\mathcal{H}
on
\mathcal{X}
(cf.
[
GM19
, Lemma 4.12]
). Denote by
D,E
the vertical
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{L}=D
,
\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{M}=E
. For each
\delta\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1)
, the
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles

\mathcal{L}_{\delta}:=(1-\delta)\mathcal{L}+\delta\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{L}+\delta D,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{M}_{\delta}:=(1-\delta)\mathcal{M}+\delta\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{M}+\delta E

are ample, and the model metrics
\phi_{\mathcal{L}_{\delta}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta\phi_{D}
,
\phi_{\mathcal{M}_{\delta}}=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta\phi_{E}
they determine converge uniformly to
\phi_{\mathcal{L}},\phi_{\mathcal{M}}
. Replacing
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathcal{L}_{\delta}
,
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
, we may thus assume that
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
are ample on
\mathcal{X}
. After replacing
L
with a multiple, we can finally assume that
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
are honest line bundles, by homogeneity of
\vol
and
\en
, which proves the claim above.

Suppose thus
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\psi=\phi_{\mathcal{M}}
with
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}
ample models of
L
on a model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. By Lemma
9.17
,

(9.14)

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}\right).

By Serre vanishing, the higher cohomology of
m\mathcal{L}
and
m\mathcal{M}
vanishes for
m\gg 1
, and Corollary
A.23
yields Knudsen–Mumford expansions

\det\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})=\frac{m^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\langle\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\rangle+O(m^{n})

and

\det\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})=\frac{m^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\langle\mathcal{M}^{n+1}\rangle+O(m^{n}),

as
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles on
\spec K^{\circ}
. Thus

\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}\right)=\log\frac{\det\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}}{\det\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}}

=\phi_{\det\Hnot(m\mathcal{L})}-\phi_{\det\Hnot(m\mathcal{M})}=\frac{m^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\left(\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\rangle}-\phi_{\langle\mathcal{M}^{n+1}\rangle}\right)+O(m^{n}).

By Theorem
8.18
, we have
\phi_{\langle\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\rangle}=\langle\phi^{n+1}\rangle
and
\phi_{\langle\mathcal{M}^{n+1}\rangle}=\langle\psi^{n+1}\rangle
. We thus get as desired

\vol(L,\phi,\psi)=\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\langle\phi^{n+1}\rangle-\psi^{n+1}\rangle\right)=\en(\phi,\psi),

by (
9.12
).
∎

10.
Transfinite diameter and Fekete points

Following the strategy developed in
[
BB10
,
BBW11
]
in the (complex) Archimedean case, we rely on Theorem
9.15
to show the existence of transfinite diameters, and then use it to establish an equidistribution result for Fekete points, assuming a differentiability property that holds under appropriate assumptions on
K
and
X
, by
[
BFJ15
,
BG+16
,
BJ18a
]
.

As in the previous section,
X
is a geometrically reduced, projective scheme over a complete valued field
K
.

10.1.
Existence of the transfinite diameter

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. Set
N:=\hnot(X,L)
, and define the
Vandermonde embedding

\Psi:\det\Hnot(X,L)\hookrightarrow\Hnot(X^{N},L^{\boxtimes N})

as the composition of the antisymmetrization operator

\begin{split}\det\Hnot(X,L)&\hookrightarrow\Hnot(X,L)^{\otimes N}\\
s_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge s_{N}&\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}}(-1)^{\operatorname{sgn}\sigma}s_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes s_{\sigma(N)}\end{split}

with the canonical isomorphism
\Hnot(X,L)^{\otimes N}\simeq\Hnot(X^{N},L^{\boxtimes N})
. Given a basis
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{N})
of
\Hnot(L)
,
\Psi(s_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{N})
can be more informally written as the Vandermonde (or Slater) determinant

\Psi(s_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{N})(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\det(s_{i}(x_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}.

Definition 10.1
.

Let
\phi
be a continuous metric on
L
, and
\|\cdot\|
be a norm on
\Hnot(L)
. We define the
Vandermonde function
of
\phi
relative to
\|\cdot\|
as

V_{\phi,\|\cdot\|}:=\frac{|\Psi(\omega)|_{\phi^{\boxtimes N}}}{\det\|\omega\|}\in\cz\left(\left(X^{N}\right)^{\mathrm{an}}\right),

and the
diameter of
\phi
relative to
\|\cdot\|
as

\delta\left(\phi,\|\cdot\|\right):=\sup_{\left(X^{N}\right)^{\mathrm{an}}}V_{\phi,\|\cdot\|}=\frac{\|\Psi(\omega)\|_{\phi^{\boxtimes N}}}{\det\|\omega\|}.

Here
\omega
is a generator of
\det\Hnot(L)
, the definition being independent of the choice of
\omega
.

Remark 10.2
.

While the canonical map
(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}\to(X^{\mathrm{an}})^{N}
is of course a homeomorphism in the Archimedean case, it is merely continuous and surjective in general in the non-Archimedean case.

Using Theorem
9.5
, we are going to establish the following existence result for transfinite diameters.

Theorem 10.3
.

For any two continuous metrics
\phi,\psi
on
L
, the
transfinite diameter

\delta_{\infty}\left(\phi,\psi\right):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\delta\left(m\phi,\|\cdot\|_{m\psi}\right)^{n!/m^{n+1}}

exists in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Further,

(10.1)

\log\delta_{\infty}\left(\phi,\psi\right)=\vol(L,\psi,\phi).

Combining (
10.1
) with Theorem
9.15
and Corollary
9.16
, we get:

Corollary 10.4
.

Assume that
L
is semiample, and let
\phi,\psi
be continuous metrics on
L
.

(i)

If
\phi,\psi
are psh, then
\delta_{\infty}(\phi,\psi)=\exp\en(\psi,\phi)
.

(ii)

If Conjecture
7.30
holds, then

\delta_{\infty}(\phi,\psi)=\exp\en(\env(\nu^{\star}\psi),\env(\nu^{\star}\phi)),

with
\nu:\widetilde{X}\to X
the normalization morphism.

For each
m
, denote by

\Psi_{m}:\det\Hnot(mL)\hookrightarrow\Hnot\left((mL)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}\right)

the Vandermonde embedding. Via
\Psi_{m}
, the supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}
restricts to a norm on the line
\det\Hnot(mL)
, which we denote by
\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}
. The key fact leading to Theorem
10.3
is the following estimate.

Lemma 10.5
.

For each
\phi\in\cz(L)
we have

\dGI\left(\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}\right)=o(m^{n+1}).

Observe first that
\dGI\left(\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}\right)
is a Lipschitz continuous function of
\phi\in\cz(L)
, with Lipschitz constant
O(m^{n+1})
. It is thus enough to prove the result for
\phi
in a dense subset of
\cz(L)
. The proof will proceed by comparison with certain pure diagonalizable norms, an
L^{2}
norm in the Archimedean case, and a lattice norm in the non-Archimedean.

Lemma 10.6
.

Assume
K
is Archimedean, pick a continuous metric
\phi
on
L
and a smooth volume form
\mu
on
X
, and denote by
\|\cdot\|_{\mu,\phi}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N},\phi^{\boxtimes N}}
the induced
L^{2}
-norms on
\Hnot(L)
and
\Hnot(L^{\boxtimes N})
. Then

\Psi^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N},\phi^{\boxtimes N}}=\sqrt{N!}\det\|\cdot\|_{\mu,\phi}

as norms on
\det\Hnot(L)
.

Proof.

The statement is equivalent to
[
BB10
, Lemma 5.3]
, and goes as follows. By Fubini, the
L^{2}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N},\phi^{\boxtimes N}}
on
\Hnot(L^{\boxtimes N})
corresponds to the tensor norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu,\phi}^{\otimes N}
under the isomorphism
\Hnot(L^{\boxtimes N})\simeq\Hnot(L)^{\otimes N}
. If
(s_{i})
is an orthonormal basis of
\Hnot(L)
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\mu,\phi}
, then the tensors
s_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes s_{i_{N}}
form an orthonormal basis of
\Hnot(L)^{\otimes N}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\mu,\phi}^{\otimes N}
. This implies that the norm of
s_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{N}
under the anti-symmetrization operator
\det\Hnot(L)\hookrightarrow\Hnot(L)^{\otimes N}
has squared-norm equal to
N!
, and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 10.7
.

Assume
K
is non-Archimedean. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a model of
L
, and
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
,
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{L}^{\boxtimes N}\right)}
be the induced lattice norms on
\Hnot(L)
and
\Hnot\left(L^{\boxtimes N}\right)
. Then

\Psi^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{L}^{\boxtimes N}\right)}=\det\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}.

Proof.

The isomorphism
\Hnot(\mathcal{X}^{N},\mathcal{L}^{\boxtimes N})\simeq\Hnot(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L})^{\otimes N}
shows that
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(\mathcal{L}^{\boxtimes N}\right)}
corresponds to the tensor norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}^{\otimes N}
under the isomorphism
\Hnot\left(L^{\boxtimes N}\right)\simeq\Hnot(L)^{\otimes N}
. On the other hand, if
(s_{i})
is an orthonormal basis of
\Hnot(L)
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
, then the tensors
s_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes s_{i_{N}}
form an orthonormal basis of
\Hnot(L)^{\otimes N}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}^{\otimes N}
, which implies this time that the anti-symmetrization operator
\det\Hnot(L)\hookrightarrow\Hnot(L)^{\otimes N}
is an isometric embedding with respect to
\det\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot(\mathcal{L})}^{\otimes N}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.5
.

Assume first that
K
is Archimedean, and pick a smooth volume form
\mu
. By the Bernstein-Markov inequality
[
BB10
, Lemma 3.2]
, the supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}
and the
L^{2}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu,m\phi}
on
\Hnot(mL)
satisfy

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{\mu,m\phi}\right)=o(m),

and hence

(10.2)

\dGI\left(\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\det\|\cdot\|_{\mu,m\phi}\right)=\left|\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\|\cdot\|_{\mu,m\phi}\right)\right|=o(m^{n+1}),

by Lipschitz continuity of relative volumes. As in
[
BB10
, Step 2, p.378]
, a successive application of the Bernstein–Markov inequality in each variable similarly shows that the induced supnorm
\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}
and
L^{2}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N_{m}},(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}
on
\Hnot((mL)^{\boxtimes N_{m}})
satisfy

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N_{m}},(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}\right)=o(m^{n+1}),

and hence

(10.3)

\dGI\left(\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N_{m}},(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}\right)=o(m^{n+1})

as well. Finally, since
\log\left(N_{m}!\right)=O(m^{n}\log m)=o(m^{n+1})
, Lemma
10.6
yields

\dGI\left(\det\|\cdot\|_{\mu,m\phi},\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{\mu^{N_{m}},(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}\right)=o(m^{n+1}),

which combines with (
10.2
) and (
10.3
) to yield the desired estimate

\dGI\left(\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}\right)=o(m^{n+1}).

Assume now that
K
is non-Archimedean. Arguing as in §
9
, we may assume after ground field extension that
K
is nontrivially valued, so that model metrics are dense in
\cz(L)
. As already noticed,

\dGI\left(\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)

is a Lipschitz continuous function of
\phi\in\cz(L)
, with Lipschitz constant
O(m^{n+1})
; by density, it is thus enough to prove the result when
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
is a model metric, determined by a
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\mathcal{L}
extending
L
on some projective model
\mathcal{X}
of
X
. After replacing
\mathcal{X}
with a higher model, we may assume that
L
also admits a model
\mathcal{M}
determined on
\mathcal{X}
(Lemma
5.8
). As in the proof of Theorem
6.6
, fix
a\geq 1
such that
a\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle, and write
m=qa+r
with
q,r\in\mathbb{N}
and
r<a
. Since
a\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
are line bundles on
\mathcal{X}
, Theorem
6.5
shows the existence of
C>0
independent of
m
such that

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{(qa\phi+r\phi_{\mathcal{M}})^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left((qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})^{\boxtimes N_{m}}\right)}\right)=O(N_{m}).

As
\phi-\phi_{\mathcal{M}}
and
r
are bounded, it follows that

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left((qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})^{\boxtimes N_{m}}\right)}\right)=O(N_{m}),

and hence

(10.4)

\dGI\left(\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left((qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})^{\boxtimes N_{m}}\right)}\right)=O(N_{m})

By Lemma
10.7
, we have

\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left((qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M})^{\boxtimes N_{m}}\right)}=\det\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M}\right)}.

On the other hand, Theorem
6.4
yields

\dGI\left(\|\cdot\|_{qa\phi+r\phi_{\mathcal{M}}},\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{M}\right)}\right)=O(N_{m}),

hence

\dGI\left(\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi},\det\|\cdot\|_{\Hnot\left(qa\mathcal{L}+r\mathcal{H}\right)}\right)=O(N_{m})

by boundedness of
\phi-\phi_{\mathcal{M}}
, and we conclude that

\dGI\left(\Psi_{m}^{\star}\|\cdot\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}},\det\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)=O(N_{m})

when
\phi
is a model metric.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.3
.

For any choice of generator
\omega\in\det\Hnot(mL)
, we have

\sup_{\left(X^{N}\right)^{\mathrm{an}}}V_{m\phi,\|\cdot\|_{m\psi}}=\frac{\|\Psi_{m}(\omega)\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}}{\det\|\omega\|_{m\psi}}

=\left(\frac{\|\Psi_{m}(\omega)\|_{(m\phi)^{\boxtimes N_{m}}}}{\det\|\omega\|_{m\phi}}\right)\left(\frac{\det\|\omega\|_{m\phi}}{\det\|\omega\|_{m\psi}}\right).

By Lemma
10.5
, we infer

\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\log\delta(m\phi,\|\cdot\|_{m})=\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\vol\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\psi},\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}\right)+o(1),

and hence

\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\log\delta(m\phi,\|\cdot\|_{m})\to\vol(\|\cdot\|_{\bullet},\|\cdot\|_{\bullet\phi}).

∎

10.2.
Equidistribution of Fekete points

Definition 10.8
.

Let
\phi\in\cz(L)
be a continuous metric on a line bundle
L
. A
Fekete configuration
for
\phi
is a point
P\in(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}
such that

\|\Psi(\omega)\|_{\phi^{\boxtimes N}}=\sup_{(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}}|\Psi(\omega)|_{\phi^{\boxtimes N}}

is achieved at
P
for some, hence any, generator
\omega\in\det\Hnot(L)
.

In terms of the Vandermonde function
V_{\phi,\|\cdot\|}
relative to any given norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\Hnot(L)
,
P\in(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}
is a Fekete configuration iff

\sup_{(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}}V_{\phi,\|\cdot\|}=V_{\phi,\|\cdot\|}(P).

Our final result is an equidistribution result for Fekete configurations of
m\phi
as
m\to\infty
, first established in the Archimedean case in
[
BBW11
]
. In order to cover various cases in one stroke, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 10.9
.

Let
L
be a semiample line bundle, and
\phi
be a continuous metric on
L
. For brevity, we shall say that
differentiability holds at
\phi
if:

(i)

the psh envelope
\env(\phi)
is continuous (hence psh);

(ii)

for all
f\in\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
we have

(10.5)

\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\vol(L,\phi+tf,\phi)=\int f\,\left(dd^{c}\env(\phi)\right)^{n}\wedge\delta_{X}.

Differentiability is known to hold at all continuous metrics when
X
is smooth,
L
is ample, and one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

K
is Archimedean
[
BB10
]
;

•

K
is non-Archimedean, trivially or discretely valued, of residue characteristic zero
[
BFJ16
,
BJ18a
]
;

•

K
is discretely valued of characteristic
p
,
(X,L)
is defined over a function field of
transcendence degree
d
, and resolution of singularities is assumed in dimension
n+d

[
GJKM19
,
BG+16
]
.

Theorem 10.10
.

Let
L
be a big and semiample line bundle, of volume
V:=\vol(L)=(L^{n})
. Let
\phi
be a continuous metric on
L
, and assume that differentiability holds at
\phi
. For each
m\gg 1
, pick a Fekete configuration
P_{m}\in(X^{N_{m}})^{\mathrm{an}}
for
m\phi
. Then
P_{m}
equidistributes to the probability measure

\mu_{\phi}:=V^{-1}\left(dd^{c}\env(\phi)\right)^{n}\wedge\delta_{X}.

In particular, Fekete configurations for
m\phi
become asymptotically unique as
m\to\infty
. Equidistribution means that

\int_{X}f\,\delta_{P_{m}}\to\int_{X}f\,\mu_{\phi}

for all
f\in\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
. Here
\delta_{P}
denotes the averaging measure over
P\in(X^{N})^{\mathrm{an}}
, or rather its image in
(X^{\mathrm{an}})^{N}
(see Remark
10.2
).

The proof of Theorem
10.10
follows the strategy of
[
BBW11
]
, itself inspired by a variational argument due to Szpiro–Ullmo–Zhang
[
SUZ97
]
.

Proof.

Set for any continuous metric
\psi

F_{m}(\psi):=-\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\log V_{m\psi,\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}}(P_{m}).

Then

F_{m}(\psi)\geq-\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}\log\delta(m\psi,\|\cdot\|_{m\phi}),

with equality for
\psi=\phi
, since
P_{m}
is a Fekete configuration for
m\phi
. By Theorem
10.3
, we infer

\liminf_{m\to\infty}F_{m}(\psi)\geq\vol(L,\psi,\phi),\,\,\,\,\,\,\lim_{m\to\infty}F_{m}(\phi)=\vol(L,\phi,\phi)=0,

and hence

(10.6)

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\left(F_{m}(\psi)-F_{m}(\phi)\right)\geq\vol(L,\psi,\phi).

For each
f\in\cz(X^{\mathrm{an}})
, observe that

F_{m}(\phi+f)=F_{m}(\phi)+c_{m}\int_{X}f\,\delta_{P_{m}}

with

c_{m}:=\frac{n!}{m^{n}}N_{m}\to\vol(L)=V.

By (
10.6
), we get

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\int f\,\delta_{P_{m}}\geq V^{-1}\vol(L,\phi+f,\phi).

Replacing
f
with
tf
,
t>0
, and using the differentiability property (
10.5
), we infer

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\int f\,\delta_{P_{m}}\geq V^{-1}\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}t^{-1}\vol(L,\phi+tf,\phi)=\int f\,\mu_{\phi}.

Applying this to
-f
in place of
f
, we conclude as desired
\lim_{m\to\infty}\int f\,\delta_{P_{m}}=\int f\,\mu_{\phi}
.
∎

10.3.
A pullback formula for transfinite diameters

In this section, we assume that
L
is an ample line bundle on
X
, and consider a
polarized endomorphism

f
of
(X,L)
, i.e. a morphism
f:X\to X
together with the data of an isomorphism
f^{\star}L\simeq dL
for some positive integer
d>1
. Since
f^{\star}L
is ample and
(f^{\star}L)^{n}=d^{n}(L^{n})
,
f
is finite, of degree
d^{n}
. By Theorem
A.22
, we thus have a canonical isomorphism

\langle(f^{\star}L)^{n+1}\rangle\simeq d^{n}\langle L^{n+1}\rangle,

which combines with the given isomorphism
f^{\star}L\simeq dL
to yield

d^{n+1}\langle L^{n+1}\rangle\simeq d^{n}\langle L^{n+1}\rangle.

This defines a canonical section

(10.7)

R_{f}\in d^{n}(d-1)\langle L^{n+1}\rangle,

which we call the
resultant section
(see Corollary
10.16
below for the choice of terminology).

On the other hand, the map
\cz(L)\to\cz(L)
defined by
\phi\mapsto d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi
, being
1/d
-Lipschitz continuous, admits a unique fixed point
\phi_{f}
, the
equilibrium metric
of
f
. For any choice of Fubini–Study metric
\phi
, the metrics
d^{-j}(f^{j})^{\star}\phi
are Fubini–Study as well, and they converge uniformly to
\phi_{f}
, which is thus psh.

Example 10.11
.

For any
d\geq 2
, the equilibrium metric of the polarized endomorphism
f
of
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
induced by
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto(x_{0}^{d}:\dots:x_{n}^{d})
is
\phi_{f}=\max_{i}\log|x_{i}|
.

Lemma 10.12
.

The resultant
R_{f}\in d^{n}(d-1)\langle L^{n+1}\rangle
has norm
1
with respect to the induced metric
d^{n}(d-1)\langle\phi_{f}^{n+1}\rangle
.

Proof.

For any continuous psh metric
\phi
on
L
, the isomorphism
\langle(f^{\star}L)^{n+1}\rangle\simeq d^{n}\langle L^{n+1}\rangle
is an isometry with respect to
\langle(f^{\star}\phi)^{n+1}\rangle
and
d^{n}\langle\phi^{n+1}\rangle
. By definition of
\phi_{f}
, the isomorphism
f^{\star}L\simeq dL
is an isometry with respect to
f^{\star}\phi_{f}
and
d\phi_{f}
. It follows that the induced isomorphism
d^{n}(d-1)\langle L^{n+1}\rangle\simeq K
is an isometry with respect to
d^{n}(d-1)\langle\phi_{f}^{n+1}\rangle
and the canonical metric on
K
, hence the result.
∎

We now get the following pull-back formula for the transfinite diameter, which generalizes
[
DMR
]
,
[
BB10
, §6.3]
in view of Corollary
10.16
below.

Theorem 10.13
.

For each continuous psh metric
\psi
, and let
c(f,\psi)
be the positive constant such that

\log c(f,\psi)=-\frac{1}{(n+1)d^{n+1}}\log|R_{f}|_{d^{n}(d-1)\langle\psi^{n+1}\rangle}.

Then

\delta_{\infty}(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi,\psi)=c(f,\psi)\,\delta_{\infty}(\phi,\psi)^{1/d},

all continuous psh metrics
\phi
.

Lemma 10.14
.

For any two continuous psh metrics
\phi,\psi
on
L
we have

\en\left(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi,d^{-1}f^{\star}\psi\right)=d^{-1}\en(\phi,\psi).

Proof.

By (
9.11
),

\en\left(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi,d^{-1}f^{\star}\psi\right)

=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\int\left(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi-d^{-1}f^{\star}\psi\right)\left(dd^{c}(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi)\right)^{i}\wedge\left(dd^{c}(d^{-1}f^{\star}\psi)\right)^{n-i}\wedge\delta_{X}

=\frac{1}{(n+1)d^{n+1}}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\int f^{\star}\left((\phi-\psi)\,\left(dd^{c}\phi\right)^{i}\wedge\left(dd^{c}\psi\right)^{n-i}\right)\wedge\delta_{X}

=\frac{d^{n}}{(n+1)d^{n+1}}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\int(\phi-\psi)\,\left(dd^{c}\phi\right)^{i}\wedge\left(dd^{c}\psi\right)^{n-i}\wedge\delta_{X}=d^{-1}\en(\phi,\psi)

since
f_{\star}\delta_{X}=d^{n}\delta_{X}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.13
.

Since
d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi_{f}=\phi_{f}
, Corollary
10.4
and the cocycle formula for
\en
yield

\log\delta_{\infty}(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi,\psi)=\en\left(\psi,d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi\right)=\en(\psi,\phi_{f})+\en\left(d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi_{f},d^{-1}f^{\star}\phi\right)

=\en(\psi,\phi_{f})+d^{-1}\en(\phi_{f},\phi)=(1-d^{-1})\en(\phi_{f},\psi)+d^{-1}\log\delta_{\infty}(\phi,\psi).

On the other hand, Lemma
10.12
yields

\log|R_{f}|_{d^{n}(d-1)\langle\psi^{n+1}\rangle}=d^{n}(d-1)\left(\langle\phi_{f}^{n+1}\rangle-\langle\psi^{n+1}\rangle\right)=d^{n}(d-1)(n+1)\en(\phi_{f},\psi),

and hence

\frac{1}{(n+1)d^{n+1}}\log|R_{f}|_{d^{n}(d-1)\langle\psi^{n+1}\rangle}=(1-d^{-1})\en(\phi_{f},\psi),

and we are done.
∎

10.4.
The case toric varieties

We now illustrate the previous pull-back formula in the toric case. We assume that
(X,L)
is a smooth projective polarized toric variety with respect to a split torus
T\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
with character lattice
M=\Hom(T,\mathbb{G}_{m})
, which thus corresponds to a Delzant polytope
\Delta\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
.

For each integer
d\geq 2
, multiplication by
d
on the dual of
M
induces a polarized endomorphism
m_{d}
of
(X,L)
, defining an equilibrium metric
\phi_{d}
on
L
and a resultant section
R_{d}\in d^{n}(d-1)\langle L^{n+1}\rangle
.
The next lemma describes the moduli space of polarized endomorphisms of degree
d
of
(X,L)
.

Lemma 10.15
.

Set
N=\dim\Hnot(L)=\#(M\cap\Delta)
. The space of polarized morphisms of degree
d
of
(X,L)
is parametrized by a Zariski open subset of

\mathbb{P}\left(\Hom\left(\Hnot(L),\Hnot(dL)\right)\right)\simeq\mathbb{P}\left(\Hnot(dL)^{N}\right)

whose complement
Z
has codimension
N-n
. In particular,
Z
has codimension greater than
1
unless
(X,L)\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
.

Proof.

Since
X
is smooth,
R(X,L)
is generated in degree one, the data of a polarized endomorphism of
(X,L)
of degree
d
is equivalent to that of a linear map
\Hnot(L)\to\Hnot(dL)
whose image is basepoint free. As a result, the space of polarized endomorphisms of degree
d
is isomorphic to the complement in
\mathbb{P}\left(\Hnot(dL)^{N}\right)
of the projection
Z
of the incidence variety

I=\left\{\left([s_{1}:\dots:s_{N}],x\right)\in\mathbb{P}\left(\Hnot(dL)^{N}\right)\times X,s_{1}(x)=\dots=s_{N}(x)=0\right\}

Since
dL
is basepoint free, the elements of
\Hnot(dL)
vanishing at a given closed point
x\in X
is a hyperplane, and it follows that
\dim I=n-1+N(N_{d}-1)
. We claim that the restriction to
I
of the first projection
\Hnot(dL)^{N}\times X\to\Hnot(dL)^{N}
is generically finite, which will imply
\codim Z=NN_{d}-1-\dim I=N-n
. Indeed, if
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in\Hnot(dL)
is a regular sequence of sections, the fiber of
I
over the
N
-tuple
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{n})\in\Hnot(dL)^{N}
is finite.

The last point of the lemma follows from the embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}\Hnot(L)
, which is an isomorphism iff
N-n=1
.
∎

In the case
N=n-1
, i.e.
(X,L)\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
, any polarized morphism of degree
d
is given by
z\mapsto[f_{0}(z):\ldots:f_{n}(z)]
for homogenous polynomials
f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}
, of degree
d
, without common zeros, and the locus
Z\subset\mathbb{P}\left(\Hnot(L)^{n+1}\right)
is an irreducible divisor of degree
(n+1)d^{n}
. As a result, it is defined by a unique polynomial
\operatorname{Res}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{n})
of degree
(n+1)d^{n}
in the coefficients of the
f_{i}
and normalized by
\operatorname{Res}(x_{0}^{d},\ldots,x_{n}^{d})=1
, cf.
[
GKZ94
, Ch. 13]
.

Corollary 10.16
.

Let
f
be a polarized morphism of degree
d
of the smooth polarized toric variety
(X,L)
.

•

If
(X,L)\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
, then
R_{f}=\operatorname{Res}(f)R_{d}
;

•

if not, then
R_{f}=R_{d}
.

Proof.

If
(X,L)\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
, we can restrict along the hyperplane determined by
x_{n}=0
and inductively compare how the Deligne products and the resultants change. In the case of the resultant, the transformation is described by the Poisson formula
[
GKZ94
, Ch. 13, Theorem 1.2]
, and the Deligne products transform accordingly. This shows they are equal up to some constant, and the constant is equal to 1 by evaluating at the polarized endomorphism
m_{d}
.

If
(X,L)
is not isomorphic to
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
, the previous lemma shows that the space of polarized degree
d
endomorphisms of
(X,L)
is isomorphic to an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{P}\left(\Hnot(dL)^{N}\right)
whose complement
Z
has codimension at least
2
. The map
f\mapsto R_{f}/R_{d}
defines a morphism
U\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which is thus constant by normality and properness of
\mathbb{P}\left(\Hnot(dL)^{N}\right)
, and hence equal to
1
by evaluating at
f=m_{d}
.
∎

Appendix A
Determinant of cohomology and Deligne pairings

The goal of this Appendix is to discuss a generalization to arbitrary schemes of results of Knudsen–Mumford
[
KM76
]
, Deligne
[
Del87
]
, Elkik
[
Elk89
]
, Munoz-Garcia
[
MG00
]
and Ducrot
[
Duc05
]
, which provide a rough Riemann–Roch theorem for the determinant of cohomology.

A.1.
Discussion of the results

For a projective scheme
X
over a field
K
, the
determinant of cohomology
of a line bundle
L
is the line (i.e. one-dimensional
K
-vector space)

\lambda(L):=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\det H^{i}(X,L),

where we use additive notation for tensor products of lines. If
\pi:X\to Y
is now a flat projective morphism of locally Noetherian schemes, it was shown by Knudsen and Mumford in
[
KM76
]
that the fiberwise determinant of cohomology of a line bundle
L
on
X
glues together to define a line bundle
\lambda_{X/Y}(L)
on
Y
. Indeed, the derived direct image
R\pi_{\star}L
is a perfect complex, i.e. locally on
Y
there exists a bounded complex
E^{\bullet}
of vector bundles with
R^{q}\pi_{\star}L
as its
q
-th cohomology sheaf, and the determinant of cohomology of
L
can then be locally described as

\lambda_{X/Y}(L)=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\det E_{i}.

Denoting by
n
the relative dimension of
\pi
, the main result in F. Ducrot’s paper
[
Duc05
]
implies that the functor
\lambda_{X/Y}:\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
so defined between the Picard categories of line bundles on
X
and
Y
admits a unique polynomial structure of degree
n+1
compatible with base change and restriction to a relative Cartier divisor (see §
A.8
for a precise statement). This result recovers in one stroke the construction of Deligne pairings
[
Elk90
,
MG00
]
and the Knudsen–Mumford expansion
[
KM76
]
. Indeed, it implies that the
(n+1)
-st iterated difference

\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle_{X/Y}:=\sum_{I\subset\{0,\ldots,n\}}(-1)^{n+1-|I|}\lambda_{X/Y}\left(\sum_{i\in I}L_{i}\right)

defines a multi-additive symmetric functor
\mathcal{P}(X)^{n+1}\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
, the
Deligne pairing
, and that we have for each
L\in\mathcal{P}(X)
an expansion

\lambda_{X/Y}(mL)=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}{m+i\choose i}M_{i}

as a function of
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, the coefficients being line bundles
M_{i}
on
Y
, and
M_{n+1}=\langle L^{n+1}\rangle_{X/Y}
. Whenever a Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch theorem is available, we infer

c_{1}\left(\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle_{X/Y}\right)=\pi_{\star}(c_{1}(L_{0})\cdot\ldots\cdot c_{1}(L_{n})\rangle,

so that Deligne pairings lift the natural push-forward operation on the right-hand side to the level of line bundles.

In the main body of the present paper, a version of these results in the possibly non-Noetherian setting of models over the valuation ring of a complete non-Archimedean field is required. The purpose of this appendix is to summarize the results leading to a generalization of the above results for arbitrary schemes.

A.2.
Polynomial maps

In order to motivate the definitions in §
A.3
, we briefly recall some background on polynomial maps and difference calculus. It is well-known that a map
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
is polynomial of degree (at most)
n
if and only if it admits an expansion

f(m)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{m+i\choose i}b_{i}

with coefficients
b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. More generally, a map
f:A\to B
between commutative groups is said to be
polynomial of degree
n
if for any given
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in A
we have an expansion

f(m_{1}x_{1}+\dots+m_{r}x_{r})=\sum_{0\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\leq n}{m_{1}+i_{1}\choose i_{1}}\dots{m_{r}+i_{r}\choose i_{r}}b_{i_{1}\dots i_{r}}

for all
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, with coefficients
b_{i_{1}\dots i_{r}}\in B
.

Polynomiality can be characterized in terms of difference calculus. For a map
f:\mathbb{Z}\to B
, define the difference
\Delta f:\mathbb{Z}\to B
by

(\Delta f)(m):=f(m+1)-f(m).

Then

\Delta{m+i\choose i}={m+i-1\choose i-1},

which can be used to show by induction on
n
that
f:\mathbb{Z}\to B
is a polynomial map of degree
n
if and only if
\Delta^{n+1}f=0
. For a map
f:\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to B
, one can introduce partial difference operators
\Delta_{i}
, and
f
is polynomial of degree
n
if and only if
\Delta^{\alpha}f=0
for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
of length
|\alpha|:=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=n+1
, where we have set
\Delta^{\alpha}=\Delta_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\Delta_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}
.

Consider now a map
f:A\to B
between commutative groups. Mimicking differential calculus, one defines the
difference

\delta_{x}f:A\to B
of
f
at
x\in A
by setting

(\delta_{x}f)(y)=f(x+y)-f(x),

and the
k
-th iterated difference

\delta^{k}_{x}f:A^{k}\to B
by

(\delta^{k}_{x}f)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}):=\delta_{x}\left(y\mapsto(\delta^{k-1}_{y}f)(x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\right)(x_{1})

=(\delta^{k-1}_{x+x_{1}}f)(x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})-(\delta^{k-1}_{x}f)(x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}).

The map
\delta^{k}_{x}f:A^{k}\to B
so defined is symmetric, as follows from the explicit expression

(A.1)

(\delta^{k}_{x}f)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\sum_{I\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}}(-1)^{k-|I|}f\left(x+\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\right).

Given
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in A
, the map
g:\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to B
defined by
g(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})=f(\sum_{i}m_{i}x_{i})
satisfies

(A.2)

(\Delta^{\alpha}g)(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})=(\delta^{|\alpha|}_{\sum_{i}m_{i}x_{i}}f)(x_{1}^{a_{1}},\ldots,x_{r}^{a_{r}})

for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
.

Using this, we conclude that
f:A\to B
is polynomial of degree
n
if and only if
\delta^{n+1}_{x}f=0
for all
x\in A
. It is in fact enough to check this condition for
x=0
. Indeed, the operator
\delta^{k}:=\delta^{k}_{0}
determines all
\delta^{k}_{x}
by

(A.3)

(\delta^{k}_{x}f)(x_{1},\dots)=(\delta^{k}f)(x+x_{1},\dots)-(\delta^{k}f)(x,\dots).

It further satisfies

(\delta^{k+1}f)(x_{1},y_{1},\dots)=(\delta^{k}f)(x_{1}+y_{1},\dots)-(\delta^{k}f)(x_{1},\dots)-(\delta^{k}f)(y_{1},\dots),

and we thus see that
f
is polynomial of degree
n
if and only if
\delta^{n}f
is multi-additive. Note also that
\delta^{k}
can be understood as a polarization operator, in the sense that
\delta^{k}(L(x^{k}))=k!L
for any symmetric multi-additive map
L:A^{k}\to B
; we can thus view the multi-additive map
\delta^{n}f:A^{n}\to B
associated to a polynomial map
f:A\to B
of degree
n
as the polarization of its degree
n
part.

Example A.1
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional projective scheme over a field
K
, with structure morphism
\pi:X\to\spec K
, and pick a line bundle
L
on
X
. The
Euler characteristic

\chi(L):=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\dim_{K}H^{i}(X,L)

only depends on the class of
L
in the Picard group
\Pic(X)
, and Snapper’s theorem implies that
\chi:\Pic(X)\to\mathbb{Z}
is a polynomial map of degree
n
, with degree
n
polarization given by the intersection pairing,
i.e.

(\delta^{n}\chi)(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})=(L_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{n}):=\deg\pi_{\star}\left(c_{1}(L_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot c_{1}(L_{n})\cdot[X]\right).

For later use, we note:

Lemma A.2
.

Let
f:A\to B
is a polynomial map of degree
n
, and set for all
0\leq i\leq n

f_{n,i}(x):=\sum_{k=i}^{n}(-1)^{k-i}{k\choose i}(\delta^{k}f)(x^{k}).

Then
f(mx)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{m+i\choose i}f_{n,i}(x)
for all
x\in A
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Since
f
is polynomial of degree
n
, we have

g(m):=f(mx)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{m+i\choose i}b_{i}

for some
b_{i}\in B
. Since
\Delta_{0}^{k}{m+i\choose i}=1
for
k\leq i
and
0
otherwise, (
A.2
) yields

(\delta^{k}f)(x^{k})=\Delta_{0}^{k}g=\sum_{i\geq k}b_{i},

and hence
b_{i}=f_{n,i}(x)
.
∎

A.3.
Polynomial functors

A
commutative Picard category
is a ’category version’ of a commutative group, or more precisely a symmetric monoidal groupoid where every object is invertible with respect to the monoidal structure. In other words, it is a category
\mathcal{A}
in which all arrows are isomorphisms, together with an additivity functor and functorial associativity and commutativity isomorphisms satisfying the expected compatibility conditions, and such that for any object
x
the endofunctors
y\mapsto x+y
and
y\mapsto y+x
are autoequivalences.

These axioms imply the existence of a neutral object
0
and of an inverse
-x
for each object
x
, both unique up to unique isomorphism. The sum
\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}
of a finite family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
is well-defined up to unique isomorphism, and satisfies the expected associativity rules. A commutative Picard category
\mathcal{A}
is
strictly commutative
if the commutativity isomorphism induces the identity on
x+x
for each
x
, in which case
x
and
-x
can be contracted within a sum without raising any sign issue.

In practice for us,
\mathcal{A}
will be the category of line bundles or
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles on a given scheme, and isomorphisms between them, both of which are strictly commutative Picard categories. Note that a commutative group can also be viewed as a strictly commutative Picard category.

In what follows,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are strictly commutative Picard categories. An
additive functor

F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a functor equipped with a functorial additivity isomorphism
F(x+y)\simeq F(x)+F(y)
which is
commutative
, expressed by the commutativity of the induced diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.39813pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.39813pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(x+y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.39813pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.39813pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(x)+F(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.5949pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-22.39813pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(y+x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.39813pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.39813pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(y)+F(x)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

and
associative
, i.e. the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{F((x+y)+z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(x+y)+F(z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(F(x)+F(y)\right)+F(z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(x+(y+z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces F(x)+F(y+z)}
\textstyle{F(x)+\left(F(y)+F(z)\right).}

These conditions then yield a consistent system of functorial additivity isomorphisms
F(\sum_{i\in I}x_{i})\simeq\sum_{i\in I}F(x_{i})
for all finite families
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are small, the associated sets of isomorphism classes
A
and
B
are commutative groups, and
F
induces a homomorphism
F:A\to B
.

A
multi-additive functor

F:\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
is defined as a functor equipped with functorial commutative and associative additivity data in each variable, such that expanding out sums in the variables does not depend on the order the operation is performed. A
symmetric functor

F:\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
is a functor equipped with symmetry isomorphisms

F(x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(n)})\simeq F(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

for each permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{r}
, compatible with the group law on
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
, and a
symmetric, multi-additive
functor has both structures, with the expected compatibility condition.

Define the
k
-th iterated difference
at an object
x
in
\mathcal{A}
of a functor
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
as the symmetric functor
\delta^{k}_{x}F:\mathcal{A}^{k}\to\mathcal{B}
defined by setting

(\delta^{k}_{x}F)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}):=\sum_{I\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}}(-1)^{k-|I|}F\left(x+\sum_{j\in I}x_{j}\right),

For
x=0
, we simply set
\delta^{k}:=\delta^{k}_{0}
. Recalling from §
A.2
that a map
f
between commutative groups is polynomial of degree
n
if and only if
\delta^{n}f
is multi-additive, we introduce:

Definition A.3
.

A
polynomial structure of degree
n
on
F
is defined as a structure of a multi-additive functor on
\delta^{n}F
, compatible with its canonical symmetry.

Ducrot introduces in
[
Duc05
, Definition 1.6.1]
the notion of
k
-cube structure
on
F
. By
[
Duc05
, Proposition 1.9]
, an
(n+1)
-cube structure on
F
induces a polynomial structure of degree
n
on
F
(and the converse is probably true as well, by
[
Duc05
, 1.5.1, (d)]
). Ducrot’s terminology comes from the following well-known result.

Example A.4
.

If
L
is a line bundle on an abelian variety
A
, the
theorem of the cube
asserts that for any variety
S
, the functor
F_{L}:A(S)\to\mathcal{P}(S)
defined by
F_{L}(x):=x^{\star}L
admits a
3
-cube structure. It is thus quadratic in our sense,
i.e.
(x,y)\mapsto(x+y)^{\star}L-x^{\star}L-y^{\star}L+0^{\star}L
is biadditive. Further, the whole structure is compatible with base change.

In analogy with Lemma
A.2
, we have:

Lemma A.5
.

Suppose that
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
admits a polynomial structure of degree
n
, and define for
0\leq i\leq n
a functor
F_{n,i}:\mathcal{A}^{i}\to\mathcal{B}
by setting

F_{n,i}(x):=\sum_{k=i}(-1)^{k-i}{k\choose i}(\delta^{k}f)(x^{k}).

For all
x\in\mathcal{A}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we then have canonical functorial isomorphisms

F(mx)\simeq\sum_{i=0}^{n}{m+i\choose i}F_{n,i}(x).

Proof.

Define
g:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{B}
by
g(m):=F(mx)-\sum_{i=0}^{n}{m+i\choose i}F_{n,i}(x)
. Since

\Delta_{m}^{k}F(mx)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-i}{k\choose i}F((m+ix)=(\delta^{k}_{mx}F)(x^{k}),

and
\Delta^{k}_{0}{m+i\choose i}=1
for
k\leq i
and
0
otherwise, we have canonical isomorphisms

(\Delta^{k}g)(0)\simeq 0
for
k=0,\ldots,n
. Further,

(\Delta^{n-1}g)(m+1)-(\Delta^{n-1}g)(m)=(\Delta^{n}g)(m)=(\delta^{n}_{mx}F)(x^{n})-(\delta^{n}F)(x^{n})

\simeq(\delta^{n}F)((m+1)x,x,\ldots,x)-(\delta^{n}F)(mx,x,\ldots,x)-(\delta^{n}F)(x,\ldots,x)\simeq 0,

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, by multiadditivity of
\delta^{n}F
. Summing up these relations, we get

(\Delta^{n-1}g)(m)\simeq(\Delta^{n-1}g)(0)\simeq 0,

and iterating the argument finally yields
g(m)\simeq 0
for all
m
.
∎

A.4.
Coherence of direct images

The following discussion is inspired in part by
[
Ull
]
. Let
X
be a scheme. A
vector bundle
on
X
is a finite, locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. A complex
F^{\bullet}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules is
pseudo-coherent
(resp.
perfect
) if
F^{\bullet}
is locally quasi-isomorphic to a bounded above (resp. bounded) complex of vector bundles. In particular, an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
F
is pseudo-coherent (resp. perfect) if it locally admits a resolution (resp. a finite resolution) by vector bundles.

A morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
is
pseudo-coherent
if
X
is locally realized as a closed subscheme of a smooth
Y
-scheme
Z
such that
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is pseudo-coherent as an
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
-module. In particular,
f
is locally finitely presented; conversely, every flat, locally finitely presented morphism is pseudo-coherent
[
Stacks
, Tag 0695]
.

The following general results hold for arbitrary schemes
X,Y
, and are respectively proved in
[
Kie72
, Theorem 2.9]
,
[
SGA6
, III, Corollaire 2.3]
, and
[
Stacks
, Tag 0B91]
.

Theorem A.6
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper, pseudo-coherent morphism, and let
F
be a pseudo-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Then:

(i)

the derived direct image
Rf_{\star}F
is pseudo-coherent;

(ii)

if
Y
is quasi-compact and
L
is an
f
-ample line bundle on
X
, then
R^{q}f_{\star}F(mL)=0
for all
q\geq 1
and
m\gg 1
.

Theorem A.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be proper, flat, finitely presented (and hence pseudo-coherent) morphism. If
F^{\bullet}
is a perfect complex on
X
, then
Rf_{\star}F^{\bullet}
is perfect on
Y
, and the construction is further compatible with arbitrary base change.

We now discuss the relation between Theorem
A.6
and their better known versions in the Noetherian case (coherence of direct images and Serre vanishing).

Recall that a ring
A
is
coherent
if every finitely generated ideal is finitely presented; the ring
A
is
stably coherent
if every polynomial ring
A[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]
is coherent. We shall say that a scheme
X
is coherent (resp. stably coherent) if it is locally the spectrum of a coherent (resp. stably coherent) ring. A scheme
X
is coherent iff its structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is coherent.

Example A.8
.

Every locally Noetherian scheme is stably coherent.

By
[
SGA6
, I, Corollaire 3.5]
, we have:

Lemma A.9
.

On a coherent scheme
X
, the following conditions for an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
F
are equivalent:

(i)

F
is coherent;

(ii)

F
is locally finitely presented;

(iii)

F
is pseudo-coherent.

More generally, a complex of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
F^{\bullet}
is pseudo-coherent iff its cohomology sheaves
H^{q}(F^{\bullet})
are coherent for all
q
, and zero for
q
sufficiently large, locally uniformly on
X
.

A
Prüfer domain
is an integral domain
A
which satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions:

(i)

every localization of
A
at a prime ideal is a valuation ring;

(ii)

every finitely generated ideal of
A
is invertible;

(iii)

every torsion-free
A
-module is flat.

The main example for us is the valuation ring
K^{\circ}
of a non-Archimedean field
K
.

Example A.10
.

Every Prüfer domain
A
is stably coherent. Indeed, every finitely generated ideal
I
of
A[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]
is torsion free, and hence flat over
A
. By
[
RG71
, Théorème 3.4.6]
,
I
is thus a finitely presented
A[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]
-module.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a locally finitely presented morphism of schemes, and assume that
Y
is stably coherent. Then:

(i)

f
is pseudo-coherent;

(ii)

X
is (stably) coherent;

Proof.

After passing to affine open subschemes, we may assume that
X
and
Y
are spectra of rings
A,B
such that
B=A[t]/I
with
I\subset A[t]=A[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]
a finitely generated ideal. By assumption,
A
is stably coherent, and hence
A[t]
is coherent. By Lemma
A.9
, the finitely presented
A[t]
-module
B
is thus pseudo-coherent, which proves (i).

Let now
J\subset B
be a finitely generated ideal. As modules over
A[t]
,
B
is finitely presented, and
J
is a finitely generated submodule. By coherence of
A[t]
,
J
is a finitely presented as an
A[t]
-module, and hence also as a
B
-module, which proves (ii).
∎

We can now state a version of Theorem
A.6
that recovers in particular the usual statement for locally Noetherian schemes.

Corollary A.12
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper, locally finitely presented morphism of schemes. Assume that
Y
is stably coherent (
e.g.
locally Noetherian, or locally finitely presented over a Prüfer domain), and let
F
be a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Then:

(i)

R^{q}f_{\star}F
is coherent for all
q
;

(ii)

if
Y
is quasi-compact and
L
is an
f
-ample line bundle on
X
, then
R^{q}f_{\star}F(mL)=0
for all
q\geq 1
and
m\gg 1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.11
,
f
is pseudo-coherent, and
X
is coherent. Thus
F
is pseudo-coherent. Theorem
A.6
directly yields (ii), and shows that
Rf_{\star}F
is pseudo-coherent. Since
Y
is coherent, this amounts to (i), by Lemma
A.9
.
∎

A.5.
The determinant of a perfect complex

Let
X
be a scheme. The
determinant
of a vector bundle
E
on
X
is the line bundle
\det E:=\bigwedge^{\rk E}E
. If

(A.4)

0\to E^{\prime}\to E\to E^{\prime\prime}\to 0

is an exact sequence of vector bundles, then there is a canonical isomorphism

(A.5)

\det E\simeq\det E^{\prime}+\det E^{\prime\prime},

where, in additive notation,
+
denotes the tensor product of line bundles, . However, given two vector bundles
E,F
, the isomorphism

\det E+\det F\simeq\det(E\oplus F)\simeq\det(F\oplus E)\simeq\det F+\det E

induced by the canonical isomorphism
E\oplus F\simeq F\oplus E
coincides with the canonical commutativity isomorphism only up to a factor
(-1)^{(\rk E)(\rk F)}
.

To deal with this sign issue, one introduces the graded determinant functor
E\mapsto(\det E,\rk E)
with values in the (non-strictly) commutative Picard category
\mathcal{P}(X)\times\mathbb{Z}
of graded line bundles, in which the commutativity isomorphism is modified according to the Koszul rule of signs as above. For the purpose of the present paper, it will however be enough to view
\det E
as an object in the strictly commutative Picard category
\mathcal{P}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
of
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundles on
X
, and we can thus ignore the previous sign issue.

In
[
KM76
, Theorem 2]
, Knudsen and Mumford showed that setting

\det E^{\bullet}:=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\det E^{i}

for each bounded complex of vector bundles
E^{\bullet}
gives rise to a functor
F^{\bullet}\mapsto\det F^{\bullet}
from the category of perfect complexes on
X
and quasi-isomorphims between them to
\mathcal{P}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. This functor commutes with base change, it is additive with respect to short exact sequences of complexes in the sense of (
A.5
). It can be uniquely characterized up to unique isomorphism by imposing further properties. By
[
KM76
, p.43, (b)]
, we have:

Lemma A.13
.

If the cohomology sheaves
H^{q}(F^{\bullet})
of a perfect complex
F^{\bullet}
are perfect (
e.g.
locally free), then

\det F^{\bullet}=\sum_{q}(-1)^{q}\det H^{q}(F^{\bullet}).

A.6.
Regular sections

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat, locally finitely presented morphism of schemes, and let
s
be a global section of a line bundle
L
on
X
, defining a closed subscheme
Z\subset X
. Recall that
s
is
f
-regular at
x\in X
if
Z
is a relative Cartier divisor at
x
, i.e.
s
is a nonzerodivisor in
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
, and
Z
is
f
-flat at
x
. If this holds for all
x\in X
, then
s
is simply called
f
-regular. By
[
EGA
, IV.11.3.7]
,
s
is
f
-regular at
x
if and only the restriction of
s
to the fiber through
x
is not a zerodivisor at
x
, and the set of
x\in X
at which this holds is open.

Lemma A.14
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be flat, proper, finitely presented morphism of schemes, and
s
be a global section of a line bundle
L
on
X
. Pick
y\in Y
, and assume that
s
is nonzero at each associated point of
X_{y}
. Then
s
is relatively regular over an open neighborhood of
y
.

Proof.

Denote by
U\subset X
the open set of points at which
s
is relatively regular. Since
X_{y}
is Noetherian (being of finite type over a field), the assumption implies that
s|_{X_{y}}
is a nonzerodivisor at each
x\in X_{y}
, and hence that
X_{y}\subset U
by the above results. As
f
is closed, it follows that
f^{-1}(V)\subset U
for some open neighborhood
V
of
y
.
∎

As a consequence, we then have the following useful existence result for relatively regular sections.

Proposition A.15
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat, projective, finitely presented morphism of schemes, and let
L
be a
f
-ample line bundle on
X
. Then
mL
admits a relatively regular section locally over
Y
for
m\gg 1
.

Proof.

Pick
y\in Y
. After replacing
Y
with an affine neighborhood of
y
and
L
with a large enough multiple, we may assume that there exists a closed embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}_{Y}
over
Y
with
L=\mathcal{O}(1)|_{X}
. The set of associated points
S
of
X_{y}
being finite, prime avoidance yields for
m\gg 1
a section
s\in\Hnot(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{N},\mathcal{O}(m))
that does not vanish at any point of
S
, and the restriction of
s
to
X
is thus relatively regular over a neighborhood of
y
, by Lemma
A.14
.
∎

A.7.
The determinant of cohomology

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat, proper, finitely presented morphism between arbitrary schemes
X,Y
, with Picard categories
\mathcal{P}(X)
,
\mathcal{P}(Y)
. By Theorem
A.7
,
Rf_{\star}
takes a perfect complex to a perfect complex, and we can thus consider its determinant. We will only be concerned with the case when the source perfect complex is a line bundle, so we wil be content with the following definition, which could be stated more generally:

Definition A.16
.

The
determinant of cohomology
is the functor
\lambda_{X/Y}:\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}
that takes a line bundle
L
on
X
to the
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle

\lambda_{X/Y}(L):=\det Rf_{\star}L.

By Lemma
A.13
, we have:

Lemma A.17
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
is such that
R^{q}f_{\star}L
is a vector bundle, for all
q
. Then

\lambda_{X/Y}(L)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{N}}(-1)^{q}\det R^{q}f_{\star}L.

This holds in particular when
f
has relative dimension
0
, i.e. a finite flat morphism. Indeed, any finite morphism
f:X\to Y
satisfies
R^{q}f_{\star}F=0
for any
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
F
and
q>0

[
Stacks
, Tag 03QP]
. By
[
Stacks
, Tag 02KB]
,
f
is flat iff
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a vector bundle, and we then have
\lambda_{X/Y}(L)=\det f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Proposition A.18
.

The determinant of cohomology satisfies the following two compatibility properties.

(i)

It commutes with arbitrary base change: for any morphism
g:Y^{\prime}\to Y
, let

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

be the corresponding Cartesian square. Then we have a canonical functorial isomorphism

\lambda_{X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}(h^{\star}L)\simeq g^{\star}\lambda_{X/Y}(L).

(ii)

If
Z
is an effective relative Cartier on
X
, then we have a canonical functorial isomorphism

(A.6)

\lambda_{X/Y}(L)-\lambda_{X/Y}(L-Z)\simeq\lambda_{Z/Y}(L|_{Z}).

Proof.

Since
L
is flat over
Y
, we have
Rf^{\prime}_{\star}h^{\star}L=Lg^{\star}Rf_{\star}L
, cf.
[
Stacks
, Tag 0A1D]
. On the other hand, the determinant functor satisfies
\det Lg^{\star}=g^{\star}\det
, hence (i). If
Z
is an effective relative Cartier divisor on
X
, then the natural exact sequence
0\to L(-Z)\to L\to L|_{Z}\to 0
induces an exact sequence of perfect complexes
0\to Rf_{\star}L(-Z)\to Rf_{\star}L\to R(f|_{Z})_{\star}L|_{Z}\to 0
, and (ii) follows by additivity of
\det
in exact sequences.
∎

A.8.
Deligne pairings and Knudsen–Mumford expansion

In this section, we fix a flat, projective, finitely presented morphism
f:X\to Y
of constant relative dimension
n
.

When
n=0
,
f:X\to Y
is a finite flat morphism, and the determinant of cohomology
\lambda_{X/Y}
provides a canonical and functorial construction of the norm of a line bundle
[
EGA
, II.6.5]
(compare for instance
[
Fer98
, Proposition 3.3]
). To see this, recall first that the norm
N_{X/Y}(h)\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}
of
h\in f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is defined as the determinant of the endomorphism of the vector bundle
f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}
defined by multiplication by
h
, yielding a multiplicative map

(A.7)

N_{X/Y}:f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}.

Now define a functor
N_{X/Y}:\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
by setting

N_{X/Y}(L)=(\det f_{\star}L)-(\det f_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X})

=\lambda_{X/Y}(L)-\lambda_{X/Y}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=(\delta\lambda_{X/Y})(L),

Lemma A.19
.

For each line bundle
L
on
X
,
N_{X/Y}(L)
coincides with the norm of
L
as defined in
[
EGA
, II.6.5]
.

Proof.

Observe that if
u\in\Hnot(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\star})
is a unit and
L
is a line bundle on
X
, multiplication by
u
defines an isomorphism
L\simeq L
, whose induced isomorphism
\det f_{\star}L\simeq\det f_{\star}L
, and hence also
N_{X/Y}(L)\simeq N_{X/Y}(L)
, are both given by multiplication by
N_{X/Y}(u)
.

By
[
Stacks
, Tag 0BUT]
,
L
is trivial in a neighborhood of each fiber of
f
, and
Y
therefore admits an open cover
(Y_{i})
with
L|_{X_{i}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
on
X_{i}:=f^{-1}(Y_{i})
. Set
Y_{ij}=Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
,
X_{ij}=X_{i}\cap X_{j}=f^{-1}(Y_{ij})
, and denote by
u_{ij}\in\Hnot(X_{ij},\mathcal{O}_{X_{ij}}^{\star})
the corresponding cocycle. The transition isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{X_{ij}}\simeq L|_{X_{ij}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X_{ij}}
is given by multiplication by
u_{ij}
. By the above observation, applying the functor
N_{X/Y}
yields an isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{ij}}\simeq N_{X/Y}(L)|_{Y_{ij}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Y_{ij}}
given by multiplication by
N_{X_{ij}/Y_{ij}}(u_{ij})
, which precisely means that
N_{X/Y}(L)
coincides with the norm of
L
as defined in
[
EGA
, II.6.5]
.
∎

When
Y
is the spectrum of a field
K
and
X
is the spectrum of a finite flat
K
-algebra
A
, the norm functor admits the following concrete description (see e.g.
[
Mor14
, Lemma 1.13]
). For
a\in A
, we have

(A.8)

N_{A/K}(a)=\prod_{n_{i}\in\Spec(A)}N_{(A/n_{i})/K}(a_{i})^{m_{i}}.

Here
a_{i}
is the image of
a
in
A/n_{i}
,
m_{i}=\operatorname{length}_{B_{n_{i}}}A_{n_{i}}
so that
\sum m_{i}[a_{i}]
is the fundamental cycle of
[A].

Arguing as in
[
Duc05
, 4.1.1]
, we next prove:

Proposition A.20
.

There is a unique way to assign to each finite flat morphism
f:X\to Y
an additivity structure on the norm functor
N_{X/Y}:\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
that is compatible with base change and such that the following diagram commutes

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 36.44206pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.75212pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N_{X/Y}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.59244pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 91.59244pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N_{X/Y}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 115.34456pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-36.44206pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N_{X/Y}(L+\mathcal{O}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.44206pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 60.44206pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N_{X/Y}(L)+N_{X/Y}(\mathcal{O}_{X})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Here the upper row is the identity, the lower row is the additivity isomorphism, and the vertical rows are deduced from the natural isomorphism of the type
L\simeq L+\mathcal{O}_{X}
and the identification
N_{X/Y}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
.

Proof.

Pick two line bundles
L,L^{\prime}
on
X
, and choose an open cover
(Y_{i})
of
Y
with trivializations
L|_{X_{i}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
,
L^{\prime}|_{X_{i}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
on
X_{i}:=f^{-1}(Y_{i})
. Given an additivity isomorphism

N_{X/Y}(L+L^{\prime})\simeq N_{X/Y}(L)+N_{X/Y}(L^{\prime})

with the desired properties, the induced isomorphisms

N_{X_{i}/Y_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}+\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}})\simeq N_{X_{i}/Y_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}})+N_{X_{i}/Y_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}})

are necessarily equal to the canonical ones obtained by multiplicativity of (
A.7
), which proves uniqueness. To establish existence, it is then enough to argue locally on
Y
, since compatibility on overlaps will follow from uniqueness, and the result is then straightforward, using again that any line bundle on
X
is trivial locally over
Y
.
∎

In the terminology of §
A.3
, Proposition
A.20
says that the functor
\lambda_{X/Y}
is polynomial of degree
1
when
n=0
. This is generalized by the next result, due to F. Ducrot.

Theorem A.21
.

[
Duc05
, Theorem 4.2]
There exists a unique way to assign to each flat, projective, finitely presented morphism
f:X\to Y
of relative dimension
n
a polynomial structure of degree
n+1
on
\lambda_{X/Y}:\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}
with the following properties:

(i)

it commutes with base change;

(ii)

it coincides with the above one when
n=0
;

(iii)

for any relative effective divisor
Z
on
X
, the polynomial structures on
\lambda_{X/Y}
and
\lambda_{Z/Y}
are compatible with the canonical restriction isomorphism

\lambda_{X/Y}(L)-\lambda_{X/Y}(L-Z)\simeq\lambda_{Z/Y}(L|_{Z}).

More precisely,
[
Duc05
, Theorem 4.2]
proves the existence of a canonical
(n+2)
-cube structure on
\lambda_{X/Y}
, which yields (and is probably equivalent to) a polynomial structure of degree
n+1
on
\lambda_{X/Y}
, as discussed in §
A.3
. Stricty speaking, the proof of Theorem
A.21
assumes
X
and
Y
to be locally Noetherian, but all the arguments apply in the general case, once Theorem
A.7
and Proposition
A.15
are available. Since a polynomial structure of degree
n+1
on
\lambda_{X/Y}
is by definition a multi-additive structure on the
(n+1)
-st difference

(\delta^{n+1}\lambda_{X/Y})(L_{0},\ldots,L_{n})=\sum_{I\subset\{0,\ldots,n\}}(-1)^{n+1-|I|}\lambda_{X/Y}\left(\sum_{i\in I}L_{i}\right),

and we can thus define the
Deligne pairing
as the functor
\mathcal{P}(X)^{n+1}\to\mathcal{P}(Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}
that takes line bundles
L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}
on
X
to the
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle

(A.9)

\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle_{X/Y}:=(\delta^{n+1}\lambda_{X/Y})(L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}).

Theorem A.22
.

The Deligne pairing satisfies the following properties.

(i)

it is multi-additive, symmetric, and commutes with base change;

(ii)

for each relative effective Cartier divisor
Z
on
X
, we have canonical multi-additive functorial isomorphisms

\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Z),L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle_{X/Y}\simeq\langle L_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z}\rangle_{Z/Y};

(iii)

if
g:X^{\prime}\to X
is a finite flat of degree
e
, then we have canonical functorial isomorphisms

\langle g^{\star}L_{0},\ldots,g^{\star}L_{n}\rangle_{X^{\prime}/Y}\simeq e\langle L_{0},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle_{X/Y}.

Proof.

(i) follows directly from Theorem
A.21
. Given
Z
as in (ii), taking the
n
-th iterated difference of the restriction isomorphism

\lambda_{Z/Y}(L|_{Z})\simeq\lambda_{X/Y}(L)-\lambda_{X/Y}(L-Z)

yields

\langle L_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z}\rangle_{Z/Y}=(\delta^{n}\lambda_{Z/Y})(L_{1}|_{Z},\ldots,L_{n}|_{Z})

\simeq(\delta^{n}\lambda_{X/Y})(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})-(\delta^{n}_{-Z}\lambda_{X/Y})(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})\simeq-(\delta^{n+1}\lambda_{X/Y})(-Z,L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}),

which is isomorphic to

(\delta^{n+1}\lambda_{X/Y})(Z,L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})=\langle Z,L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}\rangle_{X/Y},

by multiadditivity of
\delta^{n+1}\lambda_{X/Y}
. Finally, let
g:X^{\prime}\to X
be finite and flat of degree
e
, so that
E:=g_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
is a rank
e
vector bundle. By the projection formula we have

R(f\circ g)_{\star}(g^{\star}L)=Rf_{\star}Rg_{\star}(g^{\star}L)=Rf_{\star}(L\otimes E).

Thus (iii) follows from
[
Duc05
, Proposition 4.7.1]
, as the latter yields a canonical isomorphism between the
(n+1)
-st difference of
L\mapsto\det Rf_{\star}(L\otimes E)
and
e\,\delta^{n+1}\lambda_{X/Y}
.
∎

By Lemma
A.5
, we finally get the following generalization of
[
KM76
, Theorem 4]
.

Corollary A.23
.

For each
0\leq i\leq n+1
, define a functor
F_{n+1,i}:\mathcal{P}(X)^{i}\to\mathcal{P}(Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}
by

F_{n+1,i}(L):=\sum_{k=i}^{n+1}(-1)^{k-i}{k\choose i}\left(\delta^{k}\lambda_{X/Y}\right)(L^{k})

For each line bundle
L
on
X
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we then have functorial isomorphisms

\lambda_{X/Y}(mL)\simeq\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}{m+i\choose i}F_{n+1,i}(L)=\frac{m^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\langle L^{n+1}\rangle_{X/Y}+O(m^{n}),

compatible with base change.
